5.3 Plurisubharmonic functions and singularities

1 Plurisubharmonic functions and singularities

A function whose complex Hessian is bounded below by a smooth form becomes plurisubharmonic after a local smooth correction. This brings the local theory of plurisubharmonic functions to a compact complex manifold, provided the smooth remainder left by the correction is kept when potentials are glued, when their Hessians are multiplied and when one integrates by parts. Normalization by the supremum then controls the resulting families globally, giving compactness, uniform exponential integrability and capacity estimates on the whole manifold. Products of Hessians require more care than the potentials themselves: without a common bound, convergence of potentials in $L^1$, or even in capacity, does not prevent their Monge–Ampère measures from concentrating.

To keep track of singularities under these operations, we compare potentials in two ways: by domination up to an additive constant, which leads to ordinary singularity types and their envelopes, and by the local integrability of holomorphic functions against exponential weights, which leads to multiplier ideals. Lelong numbers, analytic approximation and modifications are the main tools for computing the latter.

1.1 Plurisubharmonic potentials

Throughout the chapter, $X$ is a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$, $\theta $ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form and $\omega $ is a Hermitian form on $X$, unless a statement specifies another setting. There are two distinct extensions of the Kähler setting: one can study a closed background on a non-Kähler manifold, or allow the background itself to be nonclosed. In both cases positivity is expressed through quasi-plurisubharmonic functions.

On an open set $U$ in a complex manifold, a plurisubharmonic function is a locally integrable upper semicontinuous function satisfying the submean inequality on every complex line in local coordinates. We write $\mathrm{PSH}(U)$ for the set of these functions. A function is quasi-plurisubharmonic if it is locally the sum of a plurisubharmonic function and a smooth real function; the set of such functions is denoted by $\mathrm{QPSH}(U)$. The following relative positivity condition makes sense on any such open set as well.

Definition 1.1.1. A quasi-plurisubharmonic function $u$ on $X$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic if

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0 \]

as a current. We denote the set of these functions by $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Since $\omega >0$, every constant function belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$.

Proposition 1.1.2. Every real distribution $u$ on $X$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ has a unique $\theta $-plurisubharmonic representative. On each relatively compact coordinate ball with coordinates $z=(z_1,\ldots ,z_ n)$, there is a constant $A>0$ such that $u+A|z|^2$ is plurisubharmonic. Conversely, every quasi-plurisubharmonic function $u$ on $X$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,C\omega )$ for some constant $C>0$.

Proof. On a coordinate ball with compact closure in a larger chart, smoothness of $\theta $ gives

\[ \theta \leq A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2 \]

for a sufficiently large $A$. Consequently,

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+A|z|^2)\geq A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2-\theta \geq 0. \]

The local distributional characterization of plurisubharmonic functions, [Chapter I, Theorem 5.8, DemCADG], gives a unique plurisubharmonic representative of $u+A|z|^2$. Subtracting the smooth correction gives a quasi-plurisubharmonic representative of $u$ on the ball. These representatives agree on overlaps: after a common local smooth correction, two of them become plurisubharmonic functions that are equal almost everywhere, hence everywhere. They therefore glue to a unique $\theta $-plurisubharmonic representative.

For the converse, cover $X$ by finitely many open sets $V_ j\Subset U_ j$, where each $U_ j$ is a chart on which $u=p_ j+f_ j$, with $p_ j$ plurisubharmonic and $f_ j$ smooth. On the compact set $\overline V_ j$, the smooth form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j$ is bounded below by $-C_ j\omega $ for some constant $C_ j$, so $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j\geq -C_ j\omega $ on $V_ j$. Any constant $C>0$ larger than all the $C_ j$ gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq -C\omega $ on $X$. $\square$

For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, choose coordinates $z$ and a constant $A>0$ as in proposition 1.1.2, and put $p=u+A|z|^2$. Then

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p+(\theta -A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2). \]

Thus local plurisubharmonic theory applies to $p$, while the smooth remainder $\theta -A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ records the original background; this remainder must be kept when forming products or integrating by parts.

Proposition 1.1.3. If $\theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho $ on an open set for a smooth function $\rho $, then $\mathrm{d}\theta =0$ on that set. In particular, at a point where $\mathrm{d}\theta \neq 0$, no neighborhood admits such a representation.

Proof. Apply $\mathrm{d}$ and use $\mathrm{d}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}=0$. $\square$

Conversely, a closed real $(1,1)$-form has smooth local $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-potentials on sufficiently small coordinate balls, by the local $\partial \bar\partial $-lemma; this local fact does not require $X$ to admit a Kähler metric.

The exterior derivative also constrains the regularity of a potential whose current is pointwise proportional to a Hermitian form. In dimension at least two, differentiating such an identity expresses the derivative of the proportionality factor through smooth coefficients.

Lemma 1.1.4. Let $X$ be a complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 2$, let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form and let $\gamma $ be a Hermitian form on $X$. Suppose that real-valued $u,a\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)$ satisfy

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=a\gamma \]

as currents. Then $a$ and $u$ are smooth.

Proof. Taking the exterior derivative and multiplying by $\gamma ^{n-2}$ gives

\[ \mathrm{d}a\wedge \gamma ^{n-1} =\mathrm{d}\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-2} -a\, \mathrm{d}\gamma \wedge \gamma ^{n-2}. \]

At each point, $\xi \mapsto \xi \wedge \gamma ^{n-1}$ is an isomorphism from real one-forms to real $(2n-1)$-forms, as one sees in a unitary frame. Its inverse depends smoothly on the point. Let $B$ and $C$ be the images of $\mathrm{d}\theta \wedge \gamma ^{n-2}$ and $\mathrm{d}\gamma \wedge \gamma ^{n-2}$ under this inverse. They are smooth real one-forms, and

\[ \mathrm{d}a=B-aC \]

in the sense of distributions. Since $a\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$, the right-hand side is locally integrable, so $a\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Differentiating the equation and using the smoothness of its coefficients, we obtain inductively $a\in W^{k,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every integer $k$, and the local Sobolev embedding shows that $a$ is smooth. Consequently $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=a\gamma -\theta $ is smooth; taking its trace in local coordinates and applying elliptic regularity shows that $u$ is smooth as well. $\square$

1.2 Changes of background

A positive current can be written as a smooth form plus a complex Hessian in more than one way. Changing the decomposition by a smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact form changes the potential by a bounded function, and positive linear combinations allow us to compare potentials belonging to different backgrounds. In this section, $\eta $ denotes a second smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$.

Proposition 1.2.1. Let $f$ be a smooth real function on $X$, and set $\theta '=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$. Then

\[ \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\longrightarrow \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta '), \qquad u\longmapsto u-f \]

is a bijection. It preserves the positive current and changes each potential by a bounded function. Moreover, $\mathrm{d}\theta '=\mathrm{d}\theta $.

Proof. For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$,

\[ \theta '+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u-f)=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u. \]

The inverse map is $v\mapsto v+f$, and $f$ is bounded because $X$ is compact. The last assertion follows from $\mathrm{d}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=0$. $\square$

Thus the natural object is the class of $\theta $ modulo smooth $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-exact forms. This quotient of all smooth real $(1,1)$-forms contains nonclosed classes; the usual Bott–Chern cohomology group is the corresponding quotient of closed forms.

Proposition 1.2.2. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\eta )$, then, for $a,b\geq 0$ with $a+b>0$,

\[ au+bv\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,a\theta +b\eta ). \]

In particular, $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is convex. If $\eta \geq \theta $, then $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\subseteq \mathrm{PSH}(X,\eta )$.

Proof. Omitting a term whose coefficient is zero, we see that $au+bv$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic. Moreover,

\[ a\theta +b\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(au+bv) =a(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)+b(\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)\geq 0. \]

For the inclusion, add the semipositive smooth form $\eta -\theta $ to $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. $\square$

By proposition 1.1.2, any two quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on $X$ belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,C\omega )$ for a common sufficiently large constant $C$. This allows us to compare their singularities within one positive background.

1.3 Maxima and the maximum principle

Maxima allow us to truncate potentials and to modify them on an open set without losing the lower curvature bound. For a smooth background, the local maximum and gluing principles for plurisubharmonic functions keep this bound: one freezes the coefficients of the background on small balls and then lets the resulting curvature error tend to zero.

Lemma 1.3.1. If $u,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, then $\max (u,v)\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Consequently, if $\theta \geq 0$ and $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, then $\max (u,-t)$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic for every real $t$.

Proof. Adding a common local smooth correction to $u$ and $v$ shows that $\max (u,v)$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic. To obtain the sharp lower bound for its Hessian, fix a positive constant $(1,1)$-form $\kappa $ on a coordinate ball. For each $\varepsilon >0$, every point of the ball has a neighborhood on which $\theta $ is close to a constant form $\theta '$, in the sense that $-\varepsilon \kappa \leq \theta -\theta '\leq \varepsilon \kappa $. Write $\theta '+\varepsilon \kappa =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r$ with a quadratic polynomial $r$. Then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+r)\geq \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$, so $u+r$ and, likewise, $v+r$ are plurisubharmonic; hence so is their maximum. It follows that

\[ \theta +2\varepsilon \kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max (u,v)\geq 0 \]

on each such neighborhood, and letting $\varepsilon \to 0$ gives $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\max (u,v)\geq 0$. The last assertion follows because constants belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ when $\theta \geq 0$. $\square$

Lemma 1.3.2. Let $Y$ be a complex manifold, let $\beta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $Y$, and let $D\subset Y$ be open. Suppose that $u$ is $\beta $-plurisubharmonic on $Y$, that $v$ is $\beta $-plurisubharmonic on $D$, and that

\[ \limsup _{D\ni y\to x}v(y)\leq u(x)\qquad (x\in \partial D). \]

Then the function equal to $\max (u,v)$ on $D$ and to $u$ on $Y\setminus D$ is $\beta $-plurisubharmonic.

Proof. Denote the glued function by $w$; it is upper semicontinuous by the boundary inequality. Freeze $\beta $ on a small coordinate ball as in the proof of lemma 1.3.1, and choose a quadratic correction $r$ such that $u+r$ and $v+r$ are plurisubharmonic on their respective domains. Off $\partial D$, the function $w+r$ is plurisubharmonic, being locally either $u+r$ or the maximum of two plurisubharmonic functions. At a point of $\partial D$, it equals $u+r$ and dominates $u+r$ on the ball, so it satisfies the submean inequality on every complex line through that point. Thus $w+r$ is plurisubharmonic on the whole ball. Letting the error of the frozen coefficients tend to zero gives $\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\geq 0$. $\square$

For quasi-plurisubharmonic functions, an inequality that holds almost everywhere holds everywhere. This allows comparisons proved for Sobolev or Lebesgue classes to be applied to the potentials themselves.

Lemma 1.3.3. Let $a,b$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on a complex manifold. If $a\leq b$ almost everywhere, then $a\leq b$ everywhere. In particular, equality almost everywhere implies equality everywhere.

Proof. On a coordinate ball, add the same smooth function $q$ to both functions so that $a+q$ and $b+q$ are plurisubharmonic. By the submean inequality and upper semicontinuity, a plurisubharmonic function is, at each point, the limit of its averages over balls centered there as the radius tends to zero. The almost-everywhere inequality orders these averages, so passing to the limit gives $a+q\leq b+q$. Subtracting $q$ proves the claim. $\square$

Lemma 1.3.4. Let $a,b$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on a complex manifold. If $a(x)<b(x)$ at a point $x$ where both values are finite, then $\{ a<b\} $ has positive volume with respect to any smooth positive volume form.

Proof. Choose a number $c$ with $a(x)<c<b(x)$. By upper semicontinuity, $a<c$ on a neighborhood $V$ of $x$. If $\{ b>c\} \cap V$ had zero volume, then $b\leq c$ almost everywhere on $V$, and lemma 1.3.3 would give $b(x)\leq c$, a contradiction. Thus $\{ b>c\} \cap V$, which is contained in $\{ a<b\} $, has positive volume. $\square$

Lemma 1.3.5. A plurisubharmonic function on $\mathbb C^ m$, $m\geq 1$, that is bounded above is constant.

Proof. First let $f$ be a smooth subharmonic function on $\mathbb C$ with $f\leq M$. Write $\sigma =(\Delta f)\, \mathrm{d}A$ for its nonnegative Laplacian measure, where $\Delta $ and $\mathrm{d}A$ are Euclidean. Green's formula for circular means gives, for $R>0$,

\[ \frac1{2\pi }\int _0^{2\pi }f(R\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}t})\, \mathrm{d}t-f(0) =\frac1{2\pi }\int _0^ R\frac{\sigma (B(0,r))}{r}\, \mathrm{d}r. \]

For fixed $r_0>0$ and $R>r_0$, the right-hand side is at least $\sigma (B(0,r_0))\log (R/r_0)/(2\pi )$, while the left-hand side is at most $M-f(0)$. Letting $R\to \infty $ gives $\sigma (B(0,r_0))=0$. Thus $f$ is harmonic, and the Liouville theorem for the positive harmonic function $M+1-f$ shows that $f$ is constant.

Now let $p$ be plurisubharmonic on $\mathbb C^ m$ and bounded above. Its convolutions with a smooth nonnegative radial probability kernel are smooth plurisubharmonic functions with the same upper bound. By the planar case, their restrictions to every affine complex line are constant, so each convolution is constant on $\mathbb C^ m$. Since the convolutions converge to $p$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$, the function $p$ is almost everywhere equal to a constant, and hence constant by lemma 1.3.3. $\square$

For a function $f$, we write $f^*(x)=\limsup _{y\to x}f(y)$ for its upper semicontinuous regularization. A subset of a complex manifold is pluripolar if each of its points has a neighborhood $U$ such that the part of the subset in $U$ is contained in $\{ p=-\infty \} $ for some $p\in \mathrm{PSH}(U)$. The local upper-envelope theorem recalled in [SGPT] states that if a nonempty family of plurisubharmonic functions is locally uniformly bounded above, then the regularization of its supremum is plurisubharmonic and differs from the supremum only on a pluripolar set.

Periodicity also forces rigidity when an entire subharmonic function has only logarithmic upper growth.

Lemma 1.3.6. Let $h$ be subharmonic on $\mathbb C$, with $h(\zeta +1)=h(\zeta )$. If there are real constants $A\geq 0$ and $B$ such that

\[ h(\zeta )\leq A\log (1+|\zeta |)+B\qquad (\zeta \in \mathbb C), \]

then $h$ is constant.

Proof. By the growth bound, the real translates $h(\cdot +s)$, $s\in [0,1]$, form a family of subharmonic functions that is locally uniformly bounded above. The local upper-envelope theorem therefore shows that

\[ H(\zeta )=\bigl (\sup _{s\in [0,1]}h(\zeta +s)\bigr )^* \]

is subharmonic. By periodicity, $H$ is invariant under every real translation, so it depends only on $\operatorname {Im}\zeta $, and its subharmonicity means that it is a convex function of $\operatorname {Im}\zeta $. On the imaginary axis it satisfies $H(\mathrm i y)\leq A\log (2+|y|)+B$. Since a nonconstant convex function on $\mathbb R$ grows at least linearly at one end, this bound shows that $H$ is constant. Thus $h\leq H$ is bounded above, and lemma 1.3.5 shows that $h$ is constant. $\square$

Lemma 1.3.7. Let $U$ be a complex manifold, let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $U$, and let $Z\subset U$ be closed and locally pluripolar. Let $u$ be $\theta $-plurisubharmonic on $U\setminus Z$ and locally bounded above near $Z$. Then its upper-limit extension, defined at $x\in Z$ by $u(x)=\limsup _{U\setminus Z\ni y\to x}u(y)$, is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic on $U$. In particular, this applies when $Z$ is a proper analytic subset of $U$.

Proof. First suppose that $u$ is plurisubharmonic. Near a point of $Z$, choose a plurisubharmonic function $\psi \leq 0$ with $\psi =-\infty $ on $Z$. For $t>0$, extend $u+t\psi $ by $-\infty $ on $Z$. The local upper bound for $u$ makes this extension upper semicontinuous. It is plurisubharmonic off $Z$, and its submean inequality on complex lines is automatic at points of $Z$, where its value is $-\infty $. It is therefore plurisubharmonic on the whole neighborhood. As $t\downarrow 0$, these functions increase, with a common local upper bound. By the local upper-envelope theorem, the regularization of their limit is a plurisubharmonic function that agrees with $u$ almost everywhere off $Z$, hence everywhere off $Z$ by lemma 1.3.3. By upper semicontinuity and the submean inequality, its value at a point of $Z$ is the upper limit of $u$ off $Z$, so it is the upper-limit extension of $u$.

For a smooth background, fix a positive constant form $\kappa $ on a coordinate chart and let $\epsilon >0$. On a sufficiently small ball, freeze $\theta $ to a constant form $\theta '$ with $-\epsilon \kappa \leq \theta -\theta '\leq \epsilon \kappa $, and choose a quadratic potential $q$ for $\theta '+\epsilon \kappa $. Applying the preceding argument to the plurisubharmonic function $u+q$ shows that the upper-limit extension satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq -2\epsilon \kappa $ on the ball. Since the extension is defined by upper limits, it does not depend on the ball, and letting $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ gives $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$. Finally, analytic subsets are locally pluripolar: locally, a proper analytic subset is contained in the $-\infty $ locus of the logarithm of the sum of the squared moduli of local defining functions. $\square$

Lemma 1.3.8. Let $X$ be a complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 2$, let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, and let $p\in X$. Every $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function on $X\setminus \{ p\} $ is locally bounded above near $p$ and extends uniquely to a member of $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. If $X$ is compact, it is bounded above on $X\setminus \{ p\} $.

Proof. Choose coordinates centered at $p$ and write $\Delta _ R=\{ z\in \mathbb C:|z|<R\} $. On the coordinate polydisk $\Delta _ R^ n$, add a smooth quadratic function to the given function to obtain a plurisubharmonic function $h$ on $\Delta _ R^ n\setminus \{ 0\} $. Write the coordinates as $(z_1,z_2,z')$, with $z'$ absent when $n=2$, and choose $0<r<R$. The compact set

\[ K=\{ |z_1|\leq r,\ |z_2|=r,\ |z'_ j|\leq r\} \]

avoids the puncture, so $h\leq M$ on $K$ for some finite $M$. Consider a point of the punctured closed polydisk of radius $r$. If $z_1\ne 0$, the disk in the $z_2$-direction through the point avoids the puncture and has its boundary in $K$, so the maximum principle gives $h\leq M$ at the point. If $z_1=0$ and $(z_2,z')\ne 0$, the disk in the $z_1$-direction through the point avoids the puncture, and its boundary consists of points with $z_1\neq 0$, where the bound has already been proved; the maximum principle again gives $h\leq M$. Subtracting the smooth correction proves the local upper bound.

By lemma 1.3.7, applied with $Z=\{ p\} $, the upper-limit extension belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. It is unique by lemma 1.3.3. If $X$ is compact, the extension is an upper semicontinuous function on a compact space and is therefore bounded above. $\square$

Lemma 1.3.9. Let $u$ be $\theta $-plurisubharmonic near a point $x$. If a $C^2$ function $a$ touches $u$ from above at $x$, that is, $a\geq u$ near $x$ and $a(x)=u(x)$, then $\theta (x)+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a(x)\geq 0$.

Proof. In coordinates centered at $x$, let $q$ be a quadratic polynomial with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q=\theta (x)$, and let $\varepsilon >0$. On a sufficiently small ball about $x$, we have $\theta \leq \theta (x)+\varepsilon \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$, so $u+q+\varepsilon |z|^2$ is plurisubharmonic, and $a+q+\varepsilon |z|^2$ touches it from above at $x$. By the submean inequality on complex lines through $x$, a $C^2$ function touching a plurisubharmonic function from above at $x$ has nonnegative complex Hessian at $x$. Hence $\theta (x)+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a(x)+\varepsilon \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2\geq 0$, and letting $\varepsilon \to 0$ proves the lemma. $\square$

1.4 Normalized compactness

Fix a smooth positive probability volume form $\mathrm{d}V$ on $X$, used for $L^1$ norms. Consider a sequence of plurisubharmonic functions on a connected domain that is locally uniformly bounded above. By the local compactness theorem recalled in [SGPT], it has a subsequence that either converges in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ or converges locally uniformly to $-\infty $. For normalized $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions on $X$, a point at which the maximum is attained excludes the second alternative, and connectedness of $X$ propagates the first one across $X$.

The submean inequality also controls upper limits at moving points.

Lemma 1.4.1. Let $u_ j,u$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on an open set $U$ in a complex manifold. Suppose that $u_ j\to u$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$ and that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\geq -\beta $ for a fixed smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $ on $U$. If $x_ j\to x$ in $U$, then

\[ \limsup _{j\to \infty }u_ j(x_ j)\leq u(x). \]

Moreover, the tail envelopes $h_ j=(\sup _{k\geq j}u_ k)^*$ decrease to $u$.

Proof. On a coordinate ball about $x$, add a common smooth correction $q$ so that $u_ j+q$ and $u+q$ are plurisubharmonic. For a fixed small radius, the submean inequality bounds $(u_ j+q)(x_ j)$ by the average of $u_ j+q$ over the ball of that radius centered at $x_ j$. As $j\to \infty $, these averages converge to the average of $u+q$ over the ball of the same radius centered at $x$: the contribution of $u_ j-u$ tends to zero in $L^1$, and the integral of the fixed locally integrable function $u+q$ over a ball depends continuously on its center. Letting the radius decrease to zero proves the inequality.

The same estimate gives local uniform upper bounds for the $u_ j$ with $j$ large. By coefficient freezing and the local upper-envelope theorem, the tail envelopes are therefore quasi-plurisubharmonic, with the same lower Hessian bound. They decrease with $j$. If their limit exceeded $u(x)$ at some point $x$, the definition of the regularization would give indices $k_ j\geq j$ and points $x_ j\to x$ contradicting the first assertion for the subsequence $(u_{k_ j})$. On the other hand, an almost everywhere convergent subsequence of $(u_ j)$ shows that $h_ j\geq u$ almost everywhere, hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3. Thus $h_ j\downarrow u$. $\square$

Theorem 1.4.2. For every smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ on $X$, the family

\[ \mathcal K_\theta =\{ u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):\sup _ Xu=0\} \]

is compact in $L^1(X,\mathrm{d}V)$. In particular, there is a constant $C_\theta $ such that

\[ \int _ X|u|\, \mathrm{d}V\leq C_\theta \quad (u\in \mathcal K_\theta ). \]

Proof. Let $(u_ j)$ be a sequence in $\mathcal K_\theta $. By upper semicontinuity, there are points $x_ j$ with $u_ j(x_ j)=0$; passing to a subsequence, we may assume that $x_ j\to x$. Cover $X$ by finitely many connected coordinate balls, and on a slightly larger ball around each of them choose a fixed smooth function $r_ i$ such that every $u_ j+r_ i$ is plurisubharmonic; such corrections exist because $\theta $ is smooth. Since $u_ j\leq 0$, these local functions are uniformly bounded above.

On a ball containing $x$ in its interior, locally uniform convergence to $-\infty $ is impossible, because the values $(u_ j+r_ i)(x_ j)=r_ i(x_ j)$ stay bounded below. Local compactness therefore gives a subsequence converging in $L^1$ on this ball. If a second ball meets the first, local compactness on the second ball cannot yield convergence to $-\infty $ on compact subsets of the intersection, since the first limit is finite almost everywhere there. Thus a further subsequence converges in $L^1$ on the second ball. Since $X$ is connected, the intersection graph of the finite cover by connected balls is connected. Proceeding along this graph and passing to subsequences finitely many times, we obtain local $L^1$ convergence on every ball, and a partition of unity then gives convergence in $L^1(X)$ to a function $u$.

The inequalities $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\geq 0$ pass to the limit in the sense of distributions. By proposition 1.1.2, $u$ has a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic representative, still denoted by $u$. Since $u_ j\leq 0$, we have $u\leq 0$ almost everywhere, hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3.

By lemma 1.4.1, $0=\lim _ j u_ j(x_ j)\leq u(x)$. Thus $u(x)=0$, and the limit belongs to $\mathcal K_\theta $. This proves sequential compactness, which is compactness in the metric space $L^1(X)$. The norm bound follows, since a compact subset of $L^1(X)$ is bounded. $\square$

Corollary 1.4.3. Let $(u_ j)$ be a decreasing sequence in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with uniformly bounded $L^1$ norms. Then its pointwise limit $u$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$; in particular, $u$ is not identically $-\infty $. Moreover, $u_ j\to u$ in $L^1$.

Proof. On each connected coordinate ball, after a common smooth correction, the limit of a decreasing sequence of plurisubharmonic functions is either plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $. Fatou's lemma, applied to the negative parts, excludes the second alternative because of the $L^1$ bound. Hence $u$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, and the inequality $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ passes to the decreasing limit. Finally, the nonnegative functions $u_1-u_ j$ increase to $u_1-u$, so monotone convergence gives $u_ j\to u$ in $L^1$. $\square$

1.5 Uniform exponential integrability

For a single quasi-plurisubharmonic function $v$ on $X$, Skoda's local integrability theorem, recalled in [SGPT], applies after local smooth corrections. Taking the smallest exponent over a finite cover gives $\mathrm{e}^{-a(v)v}\in L^1(X)$ for some $a(v)>0$. Normalized compactness makes the exponent and the bound for the integral uniform: otherwise, a convergent weighted sum of counterexamples would violate this qualitative statement.

Let $K_\omega $ be the constant of theorem 1.4.2 for the background $\omega $, so that every $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\sup _ Xv=0$ satisfies

1.1
\begin{equation} \int _ X|v|\, \mathrm{d}V\leq K_\omega . \end{equation}

Lemma 1.5.1. There are constants $\alpha >0$ and $C<+\infty $, depending only on $(X,\omega ,\mathrm{d}V)$, such that

\[ \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha v}\, \mathrm{d}V\leq C \quad \hbox{for all }v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\hbox{ with }\sup _ Xv=0. \]

Proof. Suppose that no such pair exists. Set $a_ j=2^{-j}$ and choose normalized potentials $v_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with

\[ \int _ X\exp \! \left(-\frac{a_ j}{j}v_ j\right)\mathrm{d}V\geq 2^ j. \]

The partial sums $V_ N=\sum _{j\leq N}a_ jv_ j$ decrease, since every $v_ j\leq 0$. They belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ by proposition 1.2.2, because $\sum _ ja_ j\leq 1$, and their $L^1$ norms are uniformly bounded by (1.1). By corollary 1.4.3, their limit $V$ is therefore $\omega $-plurisubharmonic. Since all summands are nonpositive, $V\leq a_ jv_ j$ for every $j$.

Fix $p>0$. For every $j\geq 1/p$,

\[ \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{-pV}\, \mathrm{d}V \geq \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{-pa_ jv_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V \geq \int _ X\mathrm{e}^{-(a_ j/j)v_ j}\, \mathrm{d}V \geq 2^ j. \]

Thus $\mathrm{e}^{-pV}$ is not integrable for any $p>0$, which contradicts the integrability of $\mathrm{e}^{-a(V)V}$ obtained above from Skoda's theorem. $\square$

1.6 Local products and capacity

Bedford–Taylor products of bounded plurisubharmonic functions, and the capacity defined through them, measure the exceptional sets of plurisubharmonic functions and control bounded approximation. We recall these classical facts of local pluripotential theory from [SGPT, GZ17]. The plurifine topology is the coarsest topology that makes all local plurisubharmonic functions continuous. It is finer than the ordinary topology; in particular, strict superlevel sets of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions are plurifine open.

Theorem 1.6.1 (Bedford–Taylor). Let $1\leq k\leq n$. If $p_1,\ldots ,p_ k$ are locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on a domain in $\mathbb C^ n$, their mixed product $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_ k$ is a positive closed current of order zero. It is symmetric, multilinear in its current factors, and continuous under simultaneous locally bounded decreasing approximation. It does not charge pluripolar sets and is local in the plurifine topology.

Here plurifine locality means that if two families of such functions agree on a plurifine open Borel set, then their products have the same restriction to that set.

Products multiplied by a bounded potential need more than the weak convergence of the unweighted products in theorem 1.6.1. An auxiliary complex variable recovers the weight from an ordinary mixed product in one more dimension, including at top degree.

Lemma 1.6.2. Let $a_ j\downarrow a$ and $b_{i,j}\downarrow b_ i$, $1\leq i\leq r\leq n$, be locally uniformly bounded plurisubharmonic functions on a domain in $\mathbb C^ n$. Then

\[ a_ j\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_{1,j}\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_{r,j} \longrightarrow a\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_ r \]

weakly as currents. The same holds for quasi-plurisubharmonic functions that become plurisubharmonic after fixed smooth corrections, and after wedging with smooth forms that converge locally in $C^\infty $.

Proof. First let $r=n$, and write $\mu _ j=\bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_{i,j}$ and $\mu =\bigwedge _ i\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_ i$. Work over a relatively compact ball on which $-M\leq a_ j,a\leq M$. On the product of this ball with a disk of radius $R$ in an auxiliary variable $w$, where $\log R^2>M+1$, set

\[ v_ j(z,w)=\max \{ a_ j(z),\log |w|^2\} ,\qquad v(z,w)=\max \{ a(z),\log |w|^2\} . \]

These are locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions, also at $w=0$, and $v_ j\downarrow v$. Pulling back the functions $b_{i,j}$ and $b_ i$ to the product and applying theorem 1.6.1 in dimension $n+1$, we obtain

\[ T_ j=\mu _ j\wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j\longrightarrow T=\mu \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v. \]

Since $\mu $ is closed, $T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v\mu )$. As $\mu $ has top degree in the base variables, only derivatives in $w$ contribute. Testing against product test functions and applying Fubini's theorem therefore identifies $T$ with $\mu (\mathrm dz)$ times the circle measure

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ w\max \{ a(z),\log |w|^2\} =\sigma _{\exp (a(z)/2)}. \]

Here $\sigma _ t$ is the uniform probability measure on the circle $|w|=t$: the radial derivative jumps from zero to $2/t$ there, so the Euclidean Laplacian has total mass $4\pi $, and our normalization of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ divides that mass by $4\pi $. This distributional computation is valid for every bounded Borel function $a$; no continuity in $z$ is needed. The same description holds for $T_ j$.

Choose a smooth radial compactly supported function $\vartheta (w)$, supported away from zero and from the circle $|w|=R$, that equals $\log |w|^2$ on all circles with $-M\leq \log |w|^2\leq M$. For every smooth compactly supported test function $\xi $ on the base,

\[ \langle T_ j,\xi \vartheta \rangle =\int \xi a_ j\, \mathrm d\mu _ j, \qquad \langle T,\xi \vartheta \rangle =\int \xi a\, \mathrm d\mu . \]

The convergence $T_ j\to T$ therefore proves the top-degree assertion.

For $r<n$, wedge with $n-r$ constant positive $(1,1)$-forms, each the Hessian of a positive quadratic function, and apply the top-degree case. By polarization, the products of positive constant $(1,1)$-forms span the real constant forms of bidegree $(n-r,n-r)$, and multiplying them by smooth test functions recovers every coefficient of the currents. For quasi-plurisubharmonic functions, add the fixed smooth corrections to the decreasing sequences, apply the result to the corrected functions, and expand; the term involving the correction to $a_ j$ converges by the ordinary decreasing continuity of the products. Finally, the local Chern–Levine–Nirenberg bounds show that these finitely many products have uniformly bounded variation on compact sets. This allows smooth forms converging locally in $C^\infty $ to replace fixed forms in the tests. $\square$

For concentric coordinate balls $B'\Subset B$ and a Borel set $E\subset B'$, the relative Bedford–Taylor capacity is

\[ \operatorname {cap}(E,B)=\sup \Bigl \{ \int _ E(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n:v\in \mathrm{PSH}(B),\ 0\leq v\leq 1\Bigr \} . \]

If $v$ is bounded, nonconstant and plurisubharmonic on $B$, applying the definition to $(v-\inf _ Bv)/(\sup _ Bv-\inf _ Bv)$ gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n(E)\leq (\sup _ Bv-\inf _ Bv)^ n\operatorname {cap}(E,B); \]

for constant $v$ both sides vanish. Write $\operatorname {Vol}(E)=\int _ E\mathrm{d}V$, where $\mathrm{d}V$ is a fixed smooth positive volume form. We use two standard local estimates on these balls, with constants $C,a>0$ depending only on the nested balls and on $\mathrm{d}V$:

  1. If $w\leq 0$ is plurisubharmonic on $B$ and $v$ is bounded and plurisubharmonic on $B$, then

    \[ \int _{B'}|w|(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n \leq C\| w\| _{L^1(B)}\| v\| _{L^\infty (B)}^ n. \]

    Since adding a constant to $v$ does not change $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$, one may first normalize $v$ by such a constant. This is the weighted Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality.

  2. For every Borel set $E\subset B'$,

    \[ \operatorname {Vol}(E)\leq C\exp \bigl (-a\operatorname {cap}(E,B)^{-1/n}\bigr ), \]

    where the right-hand side is zero when the capacity is zero. This is the classical local volume–capacity estimate.

Both are classical results of local pluripotential theory [Chapters 3 and 4, GZ17]; the conventions for bounded products and capacity in [Chapter 2, SGPT] agree with these up to fixed constants.

The dependence on the range of the potential can be read off from the usual cutoff argument. Let $M>0$ and let $p$ be plurisubharmonic on $B$ with $0\leq p\leq M$. Let $\kappa =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ be the Euclidean form, and choose a cutoff function $\chi $ supported in $B$, with $0\leq \chi \leq 1$, equal to one on a smaller ball and satisfying $-C\kappa \leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \leq C\kappa $. Integration by parts gives, for $1\leq j\leq n$,

\[ \begin{split} \int \chi (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ j\wedge \kappa ^{n-j} & =\int p\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^{j-1}\wedge \kappa ^{n-j}\\ & \leq CM\int _{\operatorname {supp}\chi }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^{j-1}\wedge \kappa ^{n-j+1}. \end{split} \]

Iterating this estimate with nested cutoffs between $B'$ and $B$, and stopping at degree one or at degree zero, we obtain respectively

1.4
\begin{equation} \begin{split} \int _{B'}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ j\wedge \kappa ^{n-j} & \leq C_ jM^{j-1}\int _ B\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p\wedge \kappa ^{n-1},\\ \int _{B'}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ j\wedge \kappa ^{n-j} & \leq C_ jM^ j. \end{split} \end{equation}

The constants depend only on the nested balls and on $j$. For bounded $p$, the integrations by parts are justified by local decreasing regularization.

Definition 1.6.3. For a compact set $E$ in an open Euclidean ball $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$, the relative extremal function is

\[ h_{E,B}=\sup \{ u\in \mathrm{PSH}(B):-1\leq u\leq 0,\ u|_ E\leq -1\} . \]

The constant $-1$ is a competitor, and the family is bounded above by zero. The local upper-envelope theorem therefore gives $h_{E,B}^*\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ with $-1\leq h_{E,B}^*\leq 0$. Allowing all competitors $u\leq 0$ with $u|_ E\leq -1$ gives the same supremum, because the truncation $\max (u,-1)$ of such a competitor is a competitor in the original family.

A sequence of functions $f_ j$ converges to $f$ in local capacity if $\operatorname {cap}(K\cap \{ |f_ j-f|>\epsilon \} ,B)\to 0$ for every $\epsilon >0$, every coordinate ball $B$ and every compact set $K\subset B$. An assertion holds quasi-everywhere, abbreviated q.e., if it holds outside a pluripolar set; in particular, differences of functions that may both be $-\infty $ are considered only off such a set. A positive measure is non-pluripolar if it gives zero mass to every pluripolar set.

A function is quasi-continuous if, for every $\epsilon >0$, it is continuous outside an open set of capacity less than $\epsilon $. Plurisubharmonic functions have this property for the local Bedford–Taylor capacity, and a Borel set has zero capacity if and only if it is pluripolar. A locally uniformly bounded monotone sequence of plurisubharmonic functions converges in capacity: to its limit if it decreases, and to the upper semicontinuous regularization of its limit if it increases. If uniformly bounded plurisubharmonic functions converge in capacity, their local products converge weakly, and this remains true after the products are multiplied by uniformly bounded quasi-continuous coefficients converging in capacity. These local results, recalled in [SGPT, GZ17], also apply to quasi-plurisubharmonic functions after a common smooth correction.

Theorem 1.6.4 (Mixed Monge–Ampère inequality). Let $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a domain, and let $\beta $ be its Euclidean Hermitian form. Let $a,b$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $U$, and let $f,g$ be nonnegative measurable functions on $U$ with

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a)^ n\geq f\beta ^ n, \qquad (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n\geq g\beta ^ n. \]

Then, for $0\leq k\leq n$,

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a)^ k\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^{n-k} \geq f^{k/n}g^{(n-k)/n}\beta ^ n. \]

The comparison principle of Bedford and Taylor also has mixed forms, recalled in [SGPT]. Let $a,b$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $U$ such that $E=\{ a<b\} \Subset U$, and let $S$ be a positive closed $(n-1,n-1)$-current that is a product of Hessians of bounded plurisubharmonic functions and smooth closed positive forms. Then

1.7
\begin{equation} \int _ E\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge S\leq \int _ E\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a\wedge S. \end{equation}

Indeed, apply the Bedford–Taylor integration by parts formula to $b_\epsilon =\max \{ a,b-\epsilon \} $, and let $\epsilon \downarrow 0$, using monotone continuity to pass to the limit. Replacing one factor at a time, we obtain, with $P=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$ and $Q=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b$,

1.8
\begin{equation} \int _ E P^{n-j}\wedge Q^ j\leq \int _ E P^ n, \qquad 0\leq j\leq n. \end{equation}

The local envelope theorem for bounded obstacles, recalled in [SGPT, GZ17], shows where an envelope can carry Monge–Ampère mass. Let $B$ be a ball with a smooth strictly plurisubharmonic defining function $h<0$, and let $f$ be a bounded quasi-continuous obstacle with $Ah\leq f\leq 0$ for some $A>0$. Then the envelope

\[ q=\bigl (\sup \{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(B):v\leq f\ \text{q.e.}\} \bigr )^* \]

satisfies $Ah\leq q\leq 0$, tends to zero at the boundary, and

1.9
\begin{equation} \mathbf1_{\{ q<f\} }(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^ n=0. \end{equation}

For a locally bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic function $v$ and a smooth convex increasing function $F$ on a neighborhood of the range of $v$, the bounded Bedford–Taylor chain rule reads

1.10
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F(v)=F'(v)\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v+F''(v)\, \mathrm{d}v\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}v. \end{equation}

The gradient term, and its products with positive Bedford–Taylor factors of bounded potentials, are nonnegative. These identities belong to the bounded local calculus recalled in [SGPT, GZ17]; they follow by decreasing regularization, and bounded potentials have the local Sobolev regularity needed to define the gradient term. In particular, inequalities derived from this formula survive local smoothing with a lower curvature error tending to zero.

Lemma 1.6.5. Let $B$ be a coordinate ball and let $\tau $ be a finite non-pluripolar measure with compact support in $B$. Then there is a bounded plurisubharmonic function $v$ on $B$ such that $\tau \ll (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$.

Proof. For $n\geq 2$, we use Cegrell's decomposition theorem. On a bounded hyperconvex domain in $\mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 2$, let $\mathcal E_0$ consist of the bounded negative plurisubharmonic functions with zero boundary values and finite total Monge–Ampère mass. The theorem states that every positive compactly supported measure $\tau $ has a decomposition

\[ \tau =g(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n+\tau _ s, \qquad v\in \mathcal E_0,\qquad 0\leq g\in L^1((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n), \]

where $\tau _ s$ is a positive measure carried by a pluripolar set. If $\tau $ is non-pluripolar, then $\tau _ s=0$, so $\tau \ll (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$.

In dimension one, the assertion follows directly from the Green potential. Let $a\leq 0$ be the Green potential of $\tau $ on the disk, normalized by $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a=\tau $. Then $v=\mathrm{e}^ a$ is bounded and subharmonic, and

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\geq \mathrm{e}^ a\tau . \]

To justify this inequality, set $a_ M=\max (a,-M)$. The chain rule (1.10) in dimension one gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\mathrm{e}^{a_ M}\geq \mathrm{e}^{a_ M}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a_ M$, and the right-hand side is at least $\mathrm{e}^ a\tau $ on $\{ a>-M\} $. Let $M\to \infty $, using the decreasing convergence of the bounded subharmonic functions $\mathrm{e}^{a_ M}$ on the left and monotone convergence on the right. Since $\{ a=-\infty \} $ is polar and $\tau $ charges no polar set, $\mathrm{e}^ a>0$ almost everywhere for $\tau $. Thus $\tau \ll \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$. $\square$

For a signed measure $\nu $, write $|\nu |$ for its total variation measure and $\| \nu \| _{\mathrm{TV}(B)}=|\nu |(B)$. On a curve, the Green representation controls capacity by the total variation of the Laplacian, together with an $L^1$ term for the harmonic remainder.

Lemma 1.6.6. Let $B'\Subset B$ be concentric disks in $\mathbb C$. There is a constant $C$, depending only on these disks, such that for all quasi-subharmonic functions $u,v$ on a neighborhood of $\overline B$ and all $t>0$,

1.11
\begin{equation} \operatorname {cap}(B'\cap \{ |u-v|>t\} ,B) \leq \frac Ct\left(\| u-v\| _{L^1(B)} +\| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u-v)\| _{\mathrm{TV}(B)}\right). \end{equation}

Here the difference $u-v$ is formed off the pole sets of $u$ and $v$.

Proof. Put $f=u-v$ and write the Jordan decomposition on $B$ as $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=\alpha ^+-\alpha ^-$. Let $g^\pm \leq 0$ be the Green potentials of $\alpha ^\pm $ on $B$ with zero boundary values. The $L^1(B)$ norm of the Green kernel of the disk is bounded uniformly in its pole, so

\[ \| g^+\| _{L^1(B)}+\| g^-\| _{L^1(B)} \leq C\| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f\| _{\mathrm{TV}(B)}. \]

Indeed, on the unit disk the kernel is $\log |(z-\zeta )/(1-\overline\zeta z)|^2$; its logarithmic singularity is uniformly integrable, and $|1-\overline\zeta z|\leq 2$. Scaling gives the bound on $B$.

The distribution $h=f-g^++g^-$ is harmonic, and the interior mean-value estimate gives

\[ \| h\| _{L^\infty (B')} \leq C\left(\| f\| _{L^1(B)}+\| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f\| _{\mathrm{TV}(B)}\right). \]

The identity $u+g^-=v+g^++h$ holds almost everywhere, hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3. Thus $|u-v|\leq -g^+-g^-+|h|$ off the polar sets of $u$ and $v$. Let $0\leq a\leq 1$ be a subharmonic test function on $B$. Since the measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$ charges no polar set, this pointwise bound, the weighted Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimate and (1.4) yield

\[ \int _{B'}|u-v|\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a \leq C\left(\| f\| _{L^1(B)}+\| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f\| _{\mathrm{TV}(B)}\right). \]

Restricting the integral to $B'\cap \{ |u-v|>t\} $, dividing by $t$ and taking the supremum over $a$ proves (1.11). $\square$

1.6.1 Polar parts on curves

Every locally finite signed measure $\nu $ on a Riemann surface splits uniquely as the sum of its polar part $\nu ^{\mathrm{pol}}$, which is carried by a polar Borel set, and a measure that charges no polar set. To construct the splitting locally, choose countably many polar Borel sets whose $|\nu |$-masses approach the supremum over all polar Borel sets, and restrict $\nu $ to their union, which is again polar. Uniqueness allows these local decompositions to be patched together and shows that $\nu \mapsto \nu ^{\mathrm{pol}}$ is linear.

For a locally quasi-subharmonic function $u$, the polar curvature of $u$ is the positive measure $\mu _ u=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^{\mathrm{pol}}=\mathbf1_{\{ u=-\infty \} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. The second equality holds because smooth corrections do not change polar parts, while bounded truncations and plurifine locality show that $\mathbf1_{\{ u>-j\} }\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ charges no polar set. The following inverse maximum principle shows that an upper bound for the difference of two potentials orders their polar curvatures.

Lemma 1.6.7. Let $B\subset \mathbb C$ be a disk and let $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$ have a distributional Laplacian that is a signed Radon measure. If $f\leq 0$ almost everywhere, then $(\Delta f)^{\mathrm{pol}}\geq 0$.

Proof. It suffices to prove $\Delta f(K)\geq 0$ for every compact polar set $K\subset B$; restriction to smaller disks and inner regularity then give the assertion. Choose open neighborhoods $O_ j$ of $K$ with smooth boundaries such that $\overline O_{j+1}\subset O_ j\Subset B$ and $\bigcap _ j\overline O_ j=K$. Let $h_ j$ be the function that equals one on $\overline O_ j$, is harmonic on $B\setminus \overline O_ j$, and vanishes on $\partial B$. The Dirichlet problem on this smooth domain shows that $h_ j$ is continuous with $0\leq h_ j\leq 1$. Its outward normal derivative on $\partial O_ j$ is nonpositive, so the jump of the derivative across $\partial O_ j$ gives $\Delta h_ j\leq 0$ on $B$ in the sense of distributions. The maximum principle gives $h_{j+1}\leq h_ j$.

Since $0\leq h_ j\leq 1$, the sequence converges in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B)$. Let $h$ be the superharmonic representative of this limit; it may differ from the pointwise limit on $K$. It is harmonic off $K$, so its Laplacian is supported on the polar set $K$. On the other hand, $-h$ is bounded and subharmonic, so its Laplacian charges no polar set, by theorem 1.6.1. Hence $h$ is harmonic on $B$. Since $0\leq h\leq h_1$ and $h_1$ tends to zero at $\partial B$, the maximum principle gives $h=0$. It follows that $h_ j\to 0$ smoothly on compact subsets of $B\setminus K$, while $h_ j=1$ on $K$.

Choose $0\leq \chi \in C_ c^\infty (B)$ equal to one near $K$. For large $j$, regularize $h_ j$ by convolution on a neighborhood of $\operatorname {supp}\chi $, obtaining smooth functions $h_{j,\epsilon }$ with $\Delta h_{j,\epsilon }\leq 0$. Since $f\leq 0$,

\[ \begin{split} \int _ B\chi h_{j,\epsilon }\, \mathrm{d}(\Delta f) & =\int _ B f\, \Delta (\chi h_{j,\epsilon })\, \mathrm{d}x\, \mathrm{d}y\\ & \geq \int _ B f\bigl (2\nabla \chi \cdot \nabla h_{j,\epsilon } +h_{j,\epsilon }\Delta \chi \bigr )\, \mathrm{d}x\, \mathrm{d}y. \end{split} \]

For fixed $j$, the convolutions converge uniformly to $h_ j$ on $\operatorname {supp}\chi $, because $h_ j$ is continuous. On the support of the derivatives of $\chi $, the function $h_ j$ is harmonic for all large $j$. We may therefore first let $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ and then let $j\to \infty $. The smooth convergence away from $K$ makes the right-hand side tend to zero, and dominated convergence for the signed measure $\Delta f$ makes the left-hand side tend to $\Delta f(K)$. This proves $\Delta f(K)\geq 0$. $\square$

Corollary 1.6.8. Let $u,v$ be locally quasi-subharmonic functions on a Riemann surface. If $u\leq v+C$ on an open set for some constant $C$, then $\mu _ u\geq \mu _ v$ there. Moreover,

1.14
\begin{equation} \mu _{\max (u,v)}=\mu _ u\wedge \mu _ v, \end{equation}

where the right-hand side is the greatest measure dominated by both $\mu _ u$ and $\mu _ v$.

Proof. Apply lemma 1.6.7 to $u-v-C$ in local coordinates. Since smooth functions have zero polar part, linearity of the polar part gives the first assertion. Applying it to $u-\max (u,v)$ and to $v-\max (u,v)$ gives the inequality $\leq $ in (1.14).

For the reverse inequality, add a common smooth correction to make $u$ and $v$ subharmonic on a coordinate disk. On a smaller disk, let $g$ be the Green potential of the restriction of $\lambda =\mu _ u\wedge \mu _ v$ to that disk, so that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g=\lambda $. The distributions $u-g$ and $v-g$ have positive Hessians and hence subharmonic representatives $a$ and $b$. Since $\max (u,v)=g+\max (a,b)$ almost everywhere, the Hessian of $\max (u,v)$ dominates $\lambda $. Restricting this inequality to a polar Borel set carrying $\lambda $ proves the reverse inequality on the smaller disk, and these local statements give it on the whole surface. $\square$

1.6.2 Products at small pole sets

A top-degree product can carry mass on a pole set, and the non-pluripolar product discards this mass. When the unbounded locus is sufficiently small, the full local current product is well defined. Singularity order then compares its residual measures on the pole set, and bounded perturbations leave these measures unchanged.

Theorem 1.6.9. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a domain and $S\subset \Omega $ a closed set of Hausdorff dimension zero. Suppose that $u_1,\ldots ,u_ q$, $1\leq q\leq n$, are plurisubharmonic on $\Omega $ and locally bounded on $\Omega \setminus S$. Then their inductively defined full current product

\[ \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\bigr )_{\mathrm{full}} \]

is a closed positive current of order zero. It is symmetric, multilinear, local in the ordinary topology, and continuous under simultaneous decreasing approximation by plurisubharmonic functions. The weighted current $u_1\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_2\wedge \cdots \wedge \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ q\bigr )_{\mathrm{full}}$ has coefficients of locally finite variation. These full products may charge $S$.

Proof. Apply the classical theorem on products with unbounded potentials, together with its monotone continuity statement, in [Chapter III, Theorem 4.5 and Proposition 4.9, pp. 152–156, DemCADG], with the underlying current $T=1$. For every choice of $m$ of the functions, that theorem requires the intersection of their unbounded loci to have zero Hausdorff measure of dimension $2n-2m+1$. This intersection lies in $S$, and $2n-2m+1\geq 1$, so the measure is zero. The same hypothesis gives local integrability of the weighted current. Symmetry, multilinearity and locality on open sets follow from the local construction of the currents and decreasing regularization. $\square$

The subscript distinguishes this product from the non-pluripolar product used later; the two coincide for locally bounded functions.

Lemma 1.6.10. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a domain and $S\subset \Omega $ a closed set of Hausdorff dimension zero. Let $a,b$ be plurisubharmonic on $\Omega $ and locally bounded off $S$, with $\{ a=-\infty \} =S$. If $a\leq b+C$ near $S$ for some finite constant $C$, then, as positive Radon measures,

\[ \mathbf1_ S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n_{\mathrm{full}} \leq \mathbf1_ S(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a)^ n_{\mathrm{full}}. \]

If $a-b$ is bounded near $S$, equality holds.

Proof. Fix a compact set $E\subset S$ and $\varepsilon >0$, and put $w_ c=\max \{ b-c,(1+\varepsilon )a\} $ for $c>0$. Since $a=-\infty $ on the compact set $E$, upper semicontinuity gives a neighborhood of $E$ on which $a<-(C+c)/\varepsilon $ and $a\leq b+C$. Off $S$, on this neighborhood, $b-c-(1+\varepsilon )a\geq -C-c-\varepsilon a>0$, while on $S$ the second branch of the maximum equals $-\infty $. Thus $w_ c=b-c$ near $E$, and locality on open sets gives $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w_ c)^ n_{\mathrm{full}}(E)=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n_{\mathrm{full}}(E)$.

As $c\to \infty $, the functions $w_ c$ decrease to $(1+\varepsilon )a$. Theorem 1.6.9 applies to every $w_ c$ with the same set $S$, so their full measures converge weakly to $(1+\varepsilon )^ n(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a)^ n_{\mathrm{full}}$. Since the mass of a compact set is upper semicontinuous under weak convergence of positive measures, we obtain

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n_{\mathrm{full}}(E) \leq (1+\varepsilon )^ n(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a)^ n_{\mathrm{full}}(E). \]

Here we apply the semicontinuity after multiplying the measures by a fixed nonnegative cutoff function with compact support, equal to one near $E$; both masses on $E$ are finite by the theorem. Letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ and applying inner regularity to all compact sets $E\subset S$ gives the asserted inequality of measures. If $a-b$ is bounded near $S$, then also $\{ b=-\infty \} =S$, so the roles of $a$ and $b$ can be interchanged, and equality follows. $\square$

1.6.3 Sobolev regularity at divisorial poles

For a potential that is a convex function of $\log |f|$, with $f$ holomorphic, the growth of the convex function at $-\infty $ determines which derivatives are integrable near the divisor of $f$. The gradient and the classical Laplacian have different thresholds. Moreover, the distributional Laplacian keeps a measure on the divisor unless the slope of the convex function tends to zero.

Let $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a sufficiently small neighborhood of $0$, and let $f\not\equiv 0$ be a holomorphic function on $U$ with $f(0)=0$. Write

\[ f=g\prod _{j=1}^ N h_ j^{m_ j},\qquad m_0=\min _ jm_ j, \]

where $g$ is a holomorphic unit and the $h_ j$ are distinct irreducible germs represented on $U$. Fix a convex nondecreasing function $\chi \in C^2(({-\infty },A])$ and shrink $U$ so that $\log |f|<A-1$. Set $v=\chi (\log |f|)$ off $Z=\{ f=0\} $; this function is plurisubharmonic and bounded above, and we extend it across $Z$ by lemma 1.3.7. In this subsection, gradients, Laplacians, Sobolev spaces and the volume form $\mathrm dV$ are the real Euclidean ones. Write $|\partial f|^2=\sum _ j|\partial f/\partial z_ j|^2$, so that $|\nabla |f||=|\partial f|$ off $Z$.

Lemma 1.6.11. After shrinking $U$, there is a constant $C>0$ such that

\[ |\partial f|\leq C|f|^{1-1/m_0}\quad \text{on }U\setminus Z. \]

For each compact set $K\subset U$, there are $C_ K,r_ K>0$ with

\[ \int _{K\cap \{ |f|=r\} }|\partial f|\, \mathrm dS\leq C_ Kr \qquad (0<r<r_ K), \]

where $\mathrm dS$ is the Euclidean surface measure on the real hypersurface $\{ |f|=r\} $. In particular, for every nonnegative measurable function $\Psi $,

\[ \int _{K\cap \{ 0<|f|<r_ K\} }\Psi (|f|)|\partial f|^2\, \mathrm dV \leq C_ K\int _0^{r_ K}\Psi (r)r\, \mathrm dr. \]

Proof. Shrink $U$ so that $|h_ j|\leq 1$ and the unit $g$, its inverse and the first derivatives of $g$ and of all $h_ j$ are bounded. For each $j$, $|f|^{1/m_0}\leq C|h_ j|^{m_ j/m_0}\leq C|h_ j|$. Logarithmic differentiation of the factorization therefore gives

\[ \frac{|\partial f|}{|f|} \leq C\left(1+\sum _ j|h_ j|^{-1}\right) \leq C|f|^{-1/m_0}, \]

which proves the first bound.

For small $\zeta \in \mathbb C$, let $\mu _\zeta $ be the Euclidean volume measure of the fiber divisor $\{ f=\zeta \} $, counted with multiplicity. As $\zeta \to 0$, we have $\log |f-\zeta |\to \log |f|$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$: by the local compactness theorem recalled at the beginning of section 1.4, it suffices to identify subsequential limits, and the convergence is locally uniform off $Z$, which identifies every such limit and excludes convergence to $-\infty $. The Poincaré–Lelong formula therefore gives weak convergence of the fiber currents, and hence of their Euclidean trace measures. Pairing with a fixed nonnegative cutoff function equal to one near $K$ shows that $\sup _{|\zeta |<r_ K}\mu _\zeta (K)<\infty $ for some $r_ K>0$.

The critical locus of $f$ has finitely many local components, and $f$ is constant on each of them. After shrinking $U$, every remaining critical component passes through $0$, where $f$ vanishes. Thus the small nonzero fibers are smooth. On $\{ |f|=r\} $, the coarea formula for the map $f$ to the circle of radius $r$ gives

\[ \int _{K\cap \{ |f|=r\} }|\partial f|\, \mathrm dS =\int _{|\zeta |=r}\mu _\zeta (K)\, \mathrm dS(\zeta ) \leq C_ Kr. \]

The coarea formula for the function $|f|$, together with $|\nabla |f||=|\partial f|$, proves the last inequality. The same formulas hold in dimension one, where the fiber volume is the counting measure. $\square$

Proposition 1.6.12. For each $2\leq p<\infty $,

\[ v\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \int _{-\infty }^ A|\chi '(s)|^ p \mathrm e^{(2-p)s/m_0}\, \mathrm ds<\infty . \]

In this case, the weak gradient of $v$ is the zero extension of $\chi '(\log |f|)\nabla \log |f|$ from $U\setminus Z$.

Proof. At a smooth point of a component of multiplicity $m_0$, outside the other components, multiplying the defining function of the component by a holomorphic $m_0$-th root of the remaining unit gives a normal coordinate $w_1$ with $f=w_1^{m_0}$. Coordinate changes with bounded derivatives and Jacobians preserve local first-order Sobolev integrability. With $r=|w_1|$ and $s=m_0\log r$, polar integration of the classical gradient gives, up to fixed positive factors,

\[ \int _0^{r_0}|\chi '(m_0\log r)|^ p r^{1-p}\, \mathrm dr =\frac1{m_0}\int _{-\infty }^{m_0\log r_0} |\chi '(s)|^ p\mathrm e^{(2-p)s/m_0}\, \mathrm ds. \]

Such smooth points occur in every neighborhood of $0$. This proves necessity, since changing the finite upper endpoint does not affect convergence.

For sufficiency, put

\[ s_\varepsilon =\tfrac 12\log (|f|^2+\varepsilon ^2), \qquad v_\varepsilon =\chi (s_\varepsilon ). \]

For small $\varepsilon >0$, these are $C^2$ plurisubharmonic functions decreasing to $v$. By convexity, $|\chi (s)|\leq C(1+|s|)$ for $s\leq A$, so the local exponential integrability of $\log |f|$, recalled in [SGPT], gives convergence in every local $L^ p$ space with $p<\infty $. By the chain rule,

\[ |\nabla v_\varepsilon | =\chi '(s_\varepsilon ) \frac{|f|}{|f|^2+\varepsilon ^2}|\partial f|. \]

Apply lemma 1.6.11 with the coarea formula, keeping two powers of $|\partial f|$ for the level estimate and bounding the other $p-2$ powers by the first inequality of the lemma. The local integral of $|\nabla v_\varepsilon |^ p$ over $K\cap \{ 0<|f|<r_ K\} $ is then bounded by

\[ C\int _0^{r_ K} |\chi '(\tfrac 12\log (r^2+\varepsilon ^2))|^ p \frac{r^{p+(p-2)(1-1/m_0)+1}}{(r^2+\varepsilon ^2)^ p} \, \mathrm dr. \]

With $s=\tfrac 12\log (r^2+\varepsilon ^2)$, we have $\mathrm ds=r\, \mathrm dr/(r^2+\varepsilon ^2)$ and $r\leq (r^2+\varepsilon ^2)^{1/2}$, so this is at most

\[ C\int _{-\infty }^ A|\chi '(s)|^ p \mathrm e^{(2-p)s/m_0}\, \mathrm ds. \]

The integral over the part where $|f|\geq r_ K$ is uniformly bounded as well. Weak compactness in Sobolev spaces and the local $L^ p$ convergence therefore show that $v\in W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Its weak gradient agrees with the classical one off $Z$, and $Z$ has Lebesgue measure zero, which proves the final assertion. $\square$

Proposition 1.6.13. Let $G$ be the zero extension of $\chi ''(\log |f|)|\nabla \log |f||^2$ from $U\setminus Z$, and put $\ell =\lim _{s\to -\infty }\chi '(s)$. For each $1\leq p<\infty $,

\[ G\in L^ p_{\mathrm{loc}}(U) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \int _{-\infty }^ A|\chi ''(s)|^ p \mathrm e^{2(1-p)s/m_0}\, \mathrm ds<\infty . \]

In the sense of distributions,

\[ \Delta v=G+\ell \, \Delta \log |f|, \]

where $\Delta \log |f|$ is a positive measure carried by the divisor of $f$. In particular, $\Delta v$ is represented by a locally $L^ p$ function exactly when $\ell =0$ and the integral above is finite.

Proof. The limit $\ell \geq 0$ exists because $\chi '$ is nonnegative and nondecreasing. Near a smooth point of a component of multiplicity $m_0$, outside the other components, the coordinate reduction to $f=w_1^{m_0}$ makes the positive complex Hessian of $v$ off $Z$ a single factor in the normal direction. Its Euclidean traces in the two coordinate systems are uniformly comparable, so polar integration gives necessity. In general, $G=\chi ''(\log |f|)|\partial f|^2/|f|^2$ off $Z$. The coarea formula and lemma 1.6.11, now bounding the remaining $2p-2$ powers of $|\partial f|$ by the first inequality of the lemma, give

\[ \int _{K\cap \{ 0<|f|<r_ K\} }G^ p\, \mathrm dV \leq C\int _0^{r_ K}|\chi ''(\log r)|^ p r^{-1-2(p-1)/m_0}\, \mathrm dr. \]

The substitution $s=\log r$ proves sufficiency. For $p=1$ the integral is always finite, since $\int _{-\infty }^ A\chi ''=\chi '(A)-\ell $.

For the distributional identity, use $s_\varepsilon $ and $v_\varepsilon $ from the preceding proof. Direct differentiation gives

\[ \begin{aligned} \Delta v_\varepsilon & =\chi ''(s_\varepsilon ) \frac{|f|^2|\partial f|^2}{(|f|^2+\varepsilon ^2)^2} +\chi '(s_\varepsilon )\Delta s_\varepsilon ,\\ \Delta s_\varepsilon & =\frac{2\varepsilon ^2|\partial f|^2}{(|f|^2+\varepsilon ^2)^2}. \end{aligned} \]

Off $Z$, the first term tends locally uniformly to $G$. By the level estimate of lemma 1.6.11 and the same substitution, its integral over $K\cap \{ |f|<r_0\} $ is at most

\[ C\int _{-\infty }^{\frac12\log (r_0^2+\varepsilon ^2)} \chi ''(s)\, \mathrm ds. \]

This bound tends to zero if we first let $\varepsilon \to 0$ and then $r_0\to 0$, so the first term converges to $G$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

The positive measures $\Delta s_\varepsilon $ converge weakly to $\Delta \log |f|$, with locally bounded masses, and the Poincaré–Lelong formula shows that this limit is a measure supported on $Z$. On $\{ |f|<r_0\} $,

\[ 0\leq \chi '(s_\varepsilon )-\ell \leq \chi '(\tfrac 12\log (r_0^2+\varepsilon ^2))-\ell , \]

while $\Delta s_\varepsilon \to 0$ uniformly on compact subsets of $\{ |f|\geq r_0\} $. Taking the same successive limits shows that $(\chi '(s_\varepsilon )-\ell )\Delta s_\varepsilon \to 0$ weakly. Since $v_\varepsilon \to v$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$, passing to the limit in the formula for $\Delta v_\varepsilon $ gives the identity. If $\ell >0$, the measure $\ell \, \Delta \log |f|$ is nonzero and singular with respect to Lebesgue measure; uniqueness of the Lebesgue decomposition therefore proves the final criterion. $\square$

Example 1.6.14. Shrink $U$ so that $\log |f|<-2$, and let $0<a<1$. Then the plurisubharmonic function

\[ v_ a=-(-\log |f|)^ a \]

has $\Delta v_ a\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$, while

\[ v_ a\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}} \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad a<\tfrac 12. \]

It belongs to no $W^{1,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ with $p>2$ and to no $W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

Proof. Here $\chi (s)=-(-s)^ a$ is of class $C^2$ on $(-\infty ,-1]$, so the preceding propositions apply with $A=-1$. Moreover, $\chi '(s)=a(-s)^{a-1}$ and $\chi ''(s)=a(1-a)(-s)^{a-2}$, so $\ell =0$. The two propositions give the assertion about the Laplacian and the first-order thresholds. At $a=1/2$, the first-order integral diverges logarithmically; for $p>2$, its exponential factor forces divergence.

For second derivatives, take a smooth point of a component of multiplicity $m$, outside the other components, with a normal coordinate $w_1$ such that $f=w_1^ m$. Write $F(r)=\chi (m\log r)$ with $r=|w_1|$. The real second derivative of $v_ a$ in the unit direction tangent to the circles $|w_1|=r$ equals $F'(r)/r=m\chi '(m\log r)/r^2$. Thus the integral of its absolute value over the normal disk is bounded below, up to a fixed positive factor, by

\[ \int _0^{r_0}\chi '(m\log r)\frac{\mathrm dr}{r} =\frac1m\int _{-\infty }^{m\log r_0}\chi '(s)\, \mathrm ds =+\infty . \]

Hence the classical Hessian of $v_ a$ off the divisor is not locally integrable, whereas local integrability is necessary for $W^{2,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Since smooth coordinate changes preserve this Sobolev class, the argument is complete. $\square$

1.6.4 Spherical Monge–Ampère measures

A bounded radial maximum gives a measure concentrated on a real hypersurface. This supplies singular measures that still give zero mass to pluripolar sets.

Lemma 1.6.15. For $r>0$, let $\sigma _ r$ be the normalized Euclidean surface measure on $\{ |z|=r\} \subset \mathbb C^ n$. Then

\[ \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log \max \{ |z|^2,r^2\} \bigr )^ n=\sigma _ r. \]

In particular, $\sigma _ r$ does not charge pluripolar sets.

Proof. Write $t=\log |z|^2$ and approximate $\max \{ t,\log r^2\} $ uniformly by smooth convex increasing functions $F_\epsilon (t)$ whose derivatives equal zero below $\log r^2-\epsilon $ and one above $\log r^2+\epsilon $. Their compositions with $t$ extend smoothly across zero. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}t$ has rank $n-1$ off zero, the chain rule gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F_\epsilon (t))^ n =n(F_\epsilon ')^{n-1}F_\epsilon ''\, \mathrm dt\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}t \wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}t)^{n-1}. \]

The integral of $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}t\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}t)^{n-1}$ over each sphere is one. Indeed, $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}t$ integrates to one on the Hopf circle, and the induced form on $\mathbb P^{n-1}$ is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (1+|w|^2)$; its top power has density $(n-1)!\pi ^{-(n-1)}(1+|w|^2)^{-n}$ on the affine chart, whose integral is one. Thus each displayed radial measure has total mass $\int ( (F_\epsilon ')^ n)'\, \mathrm dt=1$. Its support shrinks to the sphere, and its rotation invariance identifies the limit as $\sigma _ r$. Uniform bounded-potential convergence identifies that limit with the product in the statement. Non-pluripolarity follows from theorem 1.6.1. $\square$

1.6.5 Measure concentration under integrable convergence

Decreasing approximation controls Bedford–Taylor products, but convergence of the potentials in $L^1$ alone does not. If plurisubharmonic functions tend to zero in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and are locally uniformly bounded, the range estimate (1.4) shows that their Monge–Ampère measures tend to zero. Without a common bound, smooth bounded potentials that tend to zero, even in capacity, can have Monge–Ampère measures converging to a point mass.

Lemma 1.6.16. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a domain. Suppose that $u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(\Omega )\cap L^\infty _{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega )$ tend to zero in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega )$ and satisfy $\sup _ j\| u_ j\| _{L^\infty (K)}<\infty $ for every compact set $K\subset \Omega $. Then $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n(K)\to 0$ for every such $K$.

Proof. Fix concentric balls $B'\Subset B\Subset B_1\Subset \Omega $, and put $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$. If $\psi $ is a nonnegative smooth cutoff function supported in $B_1$ and equal to one near $\overline B$, integration by parts gives

\[ 0\leq \int _ B\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\wedge \beta ^{n-1} \leq \int _{B_1}u_ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi \wedge \beta ^{n-1} \leq C\| u_ j\| _{L^1(B_1)}\longrightarrow 0. \]

Choose $M>0$ with $|u_ j|\leq M$ on $B$ for all $j$, and apply the first estimate in (1.4) to $u_ j+M$, whose values lie in $[0,2M]$. It bounds the top-degree mass of $B'$ by $C(2M)^{n-1}$ times the trace integral over $B$, which tends to zero; for $n=1$ the trace estimate itself suffices. Covering $K$ by finitely many such balls proves the lemma. $\square$

The construction uses a regularized maximum of a small multiple of a logarithm in one variable and a potential in the remaining variables. Its Monge–Ampère measure can be computed exactly by integration over circles in the first variable, and this will keep its top-degree mass fixed while the logarithmic coefficient tends to zero.

Lemma 1.6.17. Let $n\geq 2$, let $U\subset \mathbb C^{n-1}$ be a domain, and let $v$ be a smooth plurisubharmonic function on $U$. Fix $\varepsilon ,R,\eta >0$ and $A\in \mathbb R$. Let $\chi _\eta $ be the convolution of $t\mapsto \max (t,0)$ with a smooth even probability density supported in $[-\eta ,\eta ]$, and put

\[ \begin{split} q(\zeta )& =\varepsilon \log (|\zeta |^2/R^2),\qquad T(\zeta ,z')=v(z')-A-q(\zeta ),\\ h(\zeta ,z')& =q(\zeta )+\chi _\eta (T(\zeta ,z')) \end{split} \]

for $\zeta \ne 0$. Then $h$ extends to a smooth plurisubharmonic function on $\mathbb C\times U$. Set

\[ w_\eta (t)=n\chi _\eta '(t)^{n-1}\chi _\eta ''(t),\qquad r_ t(z')=R\exp \bigl ((v(z')-A-t)/(2\varepsilon )\bigr ). \]

The function $w_\eta $ is nonnegative, supported in $[-\eta ,\eta ]$, and has integral one. For every $f\in C_ c(\mathbb C\times U)$,

1.19
\begin{equation} \begin{split} \int f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n =\varepsilon \int _ U\biggl [\int _{-\eta }^{\eta }w_\eta (t) \int _0^{2\pi }f(r_ t(z')\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\vartheta },z') \frac{\mathrm d\vartheta }{2\pi }\, \mathrm dt\biggr ] (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-1}. \end{split} \end{equation}

Proof. By convexity, $0\leq \chi _\eta '\leq 1$ and $\chi _\eta ''\geq 0$. The function $\chi _\eta $ vanishes on $(-\infty ,-\eta ]$ and equals $t$ on $[\eta ,\infty )$. Since $T\to +\infty $ as $\zeta \to 0$, we have $h=v-A$ near each point with $\zeta =0$, which gives the smooth extension. Off the hyperplane $\{ \zeta =0\} $, the function $q$ is pluriharmonic and

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\chi _\eta '(T)\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v +\chi _\eta ''(T)\, \mathrm dT\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}T\geq 0. \]

The second summand has rank at most one, and the first has rank at most $n-1$. Hence

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n=n\chi _\eta '(T)^{n-1}\chi _\eta ''(T) \, \mathrm dT\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}T\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{n-1}. \]

The last factor has top degree in the variables $z'$, so the derivatives of $v$ in $\mathrm dT\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}T$ contribute nothing. Write $s=\log (|\zeta |^2/R^2)$. In polar coordinates, $\mathrm dq=\varepsilon \, \mathrm ds$ and $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}q=\varepsilon \, \mathrm d\vartheta /(2\pi )$. At fixed $z'$, the change of variables from $s$ to $t=v-A-\varepsilon s$ gives (1.19). There is no contribution near $\zeta =0$, where $h=v-A$ and the form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$ has rank at most $n-1$. Finally, $w_\eta $ is the derivative of $(\chi _\eta ')^ n$, whose values at $-\eta $ and $\eta $ are zero and one. $\square$

Proposition 1.6.18. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 2$, be a bounded domain and $a\in \Omega $. For every $c>0$, there are smooth bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_ j\leq 0$ on $\Omega $ such that

\[ \| u_ j\| _{L^ p(\Omega )}\longrightarrow 0\quad (1\leq p<\infty ), \qquad (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n\longrightarrow c\delta _ a \quad \text{vaguely}. \]

The same sequence tends to zero in local capacity. Here the norms are taken with respect to Lebesgue measure, and vague convergence means convergence against every compactly supported continuous test function.

Proof. Translate $a$ to the origin and write $z=(\zeta ,z')\in \mathbb C\times \mathbb C^ m$, $m=n-1$. It suffices to construct measures of mass one and then multiply the potentials by $c^{1/n}$. For $\delta >0$, put $H_\delta (z')=\log (|z'|^2+\delta ^2)$. With $s=|z'|^2$, the complex Hessian of $H_\delta $ has the eigenvalue $(s+\delta ^2)^{-1}$ with multiplicity $m-1$ in the directions orthogonal to $z'$ and the eigenvalue $\delta ^2(s+\delta ^2)^{-2}$ in the radial direction. Taking the determinant and integrating in polar coordinates gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\delta )^ m= \frac{m!}{\pi ^ m}\frac{\delta ^2}{(|z'|^2+\delta ^2)^{m+1}} \, \mathrm d\lambda _{2m}, \qquad \int _{|z'|<b}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\delta )^ m =\left(\frac{b^2}{b^2+\delta ^2}\right)^ m, \]

where $\lambda _{2m}$ is Lebesgue measure. These probability measures tend to $\delta _0$ as $\delta \downarrow 0$, since their mass outside each ball about the origin tends to zero.

Choose $R>\max (1,\sup _\Omega |\zeta |)$ and a ball $P\subset \mathbb C^ m$ containing the projection of $\Omega $ to the $z'$ variables. Set

\[ \begin{gathered} \delta _ j=\varepsilon _ j=j^{-1},\qquad \eta _ j=j^{-2}, \qquad v_ j=\varepsilon _ j^{-1/m}H_{\delta _ j},\\ A_ j=\sup _ Pv_ j+2\varepsilon _ j\log (Rj). \end{gathered} \]

Apply lemma 1.6.17 on $\mathbb C\times \mathbb C^ m$ to these data, and let $u_ j=h_ j-\eta _ j$, where $h_ j$ is the resulting function. The convolution satisfies $0\leq \chi _{\eta _ j}(t)-\max (t,0)\leq \eta _ j$, and $q+\max (T,0)=\max (q,v_ j-A_ j)$. Both branches of this maximum are negative on $\Omega $, so

\[ \varepsilon _ j\log (|\zeta |^2/R^2)-\eta _ j \leq u_ j\leq 0. \]

Each $u_ j$ is smooth and bounded on $\Omega $, since the second branch $v_ j-A_ j$ is bounded below on the bounded set $P$. The logarithm has finite $L^ p(\Omega )$ norm for every finite $p$, by polar integration on a fixed polydisk containing $\Omega $. Hence $u_ j\to 0$ in all these norms.

Let $B'\Subset B\Subset \Omega $ be concentric balls, and let $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ with $0\leq w\leq 1$. Since $|u_ j|\leq \varepsilon _ j|\log (|\zeta |^2/R^2)|+\eta _ j$, the weighted Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimate applied to $\log (|\zeta |^2/R^2)$, together with the range estimate (1.4) for the constant term, gives

\[ \int _{B'}|u_ j|(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n\leq C_ B(\varepsilon _ j+\eta _ j), \]

where $C_ B$ depends on the fixed balls and the logarithm but not on $j$ or $w$. Restricting the integral to $B'\cap \{ |u_ j|>t\} $, dividing by $t>0$ and taking the supremum over $w$ shows that the capacity of $B'\cap \{ |u_ j|>t\} $ relative to $B$ tends to zero.

In (1.19), the factor $\varepsilon _ j(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ m$ equals $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_{\delta _ j})^ m$. For $z'\in P$ and $|t|\leq \eta _ j$, the radii satisfy

\[ r_ t(z')\leq j^{-1}\exp (\eta _ j/(2\varepsilon _ j)) \leq \mathrm e^{1/2}/j. \]

Extend $f\in C_ c(\Omega )$ by zero to $\mathbb C^ n$. Outside the projection of $\operatorname {supp}f$ to the $z'$ variables, both $f(r_ t\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\vartheta },z')$ and $f(0,z')$ vanish. On this projection, the uniform continuity of $f$ and the bound on the radii make their difference tend to zero uniformly. Since the weights $w_{\eta _ j}(t)\, \mathrm dt$ and $\mathrm d\vartheta /(2\pi )$ and the measures $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_{\delta _ j})^ m$ all have total mass one, it follows that

\[ \int _\Omega f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n -\int _{\mathbb C^ m}f(0,z')(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_{\delta _ j})^ m \longrightarrow 0. \]

The second integral tends to $f(0)$, which proves the asserted vague limit. $\square$

Each approximating measure has a smooth density and charges no pluripolar set, whereas the limit is a point mass. By lemma 1.6.16, the potentials therefore have no common lower bound on some compact set. Thus convergence in capacity is compatible with concentration of the Monge–Ampère measures once common local bounds are lost.

1.6.6 Adding a prescribed local measure

The concentration in proposition 1.6.18 can be produced on top of a given bounded potential $b$ and repeated, so as to add an arbitrary prescribed measure to $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n$. Adding a perturbation $h$ to $b$ creates the mixed terms $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^{n-r}\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ r$. A mixed Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimate controls them, provided that the lower-degree currents of $h$ are kept small.

Lemma 1.6.19. Fix the data $n,U,v,\varepsilon ,R,\eta ,\chi _\eta $ of lemma 1.6.17, and write $h_ A$ for the smooth plurisubharmonic function $h$ of that lemma, regarded as a function of $A$. Put $D=\{ 0\} \times U$, and write $[D]$ for the current of integration over this hypersurface. Then, for every $1\leq r\leq n$,

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ A)^ r\longrightarrow \varepsilon [D]\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{r-1} \quad \text{as currents on }\mathbb C\times U,\ A\to \infty . \]

The product on the right is integration of the restriction of the smooth form $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^{r-1}$ over $D$.

Proof. Write $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$, $q=\varepsilon \log (|\zeta |^2/R^2)$ and $T_ A=v-A-q$. The rank-one calculation in the proof of lemma 1.6.17 gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ A)^ r=\chi _\eta '(T_ A)^ rS^ r +r\chi _\eta '(T_ A)^{r-1}\chi _\eta ''(T_ A) \, \mathrm dT_ A\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}T_ A\wedge S^{r-1} \]

off $D$; since $h_ A$ is smooth and $D$ has measure zero, this formula determines the current. Fix a smooth compactly supported test form. On the projection of its support to $U$, the function $v$ and its derivatives are bounded. Hence the support of $\chi _\eta '(T_ A)$ lies in $\{ |\zeta |\leq s_ A\} $, where $s_ A=C\exp (-A/(2\varepsilon ))$ and $C$ depends only on this compact set and the fixed data. The coefficients involving $v$ and $\chi _\eta $ are bounded at the fixed parameters, so the first summand has pairing $O(s_ A^2)$. In the second summand, the term with two derivatives of $v$ also has pairing $O(s_ A^2)$, and the two cross terms have pairing $O(s_ A)$: the derivative of $q$ in the $\zeta $ direction has size $O(|\zeta |^{-1})$, and its area integral over the disk $\{ |\zeta |\leq s_ A\} $ is $O(s_ A)$.

It remains to integrate the term containing $\mathrm dq\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}q$. The logarithmic change of the radial variable used for (1.19) leaves the factor $\varepsilon $ and the probability weight

\[ r\chi _\eta '(t)^{r-1}\chi _\eta ''(t)\, \mathrm dt, \qquad -\eta \leq t\leq \eta . \]

Only the component of the test form in the $z'$ directions enters this term, and on the shrinking circles its coefficients tend uniformly to their values at $\zeta =0$. Integration in the angle therefore gives, in the limit, exactly the pairing with $\varepsilon [D]\wedge S^{r-1}$. This proves the convergence in every degree. $\square$

Lemma 1.6.20. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a domain, let $K$ and $L$ be compact subsets of $\Omega $ with $K$ contained in the interior $L^\circ $ of $L$, and put $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$. Let $b$ be a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $\Omega $, and let $T$ be a smooth positive closed $(r,r)$-form, $1\leq r<n$. Then

\[ \int _ K(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^{n-r}\wedge T \leq C_{K,L,n}(1+\| b\| _{L^\infty (L)})^{n-r} \int _ L T\wedge \beta ^{n-r}. \]

Proof. Put $M=1+\| b\| _{L^\infty (L)}$. Cover $K$ by finitely many balls whose larger concentric balls have closures in $L^\circ $.
