Replacing $b$ by $b+M$, which has the same Hessian and takes values in $[0,2M]$ on $L$, we may assume $0\leq b\leq 2M$ there. For nested cutoff functions $\psi $, integration by parts against closed forms gives

\[ \int \psi \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\wedge W =\int b\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi \wedge W \leq CM \int _{\operatorname {supp}\psi }\beta \wedge W, \]

where $W$ is $T$ multiplied by the remaining positive closed factors. Apply this successively to the $n-r$ Hessians of $b$. For bounded $b$, the integrations by parts are justified by local decreasing regularization and theorem 1.6.1, with one common bound on the range. Summing over the finite cover gives the asserted constant. $\square$

Proposition 1.6.21. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 2$, be a bounded domain, let $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(\Omega )\cap L^\infty (\Omega )$, $a\in \Omega $ and $c>0$. Given finitely many $f_ i\in C_ c(\Omega )$ and numbers $\tau ,t>0$, there is a smooth bounded plurisubharmonic function $h\leq 0$ on $\Omega $ such that

\[ \begin{gathered} \| h\| _{L^ p(\Omega )}\leq C_{\Omega ,p}t\quad (1\leq p<\infty ),\\ \left|\int _\Omega f_ i\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(b+h))^ n-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n\bigr ) -cf_ i(a)\right|<\tau \quad \text{for every }i. \end{gathered} \]

For concentric balls $B'\Subset B\Subset \Omega $ and every $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ with $0\leq w\leq 1$, it also satisfies

\[ \int _{B'}|h|(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n\leq C_{B',B,\Omega }t. \]

These constants are independent of $b,a,c$, the test functions and the tolerances.

Proof. We take for $h$ a function from lemma 1.6.17 whose top-degree measure approximates $c\delta _ a$ and whose lower-degree currents are small; lemma 1.6.20 then controls the mixed terms.

Write $a=(a_1,a')$ and $m=n-1$, and fix $R=2\sup _\Omega |z_1|+2$. For $\delta >0$, put $H_\delta (z')=\log (|z'-a'|^2+\delta ^2)$. By the radial calculation in the proof of proposition 1.6.18, $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\delta )^ m$ is a probability measure tending to $\delta _{a'}$ as $\delta \downarrow 0$. Extend every $f_ i$ by zero outside $\Omega $, and choose $\delta $ such that

\[ \left|c\int _{\mathbb C^ m}f_ i(a_1,z')(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\delta )^ m -cf_ i(a)\right|<\tau /3\quad \text{for all }i. \]

Choose compact sets $K,L\subset \Omega $ with $K\subset L^\circ $ and $K$ containing the supports of all $f_ i$, and a nonnegative smooth cutoff function $\psi $ with compact support in $\Omega $, equal to one near $L$. Put $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$. Expanding the top power of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(b+h)$, summing the bounds of lemma 1.6.20 with their binomial coefficients and the norms $\| f_ i\| _\infty $, we find $\gamma >0$ such that the mixed terms contribute less than $\tau /3$ to every test integral whenever $h$ is smooth and plurisubharmonic and

\[ \int _ L(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ r\wedge \beta ^{n-r}<\gamma , \qquad 1\leq r<n. \]

Fix $0<\eta \leq t/2$. Apply lemma 1.6.17 on $\mathbb C\times \mathbb C^ m$ with transverse variable $\zeta =z_1-a_1$ and $v=c^{1/m}\varepsilon ^{-1/m}H_\delta $. For $r<n$, lemma 1.6.19 gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ A)^ r\longrightarrow \varepsilon ^{(n-r)/(n-1)}c^{(r-1)/(n-1)} [\{ z_1=a_1\} ]\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\delta )^{r-1} \]

as $A\to \infty $. For the fixed $\delta $, the integrals of these limits against $\psi \beta ^{n-r}$ are finite, and their exponents of $\varepsilon $ are positive. Choose $0<\varepsilon \leq t/2$ so that each of them is less than $\gamma /2$. With these parameters fixed, choose $A$ so large that the integrals $\int _ L(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ A)^ r\wedge \beta ^{n-r}$ are less than $\gamma $, that $v-A\leq 0$ on the projection of $\Omega $ to the $z'$ variables, and that

\[ \left|\int _\Omega f_ i(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ A)^ n -c\int _{\mathbb C^ m}f_ i(a_1,z')(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\delta )^ m\right|<\tau /3 \quad \text{for all }i. \]

The last choice follows directly from (1.19), because the circles there shrink uniformly over the projected supports of the tests and $\varepsilon (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ m=c(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H_\delta )^ m$.

Set $h=h_ A-\eta $. As in the proof of proposition 1.6.18, $h$ is smooth, bounded and nonpositive on $\Omega $, with

\[ \varepsilon \log (|z_1-a_1|^2/R^2)-\eta \leq h\leq 0. \]

The logarithm has $L^ p(\Omega )$ norm bounded uniformly for $a_1$ in the projection of $\Omega $ to the first coordinate: after translation, its one-variable integral is bounded by polar integration on one fixed disk. This uniform bound, the weighted Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimate and the range estimate (1.4) give the asserted bound for the integrals over $B'$. Since $\varepsilon +\eta \leq t$, both estimates hold with constants depending only on $\Omega $, $p$, $B'$ and $B$.

Finally, multilinearity of bounded products gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(b+h))^ n=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n+(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ n +\sum _{r=1}^{n-1}\binom nr (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^{n-r}\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h)^ r. \]

The choice of $\delta $, the choice of $A$ for the top-degree term and the estimate of the mixed terms each contribute less than $\tau /3$ to every test integral. This proves the required inequalities, with one choice of $h$ for all finite $p$. $\square$

Theorem 1.6.22. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 2$, be a bounded domain, let $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(\Omega )\cap L^\infty (\Omega )$, and let $\nu $ be a positive Radon measure on $\Omega $. There are bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_ j\leq b$ on $\Omega $ such that

\[ \begin{gathered} \| u_ j-b\| _{L^ p(\Omega )}\longrightarrow 0\quad (1\leq p<\infty ),\\ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n\longrightarrow (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n+\nu \quad \text{vaguely}. \end{gathered} \]

The same sequence converges to $b$ in local capacity. If $b$ is smooth, every $u_ j$ can be chosen smooth. The measure $\nu $ may have infinite total mass.

Proof. Choose relatively compact open sets exhausting $\Omega $, each with closure contained in the next one. On each of these sets, take a countable family of smooth compactly supported functions that is uniformly dense in the space of continuous functions vanishing at its boundary, and add a nonnegative smooth compactly supported cutoff function equal to one on its closure. Enumerate the union of these families as $(f_ i)$. If positive measures converge against every $f_ i$ to a fixed positive Radon measure, they converge vaguely: the cutoff functions give common local mass bounds, and uniform approximation then handles every continuous compactly supported test function.

For each $j$, approximate $\nu $ against $f_1,\ldots ,f_ j$ by a finite positive atomic measure

\[ \nu _ j=\sum _{s=1}^{N_ j}c_{j,s}\delta _{a_{j,s}},\qquad \left|\int _\Omega f_ i(\nu _ j-\nu )\right|<j^{-1}\quad (i\leq j). \]

Such an approximation uses only the finite mass of $\nu $ on a compact neighborhood of the supports of these tests: a finite partition on which all the tests have small oscillation provides the atoms and their positive weights. Atoms of weight zero are omitted. If $N_ j=0$, set $u_ j=b$. Otherwise start with $b_{j,0}=b$ and apply proposition 1.6.21 successively to $b_{j,s-1}$, $a_{j,s}$ and $c_{j,s}$, with the tests $f_1,\ldots ,f_ j$ and $\tau =t=(jN_ j)^{-1}$. This gives perturbations $h_{j,s}$, and we define

\[ b_{j,s}=b_{j,s-1}+h_{j,s},\qquad u_ j=b_{j,N_ j}. \]

Every intermediate potential is bounded and plurisubharmonic, so the proposition applies at each step; the choice at the next step may depend on the bound of the current potential, and each perturbation serves all finite $p$ at once. Summing the norm estimates yields $\| u_ j-b\| _{L^ p(\Omega )}\leq C_{\Omega ,p}/j$, and since every $h_{j,s}\leq 0$, we have $u_ j\leq b$. Summing the measure errors gives

\[ \left|\int _\Omega f_ i\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n-\nu \bigr )\right| <2/j\quad (i\leq j). \]

By the choice of the family $(f_ i)$, this proves the asserted vague limit, also when $\nu (\Omega )=\infty $.

For fixed concentric balls $B'\Subset B\Subset \Omega $, the perturbations are nonpositive, so $|u_ j-b|=\sum _ s|h_{j,s}|$. The last estimate of proposition 1.6.21 bounds the integral of this function against $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ over $B'$ by $C_{B',B,\Omega }/j$, for every $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ with $0\leq w\leq 1$. Restricting to the set where $|u_ j-b|$ exceeds a fixed threshold, dividing by the threshold and taking the supremum over $w$ proves convergence in local capacity. If $b$ is smooth, the finite sums defining $u_ j$ are smooth as well. $\square$

The construction takes place in a bounded Euclidean domain, without prescribed boundary values and without a common bound on the approximating potentials. These freedoms allow concentration even under convergence in local capacity; a fixed global Hermitian background imposes further constraints.

1.6.7 Full cluster sets at the zero potential

Theorem 1.6.22 prescribes one limit of the Monge–Ampère measures, but a single sequence of potentials can have many subsequential limits. In fact, every vaguely closed set of measures is the set of all subsequential limits for a suitable sequence of potentials tending to zero. For a domain $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, write $\mathcal M_+(\Omega )$ for the set of positive Radon measures on $\Omega $, including zero and measures of infinite total mass, with the vague topology defined by integration against $C_ c(\Omega )$.

Lemma 1.6.23. There are real functions $f_ i\in C_ c^\infty (\Omega )$ such that

\[ d_ v(\alpha ,\beta )=\sum _{i\geq 1}2^{-i} \frac{\left|\int _\Omega f_ i(\alpha -\beta )\right|}{1+\left|\int _\Omega f_ i(\alpha -\beta )\right|} \]

is a metric inducing the vague topology on $\mathcal M_+(\Omega )$. This metric space is separable.

Proof. Choose relatively compact open sets $U_\ell $ exhausting $\Omega $, with $\overline U_\ell \subset U_{\ell +1}$. For each $\ell $, choose a countable uniformly dense subset of $C_0(U_{\ell +1};\mathbb R)$ consisting of smooth compactly supported functions, extended by zero to $\Omega $; such families exist because $C_0(U_{\ell +1};\mathbb R)$ is separable and its elements can be approximated uniformly by smooth functions. Add a nonnegative smooth compactly supported cutoff function equal to one near $\overline U_{\ell +1}$, and enumerate the union of all these functions as $(f_ i)$.

Call the maps $\alpha \mapsto \int _\Omega f_ i\, \mathrm d\alpha $ the coordinates. If positive measures converge to a fixed measure in every coordinate, the cutoff coordinates give common mass bounds on each $\overline U_{\ell +1}$, and uniform approximation then gives convergence against every $g\in C_ c(\Omega )$. The same argument, applied near a fixed measure, shows that $\alpha \mapsto \int _\Omega g\, \mathrm d\alpha $ is continuous for the topology generated by the coordinates: bound the relevant cutoff coordinate on a neighborhood, choose one smooth test that is uniformly close to $g$, and shrink the neighborhood using the coordinate of that test. Thus the coordinates generate exactly the vague topology, and they separate measures.

The function $s\mapsto s/(1+s)$ is increasing and subadditive on $[0,\infty )$. Applying it to the triangle inequalities for the coordinates gives the triangle inequality for $d_ v$, and $d_ v$ is positive on distinct measures because the coordinates separate them. Each coordinate is continuous for this metric, and the tails of the series are uniformly small, so the metric induces exactly the topology generated by the coordinates.

Finally, the finite atomic measures with nonnegative rational weights at points of a fixed countable dense subset of $\Omega $ form a countable dense family. To approximate a measure in finitely many coordinates, partition a compact neighborhood of the supports of the corresponding tests into sets on which all these tests have small oscillation, place an atom in each set of nonzero mass, and then perturb its location and weight. Only the finite mass of that compact neighborhood is used, and the remaining tail of the metric is arbitrarily small. $\square$

The metric controls limits along corresponding subsequences: if $d_ v(\alpha _ j,\beta _ j)\to 0$ and one of the sequences converges vaguely to a fixed measure along a subsequence, then, by the triangle inequality, so does the other. Without common local mass bounds, however, $d_ v(\alpha _ j,\beta _ j)\to 0$ need not imply that $\int g(\alpha _ j-\beta _ j)\to 0$ for every $g\in C_ c(\Omega )$. For example, fix $a\in \Omega $, a unit vector $e$, and numbers $r_ j\downarrow 0$ with $a+r_ je\in \Omega $, and put

\[ \alpha _ j=r_ j^{-1/2}\delta _{a+r_ je},\qquad \beta _ j=r_ j^{-1/2}\delta _ a. \]

For every smooth compactly supported test, the difference of the two integrals is $O(r_ j^{1/2})$, so $d_ v(\alpha _ j,\beta _ j)\to 0$ because the tails of the series are small. On the other hand, if $\chi \in C_ c^\infty (\Omega )$ equals one near $a$, the continuous test function $g(z)=\chi (z)|z-a|^{1/2}$ gives $\int g(\alpha _ j-\beta _ j)=1$ for all large $j$.

Theorem 1.6.24. Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 2$, be a bounded domain. A set $F\subset \mathcal M_+(\Omega )$ is vaguely closed if and only if there are smooth bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_ j\leq 0$ on $\Omega $, tending to zero in $L^ p(\Omega )$ for every $1\leq p<\infty $ and in local capacity, whose full vague cluster set is $F$:

\[ F=\left\{ \mu \in \mathcal M_+(\Omega ):\ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{j_ k})^ n\longrightarrow \mu \text{ vaguely for some }j_1<j_2<\cdots \right\} . \]

The empty set is allowed.

Proof. In a metric space, the full cluster set of a sequence is the intersection of the closures of its tails: a point of this intersection can be approximated within $1/k$ at an index larger than the preceding one, successively for $k=1,2,\ldots $. The full cluster set is therefore closed, and lemma 1.6.23 proves necessity.

For sufficiency, suppose first that $F$ is nonempty and closed. A subset of a separable metric space is separable, so we may choose a dense sequence $\mu _1,\mu _2,\ldots $ in $F$, repetitions allowed. Concatenating the finite lists $(\mu _1)$, $(\mu _1,\mu _2)$, $(\mu _1,\mu _2,\mu _3)$, and so on, we obtain a sequence $(\nu _ j)$ in which every $\mu _ k$ appears arbitrarily late. Hence every point of $F$ is the limit of a subsequence, and since $F$ is closed, no other subsequential limit occurs. If $F$ is empty, take $\nu _ j=j\delta _ a$ for a fixed $a\in \Omega $. A nonnegative compactly supported test function equal to one at $a$ has integral $j$ against $\nu _ j$, so this sequence has no vaguely convergent subsequence.

In either case, let $(f_ i)$ and $d_ v$ be as in lemma 1.6.23, and perform the finite atomic construction from the proof of theorem 1.6.22 separately for each $j$, with background zero, target $\nu _ j$ and tests $f_1,\ldots ,f_ j$. Thus we approximate $\nu _ j$ in these coordinates by a finite atomic measure with error less than $1/j$. If this measure has $N_ j>0$ atoms, apply proposition 1.6.21 successively with $t=\tau =(jN_ j)^{-1}$; if it has none, take $u_ j=0$. The resulting smooth bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_ j\leq 0$ satisfy

\[ \| u_ j\| _{L^ p(\Omega )}\leq C_{\Omega ,p}/j, \qquad \left|\int _\Omega f_ i\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n-\nu _ j\bigr )\right| <2/j\quad (i\leq j). \]

On every fixed pair of concentric balls $B'\Subset B\Subset \Omega $, the bounds of that proposition for test measures $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$, with $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ and $0\leq w\leq 1$, also sum to $C_{B',B,\Omega }/j$. Dividing by a positive threshold and taking the supremum over $w$ gives convergence in local capacity. These estimates require neither convergence of the targets $\nu _ j$ nor a common local mass bound for them.

Splitting the series defining the metric after its first $j$ terms gives

\[ d_ v\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n,\nu _ j\bigr )\leq 2/j+2^{-j}\longrightarrow 0. \]

Consequently, the two sequences of measures have the same limits along corresponding subsequences, and hence the same full cluster set $F$. $\square$

For subharmonic functions on a planar domain, by contrast, $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ convergence to zero forces $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\to 0$ in the sense of distributions, and positivity, tested against compactly supported cutoff functions, then gives vague convergence to zero. The freedom in the theorem therefore begins in dimension two.

1.6.8 Cluster sets at a bounded background

Let $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a domain, $n\geq 1$, and let $b$ be a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $\Omega $. If potentials approach $b$ from below in local capacity, every limit of their Monge–Ampère measures must dominate $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n$. Truncation at a fixed distance below $b$ provides a common bound, and plurifine locality keeps the measure outside the small set where the truncation changes the potential.

Lemma 1.6.25. Let $b,u_ j$ be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $ with $u_ j\leq b$ and $u_ j\to b$ in local capacity. Then, for every nonnegative $f\in C_ c(\Omega )$,

\[ \liminf _{j\to \infty }\int _\Omega f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n \geq \int _\Omega f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n. \]

In particular, every vague subsequential limit of $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n$ dominates $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n$.

Proof. First suppose that $f$ is supported in $B'$, where $B'\Subset B\Subset \Omega $ are concentric balls. Put $v_ j=\max (u_ j,b-1)$ and $E_ j=\{ u_ j\leq b-1\} $. The functions $v_ j$ lie between $b-1$ and $b$ and tend to $b$ in local capacity. Because of their common bounds, the continuity of bounded products recalled in section 1.6 gives $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n\to (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n$ vaguely on $B$. With $R=\sup _ B b-\inf _ B b+1$, the capacity bound for bounded potentials gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n(E_ j\cap \operatorname {supp}f) \leq R^ n\operatorname {cap}(E_ j\cap \operatorname {supp}f,B) \longrightarrow 0, \]

where the limit holds because $E_ j\subset \{ |u_ j-b|>1/2\} $. On the plurifine open Borel set $\{ u_ j>b-1\} $ we have $v_ j=u_ j$, so theorem 1.6.1 shows that the two measures agree there. By positivity,

\[ \int f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n \geq \int f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n -\| f\| _\infty (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n(E_ j\cap \operatorname {supp}f). \]

Taking the lower limit proves the assertion for such $f$. For a general $f$, use a finite nonnegative partition of unity subordinate to a cover of its support by such balls. Applying the inequality along a vaguely convergent subsequence proves the final assertion. $\square$

Theorem 1.6.26. Assume that $\Omega $ is bounded, $n\geq 2$, and $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(\Omega )\cap L^\infty (\Omega )$. For a set $F\subset \mathcal M_+(\Omega )$, the following are equivalent:

  1. $F$ is vaguely closed and every $\mu \in F$ satisfies $\mu \geq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n$;

  2. there are bounded plurisubharmonic functions $u_ j\leq b$ on $\Omega $, converging to $b$ in $L^ p(\Omega )$ for every $1\leq p<\infty $ and in local capacity, whose full vague cluster set is $F$.

The empty set is allowed. If $b$ is smooth, every $u_ j$ in the second condition can be chosen smooth.

Proof. If the second condition holds, lemma 1.6.25 gives the measure inequality, and the full cluster set is closed in the metrizable vague topology by the argument at the beginning of the proof of theorem 1.6.24.

Conversely, assume the first condition. If $F$ is nonempty, the concatenation of a dense sequence used in the proof of theorem 1.6.24 gives measures $\nu _ j\in F$ whose full vague cluster set is $F$. If $F$ is empty, take $\nu _ j=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n+j\delta _ a$ for a fixed $a\in \Omega $. A nonnegative compactly supported test function equal to one at $a$ has integral at least $j$ against $\nu _ j$, so this sequence has no vaguely convergent subsequence. In either case, $\nu _ j-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n$ is a positive Radon measure.

Let $(f_ i)$ and $d_ v$ be as in lemma 1.6.23. For each $j$, approximate the excess $\nu _ j-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n$ against $f_1,\ldots ,f_ j$ by a positive finite atomic measure, with each error less than $1/j$. If this measure has $N_ j>0$ atoms, perform the construction from the proof of theorem 1.6.22, starting at $b$ and using $\tau =t=(jN_ j)^{-1}$ for each atom; if it has none, take $u_ j=b$. Summing the estimates of proposition 1.6.21 gives

\[ \| u_ j-b\| _{L^ p(\Omega )}\leq C_{\Omega ,p}/j, \qquad \left|\int _\Omega f_ i\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n-\nu _ j\bigr )\right| <2/j\quad (i\leq j). \]

For every fixed pair of concentric balls $B'\Subset B\Subset \Omega $, the same construction bounds the integral of $|u_ j-b|$ over $B'$ against $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)^ n$ by $C_{B',B,\Omega }/j$, for every $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ with $0\leq w\leq 1$. Dividing by a fixed positive threshold and taking the supremum over $w$ gives convergence in local capacity. Each $u_ j$ is bounded and lies below $b$, and it is smooth when $b$ is smooth. One sequence serves all finite $p$, and no common local mass bound on the $\nu _ j$ is needed.

The error in the first $j$ coordinates and the tail of the series yield

\[ d_ v\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j)^ n,\nu _ j\bigr )\leq 2/j+2^{-j}\longrightarrow 0. \]

By the remark following lemma 1.6.23, the two sequences of measures have the same limits along corresponding subsequences. Hence their full vague cluster sets coincide. $\square$

For example, on the unit ball the function $b=|z|^2-1$ has nonzero Monge–Ampère measure. For every point $a$ of the ball, the theorem allows the cluster sets $\{ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n\} $, $\{ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b)^ n+\delta _ a\} $ and the empty set, but it excludes $\{ 0\} $; this exclusion relies on the convergence in local capacity. In dimension one, $L^1$ convergence alone forces $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\to \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b$ vaguely, so the full cluster set is always $\{ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b\} $.

1.7 Products with smooth backgrounds

The Bedford–Taylor construction also defines products of currents of the form $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ with $u$ locally bounded: one expands them into Hessians of plurisubharmonic functions and smooth forms. Since the result does not depend on the local correction, these products are defined globally. Their positivity is not visible term by term; it follows from the lower curvature bound by freezing the coefficients of the background. For a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ and a function $v$, we write $\theta _ v=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$, and similarly $\omega _ v=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ for other backgrounds. Fix $1\leq k\leq n$.

Let $u$ be locally bounded and $\theta $-plurisubharmonic. On a relatively compact coordinate ball with coordinates $z$, choose $A>0$ such that $p=u+q$ is plurisubharmonic, where $q=A|z|^2$. Set $\beta =\theta -\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q$ and define

1.21
\begin{equation} (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ k :=\sum _{j=0}^ k\binom {k}{j}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ j\wedge \beta ^{k-j}. \end{equation}

Each summand is a product from theorem 1.6.1 multiplied by a smooth form. The individual terms of this expansion need not be positive.

Proposition 1.7.1. The construction (1.21) and its mixed version are independent of the local correction $q$ and of the decomposition of each current into a smooth form and the Hessian of a potential. They define global products of currents with locally bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic potentials. These products are symmetric, multilinear and plurifinely local, and they do not charge pluripolar sets. If every factor is positive, their mixed product is positive.

Proof. On the intersection of two coordinate balls, the two corrected potentials differ by a smooth function. The rule $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(p+f)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$, for bounded $p$ and smooth $f$ in bounded local products, follows by smooth approximation; together with the binomial formula, it shows that the two expansions agree. The same argument handles a smooth change in the decomposition of a current. Moreover, any two decompositions of the same $(1,1)$-current differ by a smooth function: the difference of the potentials has a smooth Hessian, and taking its local Euclidean trace and applying elliptic regularity shows that it is smooth. Symmetry, multilinearity and locality then follow term by term. Bounded Bedford–Taylor products wedged with smooth forms have locally finite variation and do not charge pluripolar sets, so these properties pass to the finite expansion as well.

It remains to prove positivity, which does not follow from the signs of the summands. Write the factors as $T_ i=\theta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i$, $1\leq i\leq k$, where each $\theta _ i$ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form and each $u_ i$ is locally bounded and quasi-plurisubharmonic, and assume $T_ i\geq 0$. Fix $\varepsilon >0$, a point of the coordinate ball and a positive constant form $\kappa $. On a sufficiently small neighborhood of the point, each $\theta _ i$ is close to a constant, hence closed, form $\theta _ i'$:

\[ -\varepsilon \kappa \leq \theta _ i-\theta _ i' \leq \varepsilon \kappa . \]

Then $S_ i=\theta _ i'+\varepsilon \kappa +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i\geq T_ i$ is a positive closed current there, with a bounded local plurisubharmonic potential, and

\[ T_ i+2\varepsilon \kappa =S_ i+(\theta _ i-\theta _ i'+\varepsilon \kappa ), \]

where the last term is a smooth semipositive form. Expanding the product of the currents $T_ i+2\varepsilon \kappa $ therefore gives a sum of positive currents, so this product is positive near the chosen point. Such neighborhoods, although they depend on $\varepsilon $, cover the coordinate ball, so the product of the $T_ i+2\varepsilon \kappa $ is positive on the whole ball. By multilinearity, it converges weakly to the product of the $T_ i$ as $\varepsilon \to 0$, which proves positivity. $\square$

The local expansion also gives continuity along decreasing sequences of uniformly locally bounded potentials: apply theorem 1.6.1 to the corrected potentials and sum the finitely many terms. Since $X$ is compact, every bounded top-degree product has finite total mass. For bounded $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ we write $\mathrm{MA}_\theta (u)=(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$.

Decreasing maxima compare the products of two ordered potentials on the set where they are equal. The comparison also holds in the presence of fixed positive factors, which will be useful in integration by parts.

Lemma 1.7.2. Let $a\leq b$ be locally bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on an $n$-dimensional complex manifold, and let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form such that $\theta _ a,\theta _ b\geq 0$. For $1\leq k\leq n$, let $W$ be a product of $n-k$ positive currents with smooth backgrounds and locally bounded potentials, with $W=1$ when $k=n$. Then

\[ \mathbf1_{\{ a=b\} }\theta _ a^ k\wedge W \leq \mathbf1_{\{ a=b\} }\theta _ b^ k\wedge W. \]

Proof. The functions $m_\delta =\max (a+\delta ,b)$ satisfy $b\leq m_\delta \leq b+\delta $ and decrease to $b$ as $\delta \downarrow 0$. By lemma 1.3.1 and proposition 1.7.1, the products $\theta _{m_\delta }^ k\wedge W$ are positive, and by bounded decreasing continuity they converge weakly to $\theta _ b^ k\wedge W$. On the plurifine open set $\{ a+\delta >b\} $, which contains $\{ a=b\} $, we have $m_\delta =a+\delta $, so plurifine locality identifies the restriction of $\theta _{m_\delta }^ k\wedge W$ to this set with that of $\theta _ a^ k\wedge W$. Hence, for every compact set $K\subset \{ a=b\} $ in a coordinate chart, the upper semicontinuity of the mass of compact sets under weak convergence gives

\[ \int _ K\theta _ a^ k\wedge W =\lim _{\delta \downarrow 0}\int _ K\theta _{m_\delta }^ k\wedge W \leq \int _ K\theta _ b^ k\wedge W. \]

Since the measures are locally finite, inner regularity extends this inequality to all Borel subsets of the contact set, and a countable cover by charts proves the assertion. $\square$

The products commute with pullback by holomorphic submersions. Here the pullback of a current is defined in local product coordinates by pulling back its distributional coefficients.

Lemma 1.7.3. Let $\pi :Z\to Y$ be a holomorphic submersion of complex manifolds. For $1\leq r\leq \dim Z$, let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ r$ be locally bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on $Y$, and let $\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _ r$ be smooth real $(1,1)$-forms on $Y$. Then

\[ \bigwedge _{i=1}^ r\bigl (\pi ^*\theta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ i\circ \pi )\bigr ) =\pi ^*\! \left(\bigwedge _{i=1}^ r(\theta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i)\right). \]

For $r>\dim Y$, the product on the left vanishes, and the right-hand side is interpreted as zero.

Proof. Work in product coordinates in which $\pi $ is a coordinate projection. On a smaller chart of $Y$, choose smooth quadratic functions $q_ i$ such that $p_ i=u_ i+q_ i$ is plurisubharmonic, and approximate each $p_ i$ by a decreasing sequence of smooth plurisubharmonic functions. Their pullbacks have the same properties on the product chart, and for smooth forms pullback commutes with wedge products. Applying bounded decreasing continuity to the local expansions (1.21) on both charts gives the asserted identity, because pullback of currents by the projection is weakly continuous: pairing the pullback with a compactly supported test form amounts to pairing the original current with the integral of the test form over the fiber variables. If $r>\dim Y$, every smooth approximating product vanishes for degree reasons, and so does its limit. $\square$

For $C^{1,1}$ potentials, these products of currents agree with the products of their measurable Hessian coefficients.

Proposition 1.7.4. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ k$ be locally $C^{1,1}$ quasi-plurisubharmonic functions, and let $\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _ k$ be smooth real $(1,1)$-forms. Then the bounded product

\[ (\theta _1+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_1)\wedge \cdots \wedge (\theta _ k+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ k) \]

is the form with locally bounded measurable coefficients obtained by taking the wedge product of the factors pointwise almost everywhere.

Proof. On a smaller coordinate ball, add a common smooth quadratic function $q$ such that each $u_ i+q$ is plurisubharmonic, and convolve with a smooth radial probability kernel. The Hessians of the convolutions are uniformly locally bounded and converge to the weak Hessians in every $L^ s$ space with $s<\infty $, so their finite products converge in $L^1$ on smaller balls. Since the potentials converge uniformly, the continuity of bounded Bedford–Taylor products identifies this limit with the pluripotential product. Expanding with respect to the smooth correction, as in (1.21), proves the assertion for the original factors. $\square$

When the Hessian coefficients are bounded, a positive lower bound for the top-degree product gives a lower bound for the current itself.

Proposition 1.7.5. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, let $h$ be a Hermitian form on $X$, and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cap C^{1,1}(X)$. If

\[ (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n\geq a h^ n \]

for a constant $a>0$, then $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \delta h$ as a current for some $\delta >0$.

Proof. Put $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. Its measurable coefficients are bounded on the compact manifold $X$, so there is $K>0$ such that, almost everywhere, the eigenvalues of $T$ relative to $h$ lie in $[0,K]$. By proposition 1.7.4, their product is the density of $T^ n$ with respect to $h^ n$, and hence it is at least $a$. Each eigenvalue is therefore at least $a/K^{n-1}$ almost everywhere. This inequality of matrices almost everywhere gives the asserted inequality of currents with $\delta =a/K^{n-1}$. $\square$

The mixed Monge–Ampère inequality of theorem 1.6.4 also holds after a smooth background is added. In the following three lemmas, $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ is an open set and $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$.

Lemma 1.7.6. Let $T_ i=\vartheta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ i\geq 0$, $i=1,2$, where the $\vartheta _ i$ are smooth real $(1,1)$-forms on $U$ and the $u_ i$ are locally bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on $U$. If

\[ T_ i^ n\geq F_ i\beta ^ n, \qquad F_ i\geq 0\text{ measurable}, \]

then, for every integer $0\leq k\leq n$,

1.22
\begin{equation} T_1^ k\wedge T_2^{n-k} \geq F_1^{k/n}F_2^{(n-k)/n}\beta ^ n. \end{equation}

Consequently, for $a,b\geq 0$,

1.23
\begin{equation} (aT_1+bT_2)^ n \geq \bigl (aF_1^{1/n}+bF_2^{1/n}\bigr )^ n\beta ^ n. \end{equation}

Proof. Fix $W\Subset U$. Since the forms $\vartheta _ i$ are smooth, there is a constant $C$ such that, for every $x\in W$ and every small ball $B_ r(x)$, the constant forms

\[ A_ i=\vartheta _ i(x)+Cr\beta \]

satisfy $0\leq S_ i\leq 2Cr\beta $ on $B_ r(x)$, where $S_ i=A_ i-\vartheta _ i$. If $q_ i$ is a quadratic potential for $A_ i$, then $u_ i+q_ i$ is plurisubharmonic on $B_ r(x)$ and

\[ \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ i+q_ i)\bigr )^ n=(T_ i+S_ i)^ n\geq F_ i\beta ^ n. \]

Apply theorem 1.6.4 to these two closed Hessians. After expansion, every term other than $T_1^ k\wedge T_2^{n-k}$ contains some $S_ i$ and is therefore bounded by $C'r\mu $, where

\[ \mu =\sum _{\ell +m\leq n-1}T_1^\ell \wedge T_2^ m\wedge \beta ^{n-\ell -m} \]

is a locally finite measure.

A finite cover and a partition of unity give, on $W$,

\[ T_1^ k\wedge T_2^{n-k} \geq F_1^{k/n}F_2^{(n-k)/n}\beta ^ n-C'r\mu . \]

Letting $r\downarrow 0$ proves (1.22), and the binomial expansion then gives (1.23). $\square$

Lemma 1.7.7. Let $\vartheta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $U$. Suppose that $u$ is continuous, $T=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$, and $T^ n\geq g^ n\beta ^ n$ with $g\geq 0$ continuous. For a nonnegative convolution kernel $\chi _\delta $ of mass one,

1.24
\begin{equation} (T*\chi _\delta )^ n\geq (g*\chi _\delta )^ n\beta ^ n \end{equation}

on every smaller domain on which the convolution is defined.

Proof. Approximate the convolution by finite convex combinations of translates, and apply (1.23), extended by induction to finitely many terms. Along these approximations, the background forms converge smoothly and the potentials converge uniformly on compact sets. To pass to the limit, add a common quadratic function and write the difference of two $n$-th powers as a telescoping sum. After pairing with a test form, integration by parts bounds each term by the uniform distance between the potentials times the mass of a mixed current, and successive integration against nested cutoff functions bounds these masses uniformly. Thus the products of the bounded potentials converge, and (1.24) follows. $\square$

For a positive $(1,1)$-current $T$ on $U$, write $T_{\mathrm{ac}}$ for the measurable $(1,1)$-form whose coefficients are the absolutely continuous parts of the coefficients of $T$, and define $\det _\beta T_{\mathrm{ac}}$ by $(T_{\mathrm{ac}})^ n=(\det _\beta T_{\mathrm{ac}})\beta ^ n$. When the top-degree product has a continuous density, the convolution estimate identifies this determinant.

Lemma 1.7.8. Let $u$ be continuous and $T=\vartheta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$, with $\vartheta $ a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $U$. If $T^ n=F\beta ^ n$ for a continuous function $F\geq 0$, then

\[ \det _\beta T_{\mathrm{ac}}=F\qquad \text{almost everywhere}. \]

Proof. Take a nonnegative smooth radial mollifier $\chi _\delta $ supported in the ball of radius $\delta $. Then $T*\chi _\delta \to T_{\mathrm{ac}}$ almost everywhere. Indeed, the convolutions of the absolutely continuous parts converge at Lebesgue points of their densities, while the convolution of the singular part $\lambda _ s$ of a coefficient measure is bounded by $C\delta ^{-2n}|\lambda _ s|(B_\delta )$, which tends to zero almost everywhere by the differentiation theorem for measures. Applying lemma 1.7.7 with $g=F^{1/n}$ and letting $\delta \downarrow 0$ gives $\det _\beta T_{\mathrm{ac}}\geq F$.

For the reverse inequality, write the convolved current as the Hessian of a decreasing regularization of a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function plus a convergent smooth correction. The continuity of bounded products gives $(T*\chi _\delta )^ n\rightharpoonup T^ n$. Fatou's lemma, applied against a nonnegative compactly supported test function, then gives $\det _\beta T_{\mathrm{ac}}\, \beta ^ n\leq T^ n=F\beta ^ n$. $\square$

Lemma 1.7.9. Let $h\leq a$ be locally bounded $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions, and let $\chi $ be a smooth function on a neighborhood of $(-\infty ,0]$ with $0\leq \chi '\leq 1$ and $\chi ''\geq 0$. Then $w=a+\chi (h-a)$ is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic and

\[ \theta _ w=(1-\chi '(h-a))\theta _ a+\chi '(h-a)\theta _ h +\chi ''(h-a)\, \mathrm{d}(h-a)\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}(h-a). \]

In particular, $\theta _ w^ n\geq \chi '(h-a)^ n\theta _ h^ n$. The positivity of $\theta _ w$ and this inequality also hold for convex functions $\chi $ of class $C^{1,1}$ on compact intervals with $0\leq \chi '\leq 1$.

Proof. The function $G(x,y)=x+\chi (y-x)$, for $y\leq x$, is convex and nondecreasing in each variable: its first derivatives are $1-\chi '$ and $\chi '$, and its Hessian is

\[ \chi ''\begin{pmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{pmatrix}\geq 0. \]

For smooth potentials, the chain rule gives the identity. For bounded potentials, add a common quadratic correction and convolve. Convolution preserves the order of the potentials, and since $G(x+r,y+r)=r+G(x,y)$, adding the same correction to both arguments adds it to $G$. The bounded chain rule (1.10) and its products with the gradient term give the identity in the limit. Freezing the smooth background as in the proof of proposition 1.7.1 preserves the exact lower bound. Every term on the right is positive, and the positivity of bounded mixed products gives the last assertion.

For a convex weight of class $C^{1,1}$, extend it convexly across zero with derivative in $[0,1]$, and smooth it in one variable. The smooth convex weights and their first derivatives converge uniformly on compact intervals, and the derivatives stay in $[0,1]$. On each smaller coordinate ball, the corresponding potentials converge uniformly to $w$. The continuity of bounded products gives convergence of their top powers, while the weights on the right-hand side converge uniformly against the fixed measure $\theta _ h^ n$. Passing to the limit proves the positivity and the product inequality with the original smooth background. $\square$

1.8 Hermitian capacity

After a fixed rescaling, a local capacity test can be glued to a constant function and so becomes a global potential on $X$. This compares the local Bedford–Taylor capacity with a capacity defined by global potentials, and it transfers to $X$ both the volume–capacity estimate and an estimate for the sublevel sets of normalized potentials.

For a Borel set $E\subset X$, the Hermitian capacity of $E$ is

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_\omega (E) =\sup _{v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega ),\ 0\leq v\leq 1} \int _ E(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n, \]

where the products are those of proposition 1.7.1. Their local expansion and (1.4) show that $\operatorname {Cap}_\omega (X)<+\infty $. On relatively compact coordinate balls, a fixed smooth correction bounds the measures of global tests by a constant times the corresponding local capacity. Conversely, the gluing construction in the proof of lemma 1.8.1 below bounds local capacity by global capacity. Consequently, the quasi-continuity of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions and the characterization of sets of capacity zero recalled in section 1.6 hold for $\operatorname {Cap}_\omega $. A sequence $u_ j$ converges to $u$ in capacity if

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_\omega (\{ |u_ j-u|>\delta \} )\longrightarrow 0 \quad \text{for every }\delta >0, \]

where pole sets may be omitted. To compare with another Hermitian form $\omega '$, choose $C\geq 1$ with $\omega '\leq C\omega $. Every test $0\leq v\leq 1$ for $\operatorname {Cap}_{\omega '}$ gives a test $v/C$ for $\operatorname {Cap}_\omega $, and the positivity of mixed products gives

\[ (\omega '+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n\leq C^ n(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v/C))^ n. \]

Thus $\operatorname {Cap}_{\omega '}\leq C^ n\operatorname {Cap}_\omega $. Interchanging the two forms shows that the capacities are equivalent up to fixed multiplicative constants, so convergence in capacity does not depend on the choice of $\omega $. Finally, the continuity statements for bounded products in section 1.6 pass to the products with smooth backgrounds, by their local expansion and a finite cover.

1.8.1 Capacity estimates

In this subsection we abbreviate $\operatorname {Cap}=\operatorname {Cap}_\omega $, and, as before, $\omega _ v=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$.

Lemma 1.8.1. There are constants $a,C>0$ such that

1.25
\begin{equation} \operatorname {Vol}(E)\leq C\exp \bigl (-a\operatorname {Cap}(E)^{-1/n}\bigr ) \end{equation}

for every Borel set $E\subset X$. Moreover, for every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\sup _ Xu=0$ and every $t>1$,

1.26
\begin{equation} \operatorname {Cap}(\{ u<-t\} )\leq C/t. \end{equation}

Proof. Cover $X$ by finitely many coordinate balls $B'_ j$, with larger concentric balls $B_ j$, $B'_ j\Subset B_ j$. A local capacity test $0\leq v\leq 1$ on $B_ j$ can be turned into a global test as follows. Choose a smooth function $\chi _ j$ with $-2\leq \chi _ j\leq 0$, equal to zero near $\overline{B'_ j}$ and to $-2$ near $\partial B_ j$. Choose $\delta >0$ so small that

\[ \omega +\delta \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi _ j\geq 0,\qquad 3\delta /2\leq 1. \]

The function

\[ V=\max \{ \delta (v+\chi _ j),-\delta /2\} +\delta /2 \quad \hbox{on }B_ j, \qquad V=0\quad \hbox{outside }B_ j, \]

is $\omega $-plurisubharmonic on $X$, because near $\partial B_ j$ the constant branch is the larger one, and it takes values between zero and one. On $B'_ j$ we have $\omega _ V\geq \delta \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$. By the positivity of bounded products,

\[ \operatorname {cap}(E\cap B'_ j,B_ j) \leq \delta ^{-n}\operatorname {Cap}(E). \]

The local volume–capacity estimate, summed over the finite cover, proves (1.25).

For the second estimate, choose on each $B_ j$ a smooth negative quadratic function $r_ j$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r_ j\geq \omega $. For every global test $0\leq v\leq 1$, the functions $u+r_ j\leq 0$ and $v+r_ j$ are plurisubharmonic on $B_ j$. Thus

\[ \begin{split} \int _{\{ u<-t\} }\omega _ v^ n & \leq t^{-1}\int _ X(-u)\omega _ v^ n\\ & \leq t^{-1}\sum _ j\int _{B'_ j}(-u-r_ j) \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v+r_ j)\bigr )^ n\\ & \leq \frac Ct\left(1+\int _ X|u|\, \mathrm{d}V\right). \end{split} \]

Here the second inequality uses $\omega _ v\leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v+r_ j)$, and the third is the weighted Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality, applied to local test functions with a common bound. The $L^1$ bound for normalized potentials in theorem 1.4.2 completes the proof after taking the supremum over $v$. $\square$

Corollary 1.8.2. Fix $p>1$. There is a constant $C_ p$ such that every nonnegative $F\in L^ p(X,\mathrm{d}V)$ satisfies

\[ \int _ E F\, \mathrm{d}V\leq C_ p\| F\| _ p\operatorname {Cap}(E)^2 \]

for every Borel set $E\subset X$.

Proof. By Hölder's inequality and (1.25), the integral is at most

\[ C\| F\| _ p\exp (-a\operatorname {Cap}(E)^{-1/n}/p'), \qquad p'=p/(p-1). \]

The function $x\mapsto x^{-2}\exp (-ax^{-1/n}/p')$ is bounded on $0<x\leq \operatorname {Cap}(X)<\infty $, and sets of capacity zero have zero volume. $\square$

The same proof bounds the integral of an $L^ p$ density by any fixed positive power of the capacity. The exponent two will be convenient for the iteration over sublevel sets in lemma 10.3.2.

1.8.2 Integration against a Hölder reference

A Hölder modulus for a bounded potential gives a modulus for integration against its Monge–Ampère measure. The proof uses a smooth approximation of the reference, with integration by parts controlling the change in its measure.

Lemma 1.8.3. Let $B'\Subset B$ be concentric balls in $\mathbb C^ n$, and let $0<\alpha \leq 1$. Suppose $p\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)\cap C^{0,\alpha }(B)$ has $C^{0,\alpha }$ norm at most $N$. For every $M\geq 1$ there is a constant $C_ M$ such that bounded $a,b\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ with $|a|,|b|\leq M$ satisfy

\[ \int _{B'}|a-b|(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ n \leq C_ M\| a-b\| _{L^1(B)}^{\alpha /(\alpha +2n)}. \]

The constants are uniform for references with the stated bound $N$.

Proof. Choose intermediate concentric balls and a smooth cutoff $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ supported in the innermost of them and equal to one on $B'$. All integrations below take place away from $\partial B$. Let $p_\varepsilon $ be a nonnegative radial convolution of $p$ on a larger intermediate ball. It is plurisubharmonic, and, for small $\varepsilon >0$,

\[ \| p-p_\varepsilon \| _\infty \leq C\varepsilon ^\alpha , \qquad 0\leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_\varepsilon \leq C\varepsilon ^{-2}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2. \]

Both references have a common bounded range. Set $f=|a-b|=2\max (a,b)-a-b$, and put

\[ T=\sum _{j=0}^{n-1}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ j\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_\varepsilon )^{n-1-j}. \]

This is a closed positive current. Factorization and integration by parts give

\[ \int \chi f\bigl ((\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ n-(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_\varepsilon )^ n\bigr ) =\int (p-p_\varepsilon )\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\chi f)\wedge T. \]

We check that the last signed measure has variation at most $C(1+M)^2$, uniformly in $\varepsilon $. The term $\chi \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f\wedge T$ is a linear combination of the positive measures formed with $\max (a,b),a,b$, whose masses are at most $CM$ by local Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimates. The term $f\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge T$ has variation at most $CM$.

For the two terms involving $\mathrm df$, use a cutoff $\eta $ equal to one near $\operatorname {supp}\chi $. If $q$ is any of $a,b,\max (a,b)$, translate it so that $0\leq q\leq 2M$. The bounded chain rule and closedness of $T$ give

\[ \int \eta \, \mathrm dq\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}q\wedge T =\frac12\int q^2\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\eta \wedge T -\int \eta q\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\wedge T\leq CM^2. \]

The last term is nonnegative before subtraction, and the first is bounded using the local mass of $T$. Since $f=2\max (a,b)-a-b$, its gradient energy is also bounded by $CM^2$. Cauchy–Schwarz against the positive current $T$ now bounds the variation of $\mathrm d\chi \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}f\wedge T$ and $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\chi \wedge \mathrm df\wedge T$ by $CM$. These are all the terms of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\chi f)\wedge T$.

The bounded formulas follow first for local smooth regularizations. To pass to bounded potentials, regularize $a,b,p$ decreasingly on the intermediate balls, write $f$ as the displayed linear combination, and apply lemma 1.6.2 to the expanded identities. The energy bounds are uniform; thus the integration by parts and its estimate survive this limit.

Writing $L=\| a-b\| _{L^1(B)}$, we have proved

1.30
\begin{equation} \int \chi f(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ n \leq C(1+M)^2\varepsilon ^\alpha +C\varepsilon ^{-2n}L. \end{equation}

For small positive $L$, take $\varepsilon =L^{1/(\alpha +2n)}$. A fixed admissible radius and the bounded local mass handle the remaining $L$. For $L=0$, let $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ in the same inequality. $\square$

Corollary 1.8.4. Let $(X,\omega )$ be compact Hermitian, and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap C^{0,\alpha }(X)$, $0<\alpha \leq 1$. If uniformly bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $a,b$ have a fixed common smooth lower curvature bound, then

\[ \int _ X|a-b|\, \omega _ u^ n \leq C\| a-b\| _{L^1(\mathrm dV)}^{\alpha /(\alpha +2n)}. \]

The constant depends on the bounds for $a,b$, their lower curvature bound and the $C^{0,\alpha }$ norm of $u$.

Proof. On each of finitely many coordinate balls, choose a fixed smooth strictly plurisubharmonic quadratic $Q$ such that $a+Q,b+Q,u+Q$ are plurisubharmonic and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}Q\geq \omega $. There $0\leq \omega _ u\leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+Q)$, so positivity of bounded products gives $\omega _ u^ n\leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+Q))^ n$. Apply lemma 1.8.3 on smaller balls covering $X$. The quadratic cancels in the difference, and the local volumes are equivalent to $\mathrm dV$. Summing proves the assertion. $\square$

The estimate before optimization also controls the tails of unbounded potentials. We need the following local form with a compact ambient space, so that the exponential bound for smooth volume is uniform.

Lemma 1.8.5. Let $(Y,\Omega )$ be compact Hermitian, let $B'\Subset B\subset Y$ be coordinate balls, and let $p\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)\cap C^{0,\alpha }(B)$ have norm at most $N$, with $0<\alpha \leq 1$. There are $c,C>0$, uniform for these references, such that

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ n\bigl (B'\cap \{ v<-t\} \bigr )\leq C\mathrm e^{-ct} \quad (t\geq 0) \]

for every $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\Omega )$ with $\sup _ Yv=0$.

Proof. Choose a smooth quadratic $Q$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}Q\geq \Omega $ on $B$ and a fixed constant $D$ such that $w=v+Q-D\leq 0$. For integers $m\geq 1$, set

\[ a_ m=\max (w,-m),\qquad b_ m=\max (w,-m-1),\qquad f_ m=a_ m-b_ m. \]

These are bounded plurisubharmonic truncations; $0\leq f_ m\leq 1$, it vanishes on $\{ w\geq -m\} $, and it equals one on $\{ w<-m-1\} $. By lemma 1.5.1 on $(Y,\Omega )$ and the bounded correction $Q-D$,

\[ \| f_ m\| _{L^1(B)}\leq C\mathrm e^{-am} \]

for a fixed $a>0$. Use the proof of lemma 1.8.3, with $M=m+1$, keeping its estimate (1.30) before optimization. Taking $\varepsilon =\mathrm e^{-am/(\alpha +2n)}$ gives

\[ \int \chi f_ m(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ n \leq C(1+m)^2\exp \left(-\frac{a\alpha m}{\alpha +2n}\right) \leq C\mathrm e^{-cm} \]

for some $c>0$. Large $m$ ensure that the convolution radius is admissible; the common local mass handles the other integers. The bounded difference between $w$ and $v$, and then choosing an integer just below the given height, prove the estimate for every real $t\geq 0$. $\square$

1.8.3 Capacity bounds for Hölder references

To pass from the exponential tail estimate to a capacity estimate, we use a closed background on an auxiliary projective space. The local capacity comparison then returns the result to the original Hermitian manifold.

For the Fubini–Study form $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ on $\mathbb P^ n$, normalized by $\int \omega _{\mathrm{FS}}^ n=1$, define for a compact set $K$

\[ T(K)=\inf _{v\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^ n,\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}),\ \sup v=0} \exp (\sup _ Kv). \]

We recall the classical global extremal-function theorem in this closed setting [GZ17]. If $K$ is non-pluripolar, the upper semicontinuous regularization of

\[ V_ K=\sup \{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(\mathbb P^ n,\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}):v|_ K\leq 0\} \]

is bounded, nonnegative and $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$-plurisubharmonic. Its Monge–Ampère probability is supported on $K$. If $K$ is pluripolar, $\sup V_ K=+\infty $ and $T(K)=0$.

Lemma 1.8.6. For every compact $K\subset \mathbb P^ n$,

\[ T(K)\leq \mathrm e\exp \bigl (-\operatorname {Cap}_{\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}}(K)^{-1/n}\bigr ). \]

The right-hand side is zero for capacity zero.

Proof. The pluripolar case follows from the recalled theorem and the characterization of capacity-zero sets. Otherwise let $M=\sup V_ K^*<\infty $. Subtracting $\sup _ Kv$ from a normalized competitor, and conversely normalizing competitors for $V_ K$, shows directly that $T(K)=\mathrm e^{-M}$.

If $M\geq 1$, the function $q=V_ K^*/M$ satisfies $0\leq q\leq 1$ and is an admissible capacity test. Since the background is closed,

\[ 1=\int _ K\omega _{V_ K^*}^ n \leq M^ n\int _ K\omega _ q^ n \leq M^ n\operatorname {Cap}_{\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}}(K). \]

Here $\omega _{V_ K^*}\leq M\omega _ q$ follows from $M\geq 1$. Thus $M\geq \operatorname {Cap}_{\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}}(K)^{-1/n}$. If $M<1$, $V_ K^*$ itself is a capacity test, so the same supported probability gives $\operatorname {Cap}_{\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}}(K)=1$. Since $T(K)\leq 1$, the stated factor $\mathrm e$ covers this case. $\square$

Proposition 1.8.7. Let $(X,\omega )$ be compact Hermitian. Fix $0<\alpha \leq 1$ and $N>0$. There are $a,C>0$ such that every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap C^{0,\alpha }(X)$ with $\| u\| _{C^{0,\alpha }}\leq N$ satisfies

\[ \omega _ u^ n(E)\leq C\exp \bigl (-a\operatorname {Cap}_\omega (E)^{-1/n}\bigr ) \quad (E\subset X\text{ Borel}). \]

Consequently, for each $s>0$ there is a uniform $C_ s$ with $\omega _ u^ n(E)\leq C_ s\operatorname {Cap}_\omega (E)^ s$.

Proof. Fix nested coordinate balls $B'\Subset B$ and a smooth quadratic $Q$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}Q\geq \omega $ on $B$. The function $p=u+Q$ is plurisubharmonic with a common Hölder bound, and $\omega _ u^ n\leq (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ n$ there. Regard $B$ as a ball in the affine chart $\mathbb C^ n\subset \mathbb P^ n$ and restrict this local measure to $B'$, extending it by zero. By lemma 1.8.5 on $(\mathbb P^ n,\omega _{\mathrm{FS}})$, it has a uniform exponential tail on every normalized global Fubini–Study potential. Its total mass is uniformly bounded by local Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimates.

For a non-pluripolar compact $K\subset B'$, choose a normalized potential $v$ with $\sup _ Kv\leq \log T(K)+1$. For small $T(K)$ the tail estimate gives

\[ (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p)^ n(K)\leq C T(K)^ c \leq C\exp \bigl (-c\operatorname {Cap}_{\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}}(K)^{-1/n}\bigr ). \]

The common mass bound handles the remaining $T(K)$, and pluripolar sets have zero local product mass. A bounded local potential for $\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}$ turns every global test $0\leq v\leq 1$ into a plurisubharmonic function of a fixed bounded range on $B$. Hence

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_{\omega _{\mathrm{FS}}}(K) \leq C_1\operatorname {cap}(K,B) \leq C_2\operatorname {Cap}_\omega (K), \]

where the last inequality is the gluing construction of lemma 1.8.1 on the original manifold. These inequalities have the required direction after taking negative inverse powers. Inner regularity of the finite local product extends the bound from compact to Borel subsets of $B'$. Summing over a fixed finite cover proves the first assertion. The second follows by bounding $x^{-s}\exp (-ax^{-1/n})$ on $0<x\leq \operatorname {Cap}_\omega (X)$. $\square$

1.8.4 Convergence of potentials and measures

For quasi-continuous obstacles, a decreasing lower semicontinuous approximation keeps the original bounds and confines the error to sets of vanishing capacity.

Lemma 1.8.8. Every bounded quasi-continuous function $f$ on $X$ admits bounded lower semicontinuous functions $f_ j$ with common bounds such that $f_ j\downarrow f$ quasi-everywhere and $f_ j\to f$ in capacity.

Proof. Choose open sets $O_ j$ of capacity at most $2^{-j}$ such that the restriction of $f$ to $X\setminus O_ j$ is continuous, and set $G_ j=\bigcup _{k\geq j}O_ k$. Then the sets $G_ j$ decrease, their capacities tend to zero, and $f$ is continuous on the closed complement of each $G_ j$. Choose $M\geq \sup _ Xf$ and define

\[ f_ j=f\ \hbox{on }X\setminus G_ j,\qquad f_ j=M\ \hbox{on }G_ j. \]

At a boundary point of $G_ j$, the values of $f_ j$ on the closed complement tend to the value of $f$ there, while the values inside $G_ j$ are at least that value. Thus $f_ j$ is lower semicontinuous. The functions $f_ j$ decrease to $f$ outside $\bigcap _ jG_ j$, a set of capacity zero. Since $f_ j-f$ is supported in $G_ j$, they also converge to $f$ in capacity. $\square$

Uniform bounds on potentials give a common capacity bound for their mixed measures.

Lemma 1.8.9. Let $A\geq 0$ and let $0\leq k\leq n$ be an integer. There is a constant $C_ A$ such that all the positive mixed measures

\[ \omega _{v_1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{v_ k}\wedge \omega ^{n-k}, \qquad v_ i\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega ), \quad \| v_ i\| _\infty \leq A, \]

are bounded above on Borel sets by $C_ A\operatorname {Cap}_\omega $. In particular, their total masses are uniformly bounded. If uniformly bounded functions tend to zero in capacity, then their integrals against these measures tend to zero uniformly in the $v_ i$.

Proof. Translate the $v_ i$ so that $0\leq v_ i\leq 2A$, and put $q=(v_1+\cdots +v_ k)/B$, where $B\geq \max \{ 2k,2kA,1\} $. Then $0\leq q\leq 1$ and

\[ \omega _ q=B^{-1}\sum _{i=1}^ k\omega _{v_ i}+(1-k/B)\omega , \]

where all coefficients are positive. Expanding $\omega _ q^ n$ shows that the given mixed product is bounded by a constant multiple of $\omega _ q^ n$, and the definition of capacity proves the first claim. The case $k=0$ follows by testing with $v=0$. For a bounded function $z$, split its integral into the parts over $\{ |z|>t\} $ and over its complement; first use convergence in capacity for fixed $t>0$, and then let $t\downarrow 0$. $\square$

The same control applies to the total variation of a product formed with a different smooth background.

Lemma 1.8.10. Let $\Omega $ be a Hermitian form and $\theta $ a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$. Suppose that $\Omega $-plurisubharmonic functions $v_ j$ have common finite upper and lower bounds and decrease to $v$. Then the signed measures

\[ \nu _ j=(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n \]

converge to $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)^ n$ against every fixed bounded quasi-continuous function. Their total variations are bounded, uniformly in $j$, by a constant multiple of the local capacity on the balls of a fixed finite cover.

Proof. On a relatively compact coordinate ball, choose a fixed quadratic function $q$ such that the functions $v_ j+q$ are plurisubharmonic; they still have common bounds. Expand

\[ (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j)^ n =\sum _{\ell =0}^ n\binom n\ell \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ j+q)\bigr )^\ell \wedge (\theta -\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q)^{n-\ell }. \]

The absolute value of the $\ell $-th summand is bounded by a fixed constant times

\[ \bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ j+q)\bigr )^\ell \wedge \kappa _0^{n-\ell }, \]

where $\kappa _0$ is a fixed positive Euclidean form. This positive mixed measure is bounded, uniformly in $j$, by a constant times the local capacity: add a fixed strictly plurisubharmonic quadratic function to $v_ j+q$, expand the top power of its Hessian, and rescale its uniformly bounded range into $[0,1]$ to apply the definition of capacity. This also covers $\ell =0$. Summing over a finite cover gives the asserted control of the total variations. The ordinary decreasing continuity of Bedford–Taylor products gives weak convergence term by term. For a bounded quasi-continuous coefficient, remove an open set of small capacity and extend the restriction of the coefficient to the compact complement to a continuous function on $X$. The variation bound controls the resulting error uniformly for the sequence and its limit, and letting the capacity of the removed set tend to zero proves the assertion. $\square$

A finite non-pluripolar measure is uniformly absolutely continuous with respect to capacity, although it need not be bounded by a power of the capacity.

Lemma 1.8.11. Let $\mu $ be a finite non-pluripolar Borel measure on $X$. Then

\[ \lim _{\delta \downarrow 0}\sup \{ \mu (E):E\text{ Borel}, \operatorname {Cap}_\omega (E)\leq \delta \} =0. \]

Consequently, convergence in capacity implies convergence in $\mu $-measure.

Proof. If the first assertion failed, there would be $\epsilon >0$ and Borel sets $E_ j$ of capacity at most $2^{-j}$ with $\mu (E_ j)\geq \epsilon $. By countable subadditivity of capacity,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_\omega \left(\limsup _ j E_ j\right)=0. \]

Hence $\limsup _ jE_ j$ is pluripolar and has $\mu $-measure zero. On the other hand, since $\mu $ is finite, continuity from above applied to the sets $\bigcup _{k\geq j}E_ k$ shows that $\limsup _ jE_ j$ has measure at least $\epsilon $, a contradiction. If $u_ j\to u$ in capacity, applying the first assertion to the sets $\{ |u_ j-u|>\delta \} $ gives the consequence. $\square$

Lemma 1.8.12. Let $u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Suppose either that $u_ j$ decreases to a function $u\not\equiv -\infty $, or that $u_ j$ increases, the sequence is uniformly bounded above, and $u=(\lim _ j u_ j)^*$. Then $u_ j\to u$ in capacity.

Proof. In the decreasing case $u$ is a common lower bound for the sequence and its limit; in the increasing case $u_1$ is one. Choose a Hermitian form $\Omega \geq \theta $ and truncate all functions at $-T$. The truncations are bounded, monotone and $\Omega $-plurisubharmonic, so the convergence in capacity of locally bounded monotone sequences, recalled in section 1.6, applies to them on the balls of a finite cover. By quasi-continuity of the common lower bound, there is an open set of arbitrarily small capacity containing its pole set such that the restriction of the lower bound to the compact complement is continuous. There it is finite and hence bounded below, so the capacity of the sublevel set of the lower bound at height $-T$ tends to zero as $T\to \infty $. Since the functions agree with their truncations outside this sublevel set, letting $T\to \infty $ proves the lemma. $\square$

A fixed bounded reference potential also controls a mixed trace in which one normalized potential varies.

Lemma 1.8.13. Let $\theta \geq 0$, and fix a bounded $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. There is a constant $C_\psi $ such that

\[ \int _ X\theta _ v\wedge \theta _\psi ^{n-1}\leq C_\psi \]

for every bounded $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\sup _ Xv=0$.

Proof. For $n=1$, Stokes' theorem gives $\int _ X\theta _ v=\int _ X\theta $, so assume $n\geq 2$. Choose a fixed Hermitian form $\Omega \geq \theta $. All currents on the left-hand side are positive, and $\Omega _\psi \geq \theta _\psi $. Hence Stokes' theorem for bounded potentials gives

\[ \int _ X\theta _ v\wedge \theta _\psi ^{n-1} \leq \int _ X\theta \wedge \Omega _\psi ^{n-1} +\int _ Xv\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega _\psi ^{n-1}). \]

The first term on the right is bounded independently of $v$. To estimate the second, differentiate:

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\Omega _\psi ^{n-1}) =(n-1)\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Omega \wedge \Omega _\psi ^{n-2} +(n-1)(n-2)\mathrm{d}\Omega \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\Omega \wedge \Omega _\psi ^{n-3}, \]

where the second term is absent for $n=2$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Omega $ and $\mathrm d\Omega \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\Omega $ are smooth forms, the absolute values of these terms are bounded by fixed multiples of the positive measures $\Omega ^2\wedge \Omega _\psi ^{n-2}$ and $\Omega ^3\wedge \Omega _\psi ^{n-3}$. The weighted local Chern–Levine–Nirenberg inequality of section 1.6 bounds the integrals of $|v|$ against these measures. Indeed, on each larger coordinate ball, add fixed smooth corrections to $v$ and $\psi $, shift the corrected $v$ so that it becomes negative, expand in the corrected positive Hessian of $\psi $, and apply that inequality on a smaller ball. The resulting bound is

\[ C(1+\| \psi \| _\infty )^ n(1+\| v\| _{L^1(X)}). \]

By theorem 1.4.2, normalized $\theta $-plurisubharmonic functions have uniformly bounded $L^1$ norms, and summing the local bounds proves the assertion. $\square$

1.8.5 Vanishing area on curves

On a compact curve, the integral of the background is the total mass of every curvature current. When this integral vanishes, positivity forces the current to vanish. Normalized compactness and the local estimate of capacity by total variation make this uniqueness stable as the area tends to zero.

Throughout this subsection, $Y$ is a compact connected Riemann surface and $\eta $ is a smooth positive $(1,1)$-form on $Y$ with $\int _ Y\eta =1$, which we use for $L^1$ norms and for the capacity $\operatorname {Cap}_\eta $ on $Y$.

Lemma 1.8.14. For a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\sigma $ on $Y$, put $A=\int _ Y\sigma $. There is a smooth real function $h$ with $\sigma =A\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$. The class $\mathrm{PSH}(Y,\sigma )$ is nonempty if and only if $A\geq 0$. If $A=0$, it consists of the functions $-h+c$, $c\in \mathbb R$, and its unique member with supremum zero is $u_\sigma =-h+\min _ Yh$.

Proof. The smooth form $\sigma -A\eta $ has integral zero, so the Poisson equation on the compact connected surface $Y$ has a smooth solution $h$, unique up to an additive constant. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\sigma )$, positivity and Stokes' theorem give

\[ 0\leq (\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)(Y)=A. \]

Conversely, $\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(-h)=A\eta $, so $-h\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\sigma )$ when $A\geq 0$. If $A=0$, every positive curvature measure has total mass zero and therefore vanishes. Thus $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+h)=0$ for every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\sigma )$, and elliptic regularity and the uniqueness of harmonic functions on $Y$ show that $u+h$ is constant. Normalizing the supremum gives the stated formula. $\square$

Theorem 1.8.15. Let $\sigma _ j$ be smooth real $(1,1)$-forms on $Y$ that converge uniformly to a smooth form $\sigma _0$, and suppose that

\[ A_ j=\int _ Y\sigma _ j\geq 0,\qquad \int _ Y\sigma _0=0. \]

Let $u_0$ be the normalized $\sigma _0$-plurisubharmonic potential of lemma 1.8.14. Then, for every $t>0$,

\[ \begin{aligned} \sup _{u\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\sigma _ j),\ \sup _ Yu=0}\| u-u_0\| _{L^1(Y)}& \longrightarrow 0,\\ \sup _{u\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\sigma _ j),\ \sup _ Yu=0} \operatorname {Cap}_\eta (\{ |u-u_0|>t\} )& \longrightarrow 0. \end{aligned} \]

Moreover, the curvature measures $\sigma _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ of these potentials tend to zero in total variation, uniformly in $u$, since each has total variation $A_ j$.

Proof. Choose $C>0$ with $\sigma _ j\leq C\eta $ for every $j$. Every sequence of normalized $\sigma _ j$-plurisubharmonic functions then lies in the compact family of normalized $C\eta $-plurisubharmonic functions of theorem 1.4.2. Every $L^1$ limit $u$ of a subsequence is normalized and, by passing to the limit in the sense of distributions, satisfies $\sigma _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$. The preceding lemma gives $u=u_0$. If the first uniform limit failed, a sequence violating it would have a subsequence converging to this same limit, a contradiction.

For a potential $u$ in the $j$-th normalized family, put $T=\sigma _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. Positivity and Stokes' theorem give $\| T\| _{\mathrm{TV}}=A_ j$, and $A_ j\to \int _ Y\sigma _0=0$. Since $\sigma _0+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_0=0$,

\[ \| \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u-u_0)\| _{\mathrm{TV}} \leq A_ j+\| \sigma _ j-\sigma _0\| _{\mathrm{TV}}\longrightarrow 0 \]

uniformly over that family. Apply lemma 1.6.6 on a fixed finite cover of $Y$ by concentric coordinate disks. The first limit and the bound above make each local capacity tend to zero uniformly, and the comparison between local and global capacity at the beginning of this section proves the second limit. The identity $\| T\| _{\mathrm{TV}}=A_ j$ proves the final assertion. $\square$

1.9 Comparing local regularizations

To glue local convolutions, we need them to agree on overlaps up to an error tending uniformly to zero. A bounded plurisubharmonic function need not be continuous, so this comparison cannot come from uniform convergence to the original function. Instead, the logarithmic convexity of its suprema over balls controls both the change of scale and the change of coordinates.

Let $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a domain and $u$ a bounded plurisubharmonic function on $U$. For $z$ in a fixed relatively compact subset of $U$ and small $\delta >0$, put

\[ S_\delta u(z)=\sup _{|\zeta -z|\leq \delta }u(\zeta ),\qquad A_\delta u(z)=\fint _{|\zeta -z|=\delta }u(\zeta )\, \mathrm{d}\sigma (\zeta ), \qquad u_\delta =u*\rho _\delta , \]

where $\mathrm{d}\sigma $ is the Euclidean surface measure and $\rho $ is a smooth nonnegative radial mollifier of integral one, supported in the unit ball, with $\rho _\delta (\zeta )=\delta ^{-2n}\rho (\zeta /\delta )$. All balls below lie in a fixed larger relatively compact subset of $U$, and $B$ denotes a bound for the oscillation of $u$ on that subset.

Lemma 1.9.1. For each fixed $A>1$,

\[ S_{A\delta }u-S_\delta u\longrightarrow 0, \qquad S_\delta u-u_\delta \longrightarrow 0 \]

uniformly on compact subsets as $\delta \downarrow 0$.

Proof. The maximum of a plurisubharmonic function over concentric balls is a convex function of the logarithm of the radius. This follows by restricting to each complex line through the center and comparing, on an annulus, with the harmonic function that is affine in $\log |\zeta |$. Fix a radius $r>A\delta $ for which all the balls of radius $r$ about points of the given compact set lie in the larger subset. Comparing secant slopes of this convex function between $\log \delta $, $\log (A\delta )$ and $\log r$ gives

1.33
\begin{equation} 0\leq S_{A\delta }u-S_\delta u \leq \frac{B\log A}{\log (r/\delta )}, \end{equation}

which proves the first assertion.

To compare the maximum with the spherical mean, we use the Poisson kernel in real dimension $2n$. For a point at distance at most $\delta /2$ from the center, the normalized Poisson kernel of the ball of radius $\delta $ is bounded below by a positive dimensional constant $c_ n$. Since $u$ is bounded above by $S_\delta u$ and by its harmonic extension from the sphere of radius $\delta $, we obtain

\[ S_\delta u-S_{\delta /2}u \geq c_ n(S_\delta u-A_\delta u). \]

These inequalities hold for bounded plurisubharmonic functions by local smoothing followed by a decreasing limit. By (1.33), $S_\delta u-A_\delta u$ therefore tends uniformly to zero.

For $0<t\leq 1$, the spherical mean is increasing in the radius, and

\[ 0\leq A_\delta u-A_{t\delta }u \leq (S_\delta u-S_{t\delta }u) +(S_{t\delta }u-A_{t\delta }u). \]

By logarithmic convexity, the first term is at most $B\log (1/t)/\log (r/(t\delta ))$, and by the preceding estimate the second is at most $C_ nB/\log (r/(t\delta ))$. Write the radial convolution as an average of the spherical means $A_{t\delta }u$ with respect to the probability density $\widehat\rho (t)=|\mathbb S^{2n-1}|t^{2n-1}\rho (t)$ on $[0,1]$, where $\rho (t)$ denotes the value of $\rho $ at radius $t$. Since $\int _0^1(1+\log (1/t))\widehat\rho (t)\, \mathrm{d}t<\infty $, integration gives

\[ 0\leq A_\delta u-u_\delta \leq \frac{C_{n,\rho }B}{\log (r/\delta )}. \]

Combining the two comparisons proves the second assertion. $\square$

Corollary 1.9.2. Let $U,V\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be domains, let $F:U\to V$ be biholomorphic, and let $u$ be bounded and plurisubharmonic on $U$. Then

\[ u_\delta -\bigl ((u\circ F^{-1})*\rho _\delta \bigr )\circ F \]

tends locally uniformly to zero as $\delta \downarrow 0$.

Proof. On a compact subset, $F$ and its inverse have uniformly bounded first derivatives. Thus, for a fixed $A>1$, the inverse image under $F$ of the ball of radius $\delta $ about $F(z)$ lies between the balls about $z$ of radii $\delta /A$ and $A\delta $. Consequently, $S_\delta (u\circ F^{-1})(F(z))$ and $S_\delta u(z)$ both lie between $S_{\delta /A}u(z)$ and $S_{A\delta }u(z)$, and the first assertion of lemma 1.9.1 shows that their difference tends to zero uniformly. The second assertion of the lemma, applied to $u$ and to $u\circ F^{-1}$, compares each of the two convolutions with the corresponding supremum regularization. $\square$

1.10 Gluing with a small curvature error

The overlap estimate allows local regularizations to be glued by a regularized maximum. Small corrections near the boundary of each chart make the corresponding local branch irrelevant there, while convexity of the regularized maximum preserves a common lower curvature bound. The curvature error is measured by the Hermitian form $\omega $.

For an integer $N\geq 1$, choose independent real random variables $Z_1,\ldots ,Z_ N$ with a common smooth even probability density supported in $[-1,1]$, and define

\[ M_\tau (t_1,\ldots ,t_ N) =\mathbb E\max _ i(t_ i+\tau Z_ i)\quad (\tau >0). \]

As a convolution, $M_\tau $ is smooth. It is convex and increasing in every argument, and, with $\mathbf1=(1,\ldots ,1)$, it satisfies

\[ \max _ i t_ i\leq M_\tau (t)\leq \max _ i t_ i+\tau , \qquad M_\tau (t+c\mathbf1)=M_\tau (t)+c \]

for $t\in \mathbb R^ N$ and $c\in \mathbb R$. If $t_ i<\max _{j\ne i}t_ j-2\tau $, then the $i$-th argument can be removed without changing the value. Moreover, $M_\tau (t)$ is nondecreasing in $\tau $, because it is a convex even function of $\tau $, the distribution of each $Z_ i$ being symmetric.

The second identity expresses invariance under translations. If smooth real functions $f_ i$, $1\leq i\leq N$, satisfy $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ i\geq -\gamma $ for a common smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\gamma $, then

1.34
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}M_\tau (f_1,\ldots ,f_ N)\geq -\gamma . \end{equation}

Indeed, in the chain rule the first partial derivatives of $M_\tau $ are nonnegative and, by the translation identity, sum to one, while the real Hessian of the convex function $M_\tau $ contributes a semipositive $(1,1)$-form.

Proposition 1.10.1. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, let $\omega $ be a Hermitian form, and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cap L^\infty (X)$. There are smooth functions $v_ j$ and positive numbers $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$ such that

\[ v_ j\downarrow u,\qquad \theta +\varepsilon _ j\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j\geq 0. \]

Proof. For fixed $\epsilon >0$, we first glue the local convolutions of $u$ by a regularized maximum, obtaining smooth functions that decrease to $u$ and have curvature error $2\epsilon \omega $; a diagonal sequence then proves the proposition.

Step 1: local regularizations. Fix $\epsilon >0$. By continuity of $\theta $, we can choose finitely many nested coordinate neighborhoods $V_ i\Subset W_ i\Subset U_ i$, with the $V_ i$ covering $X$, and smooth quadratic functions $r_ i$ such that

\[ 0\leq \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r_ i-\theta \leq \epsilon \omega \quad \hbox{on }U_ i. \]

To do so, freeze the coefficients of $\theta $ at the center of a chart, add a small positive constant form, and shrink the coordinate neighborhood. Then $p_ i=u+r_ i$ is a bounded plurisubharmonic function on $U_ i$; let $p_{i,\delta }$ be its convolution in the coordinates of $U_ i$.

On an overlap, express both $p_ i$ and $p_ j$ in the coordinates of $U_ j$. Their difference is the smooth function $r_ i-r_ j$, so the difference of their convolutions in these coordinates tends uniformly to $r_ i-r_ j$. By corollary 1.9.2, changing the coordinates in which $p_ i$ is convolved introduces another uniformly vanishing error. Thus, on the intersections of the closures of the $W_ i$,

1.35
\begin{equation} (p_{i,\delta }-r_ i)-(p_{j,\delta }-r_ j)\longrightarrow 0 \quad \hbox{uniformly}. \end{equation}

Step 2: gluing. Choose functions $\eta _ i\in C^\infty (U_ i,[-1,0])$ that vanish on $V_ i$ and equal $-1$ near the boundary of $W_ i$, and a constant $C\geq 1$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\eta _ i\geq -C\omega $. The cover, the functions $r_ i,\eta _ i$ and the constant $C$ may depend on $\epsilon $; they are fixed while $\delta $ tends to zero. Set

\[ f_{i,\delta }=p_{i,\delta }-r_ i+(\epsilon /C)\eta _ i \quad \hbox{on }W_ i. \]

Each of these functions satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_{i,\delta }\geq -\theta -2\epsilon \omega $. Every boundary point of $W_ i$ lies in some $V_ j$, and there $\eta _ j=0$ while $\eta _ i=-1$. By (1.35), for all small $\delta $,

\[ f_{i,\delta }<f_{j,\delta }-\epsilon /(2C) \]

on a neighborhood of that boundary point. Since the cover is finite, this separation holds uniformly.

Let $\tau (\delta )\downarrow 0$ as $\delta \downarrow 0$, with $\tau (\delta )<\epsilon /(8C)$, and define the value at each point as the regularized maximum $M_{\tau (\delta )}$ of the functions $f_{i,\delta }$ for those $i$ with the point in $W_ i$. A branch disappears only near the boundary of its $W_ i$, where it lies below another branch by more than $2\tau $. By the removal property of $M_\tau $, these local formulas therefore define a global smooth function $\psi _{\epsilon ,\delta }$, and (1.34) gives

\[ \theta +2\epsilon \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi _{\epsilon ,\delta }\geq 0. \]

At every point there is a branch with $\eta _ i=0$, and $p_{i,\delta }\geq p_ i$; hence $\psi _{\epsilon ,\delta }\geq u$. For fixed $\epsilon $, the local convolutions decrease with $\delta $, and the regularized maximum decreases when both its arguments and $\tau $ decrease. Its pointwise limit is

\[ \max _{i:x\in W_ i}\bigl (u(x)+(\epsilon /C)\eta _ i(x)\bigr )=u(x). \]

Thus $\psi _{\epsilon ,\delta }\downarrow u$ as $\delta \downarrow 0$, and also in $L^1$.

Step 3: a diagonal sequence. Take $\epsilon _ j\downarrow 0$ and put $v_ j=\psi _{\epsilon _ j,\delta _ j}+2^{-j}$, where $\delta _ j$ is chosen so small that

\[ \| \psi _{\epsilon _ j,\delta _ j}-u\| _{L^1}\leq 2^{-j}, \qquad v_ j<v_{j-1}\quad (j\geq 2). \]

The second requirement can be met because $v_{j-1}\geq u+2^{-j+1}$. Indeed, as $\delta \downarrow 0$, the continuous functions $\psi _{\epsilon _ j,\delta }+2^{-j}-v_{j-1}$ decrease pointwise to a function bounded above by $-2^{-j}$. Their sets of nonnegative values are decreasing compact sets with empty intersection, so one of them is empty. The resulting sequence $(v_ j)$ decreases, converges to $u$ in $L^1$, and stays above $u$. Its pointwise limit is $(\theta +2\epsilon _ k\omega )$-plurisubharmonic for every $k$, hence $\theta $-plurisubharmonic, and it equals $u$ almost everywhere; by lemma 1.3.3, it equals $u$ everywhere. Taking $\varepsilon _ j=2\epsilon _ j$ proves the proposition. $\square$

1.11 Regularization in a fixed Hermitian background

For $\theta =\omega $, rescaling the approximants absorbs the curvature error into the positive background. Subtracting a constant first makes the approximants negative, so that this rescaling preserves their decreasing order.

Theorem 1.11.1. For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap L^\infty (X)$, there are smooth functions $u_ j\downarrow u$ such that

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j>0. \]

The functions $u_ j$ are uniformly bounded.

Proof. Subtracting a constant, we may assume $u\leq -2$. Apply proposition 1.10.1 with $\theta =\omega $, obtaining smooth functions $v_ j\downarrow u$ and positive numbers $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$ with $(1+\varepsilon _ j)\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j\geq 0$. The compact sets $\{ v_ j\geq -1\} $ decrease to the empty set, since $u\leq -2$. After discarding finitely many terms, we may therefore assume $v_ j<0$ everywhere. Put

\[ u_ j=\frac{v_ j}{1+2\varepsilon _ j}. \]

Then

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j \geq \frac{\varepsilon _ j}{1+2\varepsilon _ j}\omega >0. \]

Since $v_ j$ is negative and decreases, while the positive denominator decreases to one, $u_ j$ also decreases, and it converges to $u$. Moreover, $u\leq v_ j\leq u_ j\leq u_1$, which gives uniform boundedness. Adding back the subtracted constant proves the theorem. $\square$
