This regularization provides the approximation used for products and mass in chapter 6. For an arbitrary smooth form $\theta $, proposition 1.10.1 only gives approximants whose curvature error tends to zero.

1.12 Singularity type and multiplier ideals

There are two natural ways to compare the singularities of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on $X$. The first asks whether one function is bounded above by the other up to an additive constant; it leads to the ordinary singularity type. The second records only which holomorphic functions are locally square integrable against the weights $\mathrm{e}^{-ku}$, $k>0$; it leads to multiplier ideals. Domination implies the corresponding inclusion of multiplier ideals. A logarithmic pole on a disk shows that a single weight loses information, which is why the second comparison uses all positive weights.

See Definition 1.5.2.

Thus $u\preceq v$ means that $u$ is at least as singular as $v$. For instance, a quasi-plurisubharmonic function with a logarithmic pole lies below every bounded potential in this order, but not conversely.

Proposition 1.12.2. The relation $\preceq $ is a preorder, and $\sim $ is an equivalence relation. If $u$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic and $f$ is a smooth function on $X$, then $u+f\sim u$. In particular, all smooth functions on $X$ have the same singularity type.

Proof. Reflexivity follows with $C=0$. If $u\leq v+C$ and $v\leq w+D$, then $u\leq w+C+D$, which proves transitivity. As for every preorder, comparison in both directions defines an equivalence relation. Since $X$ is compact, a smooth function $f$ satisfies $|f|\leq A$ for some constant $A$, and then $u-A\leq u+f\leq u+A$. Taking $u=0$ shows that every smooth function has the singularity type of the constants. $\square$

Some later results require the singularities of a potential to be concentrated on a small closed set. A closed subset $A\subseteq X$ is complete pluripolar if every point of $X$ has a neighborhood $U$ with a plurisubharmonic function $p$ on $U$ such that $A\cap U=\{ p=-\infty \} $. A quasi-plurisubharmonic function has small unbounded locus if it is locally bounded outside a closed complete pluripolar set.

See Definition 12.2.5.

Changing the volume form does not change local integrability: on relatively compact coordinate neighborhoods, the ratio of two smooth positive volume forms is bounded above and below by positive constants. In particular, the definition does not involve a choice of Hermitian metric, and it applies verbatim to quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on any complex manifold.

Proposition 1.12.4. If $u\preceq v$, then $\mathcal I(ku)\subseteq \mathcal I(kv)$ for every real $k>0$. If $u\sim v$, then these ideals agree for every $k>0$.

Proof. Let $g$ be a local holomorphic function on $X$. If $u\leq v+C$, then $\mathrm{e}^{-kv}\leq \mathrm{e}^{kC}\mathrm{e}^{-ku}$. Multiplying by $|g|^2$ shows that local integrability of $|g|^2\mathrm{e}^{-ku}$ implies local integrability of $|g|^2\mathrm{e}^{-kv}$. If $u\sim v$, the same argument applies in both directions. $\square$

Example 1.12.5. On a disk in $\mathbb C$ centered at the origin, with coordinate $z$, let $\lambda \geq 0$ and put $u(z)=\lambda \log |z|^2$ for $\lambda >0$, while $u=0$ for $\lambda =0$. Then

\[ \mathcal I(u)_0=(z^{\lfloor \lambda \rfloor }). \]

Indeed, if a nonzero holomorphic germ has order $m$ at the origin, its squared modulus is comparable to $|z|^{2m}$ near the origin. In polar coordinates, its weighted integral over a disk of sufficiently small radius $\epsilon >0$ is finite precisely when

\[ \int _0^\epsilon r^{2m-2\lambda +1}\, \mathrm{d}r<\infty , \qquad \text{equivalently}\qquad m>\lambda -1. \]

The smallest nonnegative integer satisfying this strict inequality is $\lfloor \lambda \rfloor $. When $\lambda $ is an integer, the borderline exponent $-1$ occurs for $m=\lambda -1$ and gives a divergent integral; this is where the strictness of the inequality matters.

The example also shows that a single multiplier ideal does not determine the singularity: different coefficients $\lambda $ can have the same integer part. For a real weight $k>0$, the same calculation applied to $ku$ gives $\mathcal I(ku)_0=(z^{\lfloor k\lambda \rfloor })$. The ideals at all weights $k>0$ recover $\lambda $: if $0\leq \lambda <\mu $, the integer parts of $k\lambda $ and $k\mu $ differ as soon as $k(\mu -\lambda )>1$.

We therefore compare multiplier ideals at all positive weights. For quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $u,v$, we write $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}v$ if $\mathcal I(ku)\subseteq \mathcal I(kv)$ for every real $k>0$, and $u\sim _{\mathcal I}v$ if $\mathcal I(ku)=\mathcal I(kv)$ for every real $k>0$. We call $\preceq _{\mathcal I}$ the ideal order; if $u\sim _{\mathcal I}v$, then $u$ and $v$ have the same multiplier-ideal type, or ideal type. By proposition 1.12.4, $u\preceq v$ implies $u\preceq _{\mathcal I}v$, and $u\sim v$ implies $u\sim _{\mathcal I}v$. These relations make sense on any complex manifold. Their relation to envelopes and to global masses requires further arguments; see chapter 16.

1.13 Analytic types and restriction

The simplest singularities of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions are logarithmic poles along the common zeros of finitely many holomorphic functions. The logarithmic growth of a general singularity at a point is measured by its Lelong number. Let $u$ be a quasi-plurisubharmonic function on a complex manifold and let $x$ be a point. Choose holomorphic coordinates centered at $x$ and a smooth function $q$ such that $p=u+q$ is plurisubharmonic near $x$, and write $B(x,r)$ for the Euclidean coordinate ball of radius $r$. The Lelong number of $u$ at $x$ is

\[ \nu (u,x)=\lim _{r\downarrow 0} \frac{\sup _{B(x,r)}p}{\log r^2}. \]

By the local growth theorem recalled in [SGPT], the limit exists and does not depend on the coordinates or on the correction $q$. With this normalization, $\log |z|^2$ has Lelong number one at the origin. For an irreducible analytic subset $Z$, the generic Lelong number of $u$ along $Z$ is

\[ \nu (u,Z)=\inf _{x\in Z}\nu (u,x). \]

By Siu's theorem, recalled in 1.13.1, $\nu (u,x)=\nu (u,Z)$ for all $x\in Z$ outside a countable union of proper analytic subsets of $Z$.

For analytic singularities we follow the convention of [SGPT]: we say that a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $u$ has analytic singularities if near every point there are holomorphic functions $f_1,\ldots ,f_ N$, not all identically zero, and a rational number $c>0$ such that

\[ u=c\log \bigl (|f_1|^2+\cdots +|f_ N|^2\bigr )+O(1), \qquad c\in \mathbb Q_{>0}. \]

The remainder is only required to be bounded; if it can be chosen smooth, we say that $u$ has analytic singularities with smooth remainder. Given a coherent ideal sheaf $\mathfrak a$ and a common coefficient $c$, we say that $u$ has analytic type $(c,\mathfrak a)$ if this holds with local generators $f_1,\ldots ,f_ N$ of $\mathfrak a$. The choice of generators does not matter: two finite systems of generators of the same ideal change the logarithm only by a bounded amount on smaller relatively compact open sets.

Proposition 1.13.1. Analytic singularities are preserved by addition, finite maxima, and multiplication by positive rational numbers. Let $u$ have analytic type $(c,\mathfrak a)$, and let $Y\subset X$ be a connected complex submanifold on which $\mathfrak a$ does not vanish identically. Then the restriction $u|_ Y$ is a quasi-plurisubharmonic function on $Y$, in particular not identically $-\infty $, and it has analytic type

\[ (c,\mathfrak a\mathcal O_ Y). \]

Here $\mathfrak a\mathcal O_ Y$ is the image ideal, generated by the restrictions to $Y$ of local sections of $\mathfrak a$.

Proof. For multiplication by a positive rational number, multiply the coefficient. Consider two presentations with coefficients $c_1,c_2$ and ideals $\mathfrak a_1,\mathfrak a_2$, and choose a positive rational number $d$ with $c_1=k d$ and $c_2=\ell d$, where $k,\ell $ are positive integers. Raising a sum of squared moduli of generators of $\mathfrak a_1$ to the power $k$ gives, up to bounded factors, the corresponding sum for $\mathfrak a_1^ k$; similarly for $\mathfrak a_2^\ell $. Products of generators of $\mathfrak a_1^ k$ and $\mathfrak a_2^\ell $ therefore present the sum with coefficient $d$, since a product of sums of squares is a sum of squares of pairwise products.

For the maximum, apply the elementary inequalities

\[ \max \{ A,B\} \leq A+B\leq 2\max \{ A,B\} ,\qquad A,B\geq 0, \]

to the two sums of squared moduli of generators of $\mathfrak a_1^ k$ and $\mathfrak a_2^\ell $. Taking logarithms shows that the maximum differs from $d$ times the logarithm of their total by a bounded amount. These are local assertions; on the compact manifold, finitely many charts give the corresponding comparisons of ordinary singularity types.

For the restriction, restrict to $Y$ the two-sided bounds that define the remainder. Since $Y$ is connected and is not contained in the zero set of $\mathfrak a$, the common zero set of the restricted generators is a proper analytic subset of $Y$, so $u|_ Y$ is finite on a dense open subset of $Y$. After a smooth local correction, the restriction theorem for plurisubharmonic functions shows that $u|_ Y$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, and the restricted bounds give the asserted presentation. $\square$

For an arbitrary quasi-plurisubharmonic function, a vanishing generic Lelong number along a submanifold does not prevent the restriction from being identically $-\infty $. For example, on a small polydisk in $\mathbb C^ n$ with coordinates $z=(z_1,\ldots ,z_ n)$ and $|z_1|<\mathrm{e}^{-1}$, the function

\[ u(z)=-\sqrt{-\log |z_1|^2} \]

is plurisubharmonic, because $t\mapsto -\sqrt{-t}$ is increasing and convex for $t<0$. Its Lelong number vanishes at every point of $\{ z_1=0\} $, but its restriction to this hyperplane is identically $-\infty $. For the analytic approximants constructed below, the analytic hypothesis in proposition 1.13.1 excludes this behavior.

1.13.1 Lelong level sets

Siu's analyticity theorem states that, for every quasi-plurisubharmonic function $u$ and every $c>0$, the upper level set $\{ x:\nu (u,x)\geq c\} $ is an analytic subset. After a smooth local correction, this is the theorem for closed positive currents, which we use in the form recalled in [SGPT]; see section 34.1 for the original source.

For a closed positive current, the Lelong monotonicity formula also bounds the trace mass of small balls. The resulting Hausdorff estimate shows that a closed positive $(j,j)$-current puts no mass on sets of zero Hausdorff $2(n-j)$-measure.

Corollary 1.13.2. Let $T$ be a closed positive $(j,j)$-current on a complex manifold of dimension $n$, with $1\leq j<n$. Its coefficient measures vanish on every Borel set of zero Hausdorff $2(n-j)$-measure.

Proof. In a coordinate chart put $\kappa =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ and $\mu =T\wedge \kappa ^{n-j}$. By the Lelong monotonicity formula in bidimension $(n-j,n-j)$, the function $r\mapsto r^{-2(n-j)}\mu (B(x,r))$ is nondecreasing; see [Chapter III, formulas (5.5)–(5.8), pp. 158–159, DemCADG]. Fix a relatively compact inner chart and a radius $r_0>0$ such that all balls of radius $r_0$ centered in it lie in a fixed compact subset of the chart. Their masses are bounded by the mass of that compact set. Hence

\[ \mu (B(x,r))\leq Cr^{2(n-j)},\qquad 0<r\leq r_0, \]

uniformly for centers in the inner chart. A Borel set in the inner chart with zero Hausdorff $2(n-j)$-measure can be covered by balls $B(x_ i,r_ i)$ with $\sum _ ir_ i^{2(n-j)}$ arbitrarily small, so it has zero $\mu $-mass. Since $T$ is positive, the total variation of each of its coefficient measures is bounded by a constant multiple of $\mu $. A countable cover by charts proves the assertion. $\square$

The non-strict inequality in Siu's theorem cannot be replaced by a strict one, even on a compact Kähler manifold.

Example 1.13.3. Let $X=\mathbb P^2$, with homogeneous coordinates $[x:y:z]$, and let $\sigma $ be the Fubini–Study form normalized by $\int _ X\sigma ^2=1$. Put $\| Z\| ^2=|x|^2+|y|^2+|z|^2$, $a_ j=2^{-j}$ and $\lambda _ j=2^{-j-1}$ for $j\geq 1$. The global function

\[ u([Z])=\log \frac{|y|^2}{\| Z\| ^2} +\sum _{j\geq 1}\lambda _ j \log \frac{|x-a_ jz|^2}{(1+a_ j^2)\| Z\| ^2} \]

belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\frac32\sigma )$. For the line $D=\{ y=0\} $ and the points $p_ j=[a_ j:0:1]$, it satisfies

\[ \{ \nu (u,\cdot )\geq 1\} =D, \qquad \{ \nu (u,\cdot )>1\} =\{ p_ j:j\geq 1\} . \]

The second set is not analytic.

Proof. For a linear form $\ell $ with coefficient vector of norm one, the function $v_\ell =\log (|\ell (Z)|^2/\| Z\| ^2)$ is nonpositive and satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_\ell =[\ell =0]-\sigma $. Its $L^1(X,\sigma ^2)$ norm is finite and independent of $\ell $, because the unitary group $U(3)$ acts on homogeneous coordinates, preserves $\sigma $, and acts transitively on such forms. Since $\sum _ j\lambda _ j=1/2$, the partial sums of the series defining $u$ decrease to an $L^1$ function. They have the common curvature lower bound $-\frac32\sigma $, so their limit is quasi-plurisubharmonic and

\[ T:=\tfrac 32\sigma +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=[D]+\sum _{j\geq 1}\lambda _ j[L_ j], \qquad L_ j=\{ x-a_ jz=0\} . \]

The series of positive closed currents converges, since every line has $\sigma $-trace mass one and the coefficients are summable.

We compute the Lelong numbers from the local mass formula. In affine coordinates $w=(w_1,w_2)$ centered at a point $q$, put $\kappa =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|w|^2$. An affine complex line $H$ at distance $d$ from $q$ satisfies

\[ \int _{H\cap B(q,r)}\kappa =(r^2-d^2)_+. \]

Consequently $r^{-2}\int _{H\cap B(q,r)}\kappa $ is at most one and tends to $\mathbf1_ H(q)$ as $r\downarrow 0$; a line that does not meet the chart contributes zero. Tonelli's theorem and dominated convergence for the summable coefficients therefore give

\[ \nu (u,q)=\nu (T,q) =\mathbf1_ D(q)+\sum _{j\geq 1}\lambda _ j\mathbf1_{L_ j}(q). \]

Here we used that a smooth local correction does not change the Lelong number. The computation also covers points at which infinitely many lines accumulate.

The lines $L_ j$ meet $D$ at the distinct points $p_ j$ and meet one another only at $p_\infty =[0:1:0]\notin D$. Thus $\nu (u,p_ j)=1+\lambda _ j$, whereas $\nu (u,q)=1$ for $q\in D\setminus \{ p_ j:j\geq 1\} $. Outside $D$, the Lelong number is at most $\sum _ j\lambda _ j=1/2$. This proves the two level-set identities. Finally, $p_ j\to p_*=[0:0:1]$ and $\nu (u,p_*)=1$, so the strict level set is not closed and hence not analytic. $\square$

1.14 The local Bergman estimates

Demailly's approximation of plurisubharmonic functions by functions with analytic singularities is built from Bergman kernels of weighted spaces of holomorphic functions. In this section we recall the local construction and its estimates. Fix a bounded pseudoconvex domain $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ and a plurisubharmonic function $\psi $ on $\Omega $. Write $\mathrm{d}\lambda $ for Euclidean volume and $B(x,r)$ for the Euclidean ball of radius $r>0$ about $x$. For a positive integer $m$, define the weighted Hilbert space of holomorphic functions

\[ \mathcal H_ m=\left\{ f\in \mathcal O(\Omega ): \| f\| _ m^2:=\int _\Omega |f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-m\psi }\, \mathrm{d}\lambda <\infty \right\} . \]

Choose an orthonormal basis $(g_{m,j})_ j$ of $\mathcal H_ m$ and define $\beta _ m=m^{-1}\log \sum _ j|g_{m,j}|^2$. The sum is the squared norm of point evaluation,

\[ \sum _ j|g_{m,j}(x)|^2 =\sup _{\| f\| _ m\leq 1}|f(x)|^2, \]

and is therefore independent of the basis. By the local finite generation part of Bergman regularization, $\beta _ m$ has analytic type $(1/m,\mathcal I(m\psi ))$ on relatively compact open subsets of $\Omega $. This follows from the generation of the multiplier ideal by weighted $L^2$ holomorphic functions and from local $L^2$ division; see [Proposition 5.7 and Section 14.A, DemAM11]. We use this local statement, together with the point-extension estimate in the proof below, as a standard input.

Proposition 1.14.1. There are constants $C_1,C_2$ independent of $m$ such that, for $B(x,r)\Subset \Omega $,

1.36
\begin{equation} \psi (x)-\frac{C_1}{m} \leq \beta _ m(x) \leq \sup _{B(x,r)}\psi +\frac1m\log \frac{C_2}{r^{2n}}. \end{equation}

Consequently $\beta _ m\to \psi $ pointwise and in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$, and

1.37
\begin{equation} \max \{ \nu (\psi ,x)-n/m,0\} \leq \nu (\beta _ m,x)\leq \nu (\psi ,x). \end{equation}

Proof. For $\| f\| _ m\leq 1$, the submean inequality gives

\[ |f(x)|^2 \leq C_2r^{-2n}\int _{B(x,r)}|f|^2\, \mathrm{d}\lambda \leq C_2r^{-2n}\mathrm{e}^{m\sup _{B(x,r)}\psi }. \]

Taking the supremum over the unit ball of $\mathcal H_ m$ and then the logarithm proves the upper bound.

For the lower bound, suppose that $\psi (x)>-\infty $. The local Ohsawa–Takegoshi point-extension theorem gives a holomorphic $f$ with $f(x)=1$ and

\[ \int _\Omega |f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-m\psi }\, \mathrm{d}\lambda \leq C \mathrm{e}^{-m\psi (x)}, \]

where the constant is independent of the plurisubharmonic weight and of $m$. After normalizing $f$ in $\mathcal H_ m$, we see that the squared norm of point evaluation is at least $C^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{m\psi (x)}$, which proves the lower bound. At a pole of $\psi $ the lower bound holds trivially.

Choosing $r=1/m$ in the upper bound and using upper semicontinuity of $\psi $ proves pointwise convergence. On a fixed relatively compact set, the same estimates bound $\beta _ m$ above by a constant and below by $\psi -C_1/m$. Since $\psi $ is locally integrable, dominated convergence also gives convergence in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

For the Lelong estimate, take suprema over $B(x,r)$ in the two inequalities, bounding the right-hand side of the upper one by a supremum over $B(x,2r)$. Divide by $\log r^2<0$ and let $r\downarrow 0$. After the division, the term $-(2n/m)\log r$ contributes $-n/m$, while the constant terms disappear. This proves the two inequalities in (1.37); since Lelong numbers are nonnegative, the lower bound may be replaced by its maximum with zero. $\square$

In [DemAM11], the local weight is $\mathrm{e}^{-2m\phi }$ and Lelong numbers are normalized by $\log |z|$. With $\psi =2\phi $, these conventions give exactly the formulas above; in particular, the loss is $n/m$ for the weight $\mathrm{e}^{-m\psi }$ and the normalization by $\log |z|^2$.

The same construction and estimates hold for every real $m>0$. The coefficient $1/m$ of the resulting logarithmic presentation is rational exactly when $m$ is rational, and we speak of an analytic type only in that case. The global sequence constructed below is indexed by integers.

For a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $u$, the generators of $\mathcal I(mu)$ also control the multiplier ideals of $u$ at nearby weights; a small loss in the weight compensates for the finite Bergman index.

Lemma 1.14.2. Let $u$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic, let $m>0$, and let $a_ m$ be a function which locally satisfies $a_ m=m^{-1}\log \sum _ k|g_ k|^2+O(1)$, where the $g_ k$ generate $\mathcal I(mu)$. For $t>0$ and $s>1$,

\[ \mathcal I(sta_ m)\subseteq \mathcal I(tu) \qquad \text{whenever}\qquad m\geq \frac{st}{s-1}. \]

Moreover, $u\preceq a_ m$ locally, and hence $\mathcal I(tu)\subseteq \mathcal I(ta_ m)$ for every $t>0$.

Proof. On a sufficiently small coordinate neighborhood, choose generators $g_1,\ldots ,g_ N$ of $\mathcal I(mu)$ and put $B=(\sum _ k|g_ k|^2)\mathrm{e}^{-mu}$. After shrinking the neighborhood, $B$ is integrable and $a_ m=m^{-1}\log \sum _ k|g_ k|^2+O(1)$ with a bounded remainder. If $f\in \mathcal I(sta_ m)$, shrink once more so that $f$ is bounded and $A=|f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-sta_ m}$ is integrable. Then, almost everywhere,

\[ |f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-tu} \leq C|f|^{2-2/s}A^{1/s}B^{t/m}. \]

Hölder's inequality with exponents $s$ and $s/(s-1)$ applies because $st/(m(s-1))\leq 1$, and it shows that the left side is locally integrable, also in the case of equality in the bound for $m$. Finally, the local Bergman lower estimate of proposition 1.14.1 gives $u\preceq a_ m$ locally; bounded smooth corrections affect neither this comparison nor any of the ideals. $\square$

1.15 The global curvature bound

We return to the compact manifold $X$ with its Hermitian form $\omega $. Demailly's global regularization theorem glues the local Bergman constructions while controlling the curvature error. We use it as a classical input in the following form.

Theorem 1.15.1 (Demailly regularization). Let $u$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on $X$ and let $\gamma $ be a continuous real $(1,1)$-form with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \gamma $. There are quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $u_ m$, defined for all sufficiently large positive integers $m$, and numbers $\epsilon _ m\downarrow 0$ such that

  1. $u_ m$ has analytic type $(1/m,\mathcal I(mu))$;

  2. $u_ m\geq u$ and $u_ m\to u$ pointwise and in $L^1(X)$;

  3. $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ m\geq \gamma -\epsilon _ m\omega $;

  4. $\max \{ \nu (u,x)-n/m,0\} \leq \nu (u_ m,x)\leq \nu (u,x)$ for all $x$.

The sequence $(u_ m)$ need not be monotone.

This is the form of [Theorem 14.12, DemAM11] used in this book. The local generators in the construction are the weighted Bergman generators of $\mathcal I(mu)$; smooth local corrections do not change this ideal. On overlaps the local Bergman sums are comparable, so the glued logarithm keeps the analytic type $(1/m,\mathcal I(mu))$. The construction freezes the continuous lower bound $\gamma $ on sufficiently small coordinate balls, and its curvature error combines the modulus of continuity of $\gamma $ with an overlap term that tends to zero as $m$ increases. It is this global curvature estimate, and not merely the local convergence of proposition 1.14.1, that we take from the theorem.

Corollary 1.15.2. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$ and $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. The approximants $u_ m$ of theorem 1.15.1 for $\gamma =-\theta $ satisfy

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ m\geq -\epsilon _ m\omega . \]

In particular, they have analytic type $(1/m,\mathcal I(mu))$.

Proof. Apply theorem 1.15.1 to $u$ with the lower bound $\gamma =-\theta $; the theorem does not require $\gamma $ to be closed. Adding $\theta $ to the curvature inequality gives the assertion. In the normalization of [DemAM11], the theorem is applied to the potential $u/2$, since $\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\pi }\partial \bar\partial (u/2)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. $\square$

Thus the approximants keep the curvature lower bound up to an error that tends to zero. Their multiplier ideals determine the limit of their ordinary envelopes; see theorem 16.5.4.

1.15.1 Regularization of closed currents

We also use the regularization of closed currents in the form recalled in [SGPT]. Let $T=\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ be a closed real $(1,1)$-current, where $\eta $ is a smooth closed form and $v$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, and set $\nu (T,x)=\nu (v,x)$. Suppose that $T\geq \gamma $ for a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\gamma $. Smooth regularization gives smooth representatives $T_ j=\eta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j$ with

\[ T_ j\geq \gamma -C\lambda _ j\omega , \]

where $C>0$ is a fixed constant and the continuous functions $\lambda _ j$ decrease pointwise to $\nu (T,\cdot )$. If all Lelong numbers of $T$ vanish, Dini's theorem shows that $\lambda _ j\to 0$ uniformly, so the loss of positivity becomes uniformly small. Analytic regularization instead gives representatives $S_ j$ with analytic singularities and

\[ S_ j\geq \gamma -\varepsilon _ j\omega ,\qquad \varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0,\qquad \nu (S_ j,x)\leq \nu (T,x). \]

Thus analytic regularization preserves strict positivity up to a small loss and creates no positive Lelong number at a point where the Lelong number of $T$ vanishes.

1.15.2 Smooth remainders after resolution

For arguments on a modification, the bounded remainder in an analytic presentation is sometimes insufficient. The following version of regularization gives a smooth remainder after resolution.

Theorem 1.15.3 (Demailly regularization). Let $\alpha $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\alpha )$. There are numbers $\delta _ j\downarrow 0$ and functions $\widetilde u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\alpha +\delta _ j\omega )$ decreasing to $u$ with the following properties. Each $\widetilde u_ j$ is smooth outside an analytic pole set, and there is a modification $\pi _ j:Y_ j\to X$, obtained by a finite sequence of blowups with smooth centers, such that locally on $Y_ j$

\[ \pi _ j^*\widetilde u_ j=\sum _\ell a_\ell \log |s_\ell |^2+b, \qquad a_\ell >0,\quad b\in C^\infty . \]

Here the $s_\ell $ are local holomorphic defining functions of divisors, the $a_\ell >0$ are real numbers, and $b$ is a smooth real function. The divisors may be taken to have simple normal crossings.

This classical theorem is applied to the closed current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ with the smooth lower bound $-\alpha $; see section 34.1 for its source. The lower bound need not be closed, and $X$ need not be Kähler. What matters here is the smooth remainder $b$ after resolution; an analytic type, whose remainder is merely bounded, would not suffice. The sequence $(\widetilde u_ j)$ need not be the multiplier-ideal sequence of theorem 1.15.1, and we do not claim that the conclusions of the two theorems hold for one and the same sequence. If $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \varepsilon \omega $ for some $\varepsilon >0$, applying the theorem with $\alpha =\theta -\varepsilon \omega $ and discarding the terms with $\delta _ j\geq \varepsilon $ gives approximants with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde u_ j\geq (\varepsilon -\delta _ j)\omega >0$; thus strict positivity is preserved.

1.16 Multiplier ideals and logarithmic poles

For a logarithmic pole along a submanifold, weighted integrability of a holomorphic germ is determined by its vanishing order in the normal directions. For general singularities, the valuative characterization of multiplier ideals and the strong openness theorem, both recalled in [SGPT], make the comparison of multiplier ideals compatible with maxima, sums and increasing limits.

In local coordinates $(z,w)\in \mathbb C^ d\times \mathbb C^ k$, with $d\geq 0$ and $k\geq 1$, let $Y=\{ w=0\} $ and denote its ideal by $\mathcal I_ Y$.

Lemma 1.16.1. If a quasi-plurisubharmonic function has the form $u=c\log |w|^2+O(1)$ with $c>0$ and locally bounded remainder, then for every $t>0$,

\[ \mathcal I(tu)=\mathcal I_ Y^{\max \{ \lfloor tc\rfloor -k+1,0\} }. \]

The zeroth power is the full structure sheaf.

Proof. The bounded remainder changes the weight only by bounded positive factors. Expand a nonzero holomorphic germ $g(z,w)$ in homogeneous polynomials in $w$, and let $a$ be the smallest degree whose term does not vanish identically. On smaller product neighborhoods, $|g(z,w)|\leq C|w|^ a$. Hence $a+k>tc$ suffices for weighted integrability, since the corresponding radial integral in $\rho =|w|$ is

\[ \int _0^\varepsilon \rho ^{2a-2tc+2k-1}\, \mathrm{d}\rho <+\infty . \]

Conversely, let $z$ be a point near the origin at which the term of degree $a$ does not vanish identically in $w$. Integration of $|g(z,w)|^2$ over the spheres $|w|=\rho $ gives a positive leading term of order $\rho ^{2a}$, so the radial integral in $w$ diverges when $a+k\leq tc$, logarithmically in the case of equality. Such points $z$ form a nonempty open set in every sufficiently small neighborhood, and Fubini's theorem shows that $g$ is not weighted-integrable near the origin. For $d=0$ this is the radial calculation at a point. Thus $g$ is weighted-integrable exactly when $a\geq \lfloor tc\rfloor -k+1$, that is, when $g$ lies in the power of $\mathcal I_ Y$ given in the statement. $\square$

Cutoff functions allow us to insert small isolated logarithmic poles into any Hermitian background.

Lemma 1.16.2. Let $\omega $ be a Hermitian form on $X$, and fix distinct points $p_1,\ldots ,p_ N$ of $X$. There are constants $C_ j>0$ such that, whenever $a_ j>0$ and $\sum _ jC_ ja_ j<1$, there is $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, smooth off these points, with

\[ u=a_ j\log |z|^2+O(1)\quad \hbox{near }p_ j,\qquad \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq \left(1-\sum _ jC_ ja_ j\right)\omega . \]

Here $z$ denotes local coordinates centered at $p_ j$. The function can be chosen to vanish outside arbitrarily small disjoint coordinate neighborhoods, with the constants depending on those neighborhoods.

Proof. Choose disjoint coordinate balls centered at the points $p_ j$ and smooth cutoffs $\chi _ j$ supported in them and equal to one near their centers. The function $\chi _ j\log |z|^2$, extended by zero, is plurisubharmonic near its pole and smooth elsewhere, so its complex Hessian is bounded below by $-C_ j\omega $. Set $u=\sum _ ja_ j\chi _ j\log |z|^2$. These lower bounds give the asserted current inequality, and the remaining properties follow from the disjointness of the supports. $\square$

Theorem 1.16.3. Let $u,v$ be plurisubharmonic functions near a point of $\mathbb C^ n$. By a local modification we mean a modification $\pi $, with smooth source, of a neighborhood of this point.

  1. The inclusions $\mathcal I(ku)\subseteq \mathcal I(kv)$ for every real $k>0$ are equivalent to the inequalities $\nu (u\circ \pi ,y)\geq \nu (v\circ \pi ,y)$ at every point $y$ of the source of every local modification $\pi $. Likewise, equality of the multiplier ideals at every positive weight is equivalent to equality of all these Lelong numbers.

  2. If $p_ j$ is an increasing sequence of plurisubharmonic functions converging almost everywhere to a plurisubharmonic function $p$, then, for each point $x$ and each $\lambda >0$,

    \[ \mathcal I(\lambda p_ j)_ x=\mathcal I(\lambda p)_ x \quad \text{for all sufficiently large }j. \]

In the first item, the neighborhoods may be shrunk as necessary; we never need to extend a local modification to a modification of a compact manifold. The second item is the increasing-limit form of the strong openness theorem used in [SGPT]. Since adding a smooth function changes neither multiplier ideals nor Lelong numbers, both items apply to quasi-plurisubharmonic functions after the local correction of proposition 1.1.2.

Lemma 1.16.4. Let $u,u',v,v'$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on $X$. For any quasi-plurisubharmonic $h$, one has

\[ u+h\sim _{\mathcal I}v+h \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad u\sim _{\mathcal I}v. \]

If $u\sim _{\mathcal I}v$ and $u'\sim _{\mathcal I}v'$, then

\[ \max (u,u')\sim _{\mathcal I}\max (v,v'),\qquad u+u'\sim _{\mathcal I}v+v',\qquad a u\sim _{\mathcal I}a v\quad (a>0). \]

Proof. Fix a coordinate neighborhood $U\subset X$ and a modification $\pi :Y\to U$ with smooth source. At every $y\in Y$, the elementary rules for the Lelong numbers of maxima, sums and positive multiples give

\[ \begin{aligned} \nu (\max (u,u')\circ \pi ,y) & =\min \{ \nu (u\circ \pi ,y),\nu (u'\circ \pi ,y)\} ,\\ \nu ((u+u')\circ \pi ,y) & =\nu (u\circ \pi ,y)+\nu (u'\circ \pi ,y),\\ \nu (a u\circ \pi ,y)& =a\nu (u\circ \pi ,y). \end{aligned} \]

These rules are unaffected by smooth local corrections. Since $u\sim _{\mathcal I}v$ and $u'\sim _{\mathcal I}v'$, the first item of theorem 1.16.3 shows that the right-hand sides do not change when $u,u'$ are replaced by $v,v'$. The converse direction of the same item, applied on each coordinate neighborhood, gives the three asserted equivalences.

For the cancellation rule, suppose that $u+h\sim _{\mathcal I}v+h$. By the first item of theorem 1.16.3, the pullbacks $(u+h)\circ \pi $ and $(v+h)\circ \pi $ have the same Lelong numbers. The common summand $h\circ \pi $ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, so its Lelong numbers are finite, and subtracting them shows that $u\circ \pi $ and $v\circ \pi $ have the same Lelong numbers. Hence $u\sim _{\mathcal I}v$ by the same item. The reverse implication is the addition rule proved above. $\square$

A residual with zero Lelong numbers can still be unbounded, but it does not change any multiplier ideal, even after addition to another potential.

Lemma 1.16.5. Let $q$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on a complex manifold and suppose that $\nu (q,x)=0$ at every point. Then $\mathrm e^{-tq}$ is locally integrable for every $t>0$. For every quasi-plurisubharmonic $v$ and every $t>0$,

\[ \mathcal I\bigl (t(v+q)\bigr )=\mathcal I(tv). \]

Proof. Fix a point $x$ and add a smooth correction to make $q$ plurisubharmonic on a bounded pseudoconvex coordinate neighborhood. Form the weighted Bergman space of proposition 1.14.1 at weight $t$. If every member vanished at $x$, the Cauchy estimates, together with the local upper bound for $q$, would give

\[ \sup _{\| g\| _ t\leq 1}|g(z)|\leq C|z-x| \]

near $x$. Its Bergman potential would consequently have Lelong number at least $1/t$, contradicting (1.37). There is therefore a weighted square-integrable holomorphic function nonzero at $x$. On a smaller neighborhood it is bounded away from zero, so its weighted norm proves integrability of $\mathrm e^{-tq}$. Smooth corrections do not change this conclusion. Thus $q\sim _{\mathcal I}0$; the addition rule in lemma 1.16.4 gives $v+q\sim _{\mathcal I}v$, which is precisely the asserted equality at every weight. $\square$

Let $v,w$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on an open set, and let $Y$ be a complex submanifold on which $v|_ Y$ is not identically $-\infty $. The restriction and subadditivity theorems give the two inclusions recalled in [SGPT] and [Section 15, DemAM11]:

1.41
\begin{equation} \mathcal I(v|_ Y)\subseteq \mathcal I(v)\mathcal O_ Y, \qquad \mathcal I(v+w)\subseteq \mathcal I(v)\mathcal I(w). \end{equation}

The first follows from the local Ohsawa–Takegoshi extension theorem. For the second, the weighted Hilbert space of holomorphic functions on a product is the Hilbert tensor product of the two weighted spaces, so the multiplier ideal of $v(x)+w(y)$ on the product is generated by products of generators of $\mathcal I(v)$ and $\mathcal I(w)$; the first inclusion, applied to the diagonal, then gives the second. These local results for plurisubharmonic functions extend to quasi-plurisubharmonic functions, in the weight convention of this book, by smooth corrections.

Multiplier ideals commute with pullback by holomorphic submersions. Here the extension $\mathcal I\mathcal O_ Z$ of an ideal sheaf $\mathcal I$ on $Y$ is the ideal sheaf on $Z$ generated by the pullbacks of its local sections.

Lemma 1.16.6. Let $p:Z\to Y$ be a holomorphic submersion of complex manifolds and let $u\in \mathrm{QPSH}(Y)$. For every $t>0$,

\[ \mathcal I(tu\circ p) =\mathcal I(tu)\mathcal O_ Z. \]

Proof. Work in product coordinates $(z,\zeta )$ with $p(z,\zeta )=z$. Write $\alpha $ for a multi-index in the fiber variables and $\mathrm{d}\lambda _\zeta $ for their Euclidean volume. A germ in the ideal on the right is a finite sum of bounded holomorphic multiples of pullbacks of germs that are square integrable against $\mathrm{e}^{-tu(z)}$. Fubini's theorem shows that it is weighted-integrable on a sufficiently small product neighborhood.

Conversely, let $F(z,\zeta )$ be a weighted-integrable holomorphic germ and expand it in the fiber variables,

\[ F(z,\zeta )=\sum _\alpha f_\alpha (z)\zeta ^\alpha . \]

The coefficient estimate for a holomorphic function on a polydisk gives, on smaller product neighborhoods,

\[ |f_\alpha (z)|^2\leq C_\alpha \int |F(z,\zeta )|^2\, \mathrm{d}\lambda _\zeta . \]

Multiplying by $\mathrm{e}^{-tu(z)}$ and integrating in $z$ shows that each coefficient germ $f_\alpha $ belongs to $\mathcal I(tu)$. Let $J$ be the extension of this ideal to the local ring $R$ of holomorphic germs on the product. Every finite Taylor sum of $F$ belongs to $J$, and the remainder of total fiber degree at least $N$ belongs to $(\zeta )^ N$. Thus the class of $F$ in $R/J$ lies in $(\zeta )^ N(R/J)$ for every $N$. By the Krull intersection theorem for the finitely generated module $R/J$ over the Noetherian local ring $R$, the intersection of these submodules is zero. Hence $F\in J$. Smooth corrections and smooth positive volume densities do not change the integrability conditions. $\square$

Example 1.16.7. Exact multiplier-ideal pullback can fail at a critical fiber. On a polydisk in $\mathbb C^ n$, $n\geq 1$, let $f(z_0,\ldots ,z_{n-1})=z_0^2$ and put $v(t)=\tfrac 12\log |t|^2$. At the origin,

\[ \mathcal I(v)=\mathcal O,\qquad \mathcal I(v\circ f)=(z_0) \ne \mathcal I(v)\mathcal O. \]

Proof. On the curve the coefficient is $1/2$, so the multiplier ideal is the zeroth power of the maximal ideal. Upstairs $v\circ f=\log |z_0|^2$ has coefficient one along the smooth divisor $\{ z_0=0\} $. Apply lemma 1.16.1 in codimension one to get its first ideal power. $\square$

This calculation concerns a fixed weight. For a surjective holomorphic map from a connected compact manifold to a curve, with connected fibers, pullback still preserves and reflects all-weight ideal types by 1.22.6.

1.16.1 Weighted distance from a sum of multiplier ideals

Membership in a multiplier ideal is a local integrability condition. On a pseudoconvex domain one can ask, more quantitatively, how small the weighted norm of a holomorphic function can be made by subtracting global sections of such ideals. A sharp $L^2$ estimate answers this question for one weight. Applied successively, with the remaining weights kept in the auxiliary weight at each step, it gives a product bound for several weights.

Throughout this subsection, $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$ is a pseudoconvex domain, $n\geq 1$, and $\mathrm{d}V$ denotes Euclidean volume. For a plurisubharmonic weight $\rho $ on $\Omega $, put

\[ \| f\| _\rho ^2=\int _\Omega |f|^2\mathrm e^{-\rho }\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

For a plurisubharmonic function $v$ on $\Omega $, we write $\Gamma (\Omega ,\mathcal I(v))$ for the space of holomorphic functions on $\Omega $ whose germs belong to $\mathcal I(v)$ at every point. Membership requires only local weighted integrability, not a finite global norm.

We first prove a division estimate with two summands. Its sharp constant comes from keeping the full sum of the twisting functions in the $\bar\partial $-estimate.

Lemma 1.16.8. Let $\psi ,\phi _1,\phi _2$ be plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $, and suppose that $S=\mathrm e^{\phi _1}+\mathrm e^{\phi _2}<1$. If $f$ is holomorphic and

\[ J=\int _\Omega \frac{|f|^2\mathrm e^{-\psi }}{S^2}\, \mathrm{d}V<+\infty , \]

then there are holomorphic $F_1,F_2$ with $F_1+F_2=f$ and

1.42
\begin{equation} \int _\Omega \bigl (|F_1|^2\mathrm e^{-\phi _1} +|F_2|^2\mathrm e^{-\phi _2}\bigr ) \mathrm e^{-\psi }\, \mathrm{d}V\leq J. \end{equation}

Proof. Step 1: a twisted $\bar\partial $-estimate. Suppose first that $\Omega $ is bounded, that the weights are smooth on a neighborhood of $\overline\Omega $ with $S<1$ there, and that $\Phi =\log S$ is strictly plurisubharmonic. Set $\chi =\phi _1+\phi _2+\psi $ and $A=(\Phi _{j\bar k})$. For a $(0,1)$-form $b$, write $|b|_ A^2$ for its squared norm with respect to the dual of this positive Hermitian matrix. We claim that every $\bar\partial $-closed $(0,1)$-form $b$ for which the right-hand side below is finite admits a solution $u$ of

1.43
\begin{equation} \bar\partial u=b,\qquad \int |u|^2\mathrm e^{\Phi -\chi }\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \int (1-\mathrm e^\Phi )|b|_ A^2 \mathrm e^{-\chi }\, \mathrm{d}V. \end{equation}

To prove this, define for $x<0$

\[ T(x)=-\log (1-\mathrm e^ x),\qquad \eta (x)=\frac{-x}{1-\mathrm e^ x}-1,\qquad \lambda (x)=-\frac{\eta '(x)}{T'(x)}. \]

The inequality $-x>1-\mathrm e^ x$ gives $\eta >0$. Also $\eta '= -[1-(1-x)\mathrm e^ x]/(1-\mathrm e^ x)^2<0$, whereas $T'>0$, so $\lambda >0$. Direct differentiation gives

\[ \eta T'-\eta '=1,\qquad \eta T''-\eta ''-\frac{(\eta ')^2}{\lambda }=0,\qquad \frac{\mathrm e^{-T}}{\eta +\lambda }=\mathrm e^ x. \]

Use the weight $h=\mathrm e^{-\chi -T(\Phi )}$ on the trivial line bundle and evaluate the twisting functions at $\Phi $. The twisted curvature is

\[ \begin{aligned} B_0& =\eta \, \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial (\chi +T(\Phi )) -\mathrm i\partial \bar\partial \eta -\mathrm i\lambda ^{-1}\partial \eta \wedge \bar\partial \eta \\ & =\eta \, \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial \chi +\mathrm i\partial \bar\partial \Phi \geq \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial \Phi . \end{aligned} \]

Let $\Lambda $ denote contraction by the Euclidean Kähler form and $\bar\partial _ h^*$ the weighted adjoint. For a compactly supported $(n,1)$-form $v$, the twisted Bochner identity and the weighted Cauchy–Schwarz inequality give

1.46
\begin{equation} \langle [B_0,\Lambda ]v,v\rangle _ h \leq \| \sqrt{\eta +\lambda }\, \bar\partial _ h^*v\| _ h^2 +\| \sqrt\eta \, \bar\partial v\| _ h^2. \end{equation}

More explicitly, the cross term is bounded by $\lambda |\bar\partial _ h^*v|^2+\lambda ^{-1}|(\bar\partial \eta )^*v|^2$; the latter term is exactly the commutator contribution of $\mathrm i\lambda ^{-1}\partial \eta \wedge \bar\partial \eta $. The terms of type $(n+1,1)$ vanish, and the remaining connection norm has a favorable sign.

The Hilbert space existence argument applied to (1.46) therefore gives

\[ \int \frac{|u|^2h}{\eta +\lambda }\, \mathrm{d}V \leq \int |b|_ A^2h\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

To see the coefficient, project a test form to $\ker \bar\partial $. On this kernel the second term of (1.46) vanishes, and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in the curvature norm bounds the functional $v\mapsto \langle b,v\rangle _ h$ by $(\int |b|_ A^2h)^{1/2}\| \sqrt{\eta +\lambda }\, \bar\partial _ h^*v\| _ h$. The Riesz representation theorem gives a solution with the asserted norm. The twisting functions are bounded on $\Omega $, since $\Phi $ is smooth and bounded away from both $-\infty $ and $0$. The argument first uses a complete Kähler metric; approximating the Euclidean metric by the Euclidean metric plus small positive multiples of a complete metric gives the Euclidean statement. Under the identification with canonical-bundle-valued forms, the $(n,0)$ norm integral and the curvature-dual $(n,1)$ integral have the same coordinate expressions as above. Since $h=\mathrm e^{-\chi }(1-\mathrm e^\Phi )$, the identities for $T,\eta ,\lambda $ now give (1.43).

Step 2: division for smooth weights. Put $v_ i=\mathrm e^{\phi _ i}/S$ and $\ell =(v_1f,v_2f)$. Equip $\mathbb C^2$ with the diagonal metric $(\mathrm e^{-\phi _1},\mathrm e^{-\phi _2})$. Then $\ell $ is orthogonal to the kernel line of the sum map, generated by $(1,-1)$, and $|\ell |^2=|f|^2/S$. Write $\delta =\phi _1-\phi _2$. The Hessian formula gives

\[ \begin{aligned} A& =v_1(\phi _{1,j\bar k})+v_2(\phi _{2,j\bar k}) +v_1v_2(\delta _ j\overline{\delta _ k}),\\ \bar\partial (v_1f)& =fv_1v_2\bar\partial \delta . \end{aligned} \]

Thus $v_1v_2|\bar\partial \delta |_ A^2\leq 1$ and, for $b=\bar\partial (v_1f)$,

\[ |b|_ A^2\mathrm e^{-\chi } \leq \frac{|f|^2\mathrm e^{-\psi }}{S^2}. \]

Solve (1.43) for this $b$ and set $F=\ell -u(1,-1)$. Since $v_1+v_2=1$, the vector $F=(F_1,F_2)$ is holomorphic and its components sum to $f$. The squared norm of $(1,-1)$ is $\mathrm e^{-\phi _1}+\mathrm e^{-\phi _2}=S\mathrm e^{-\phi _1-\phi _2}$. Orthogonality and (1.43) consequently give

\[ \begin{aligned} \int |F|^2\mathrm e^{-\psi } & =\int \frac{|f|^2\mathrm e^{-\psi }}{S} +\int |u|^2\mathrm e^{\Phi -\chi }\\ & \leq \int \frac{|f|^2\mathrm e^{-\psi }}{S^2}. \end{aligned} \]

This proves (1.42) for smooth weights.

Step 3: general weights. Exhaust $\Omega $ by bounded pseudoconvex domains with compact closures in $\Omega $. On each of them, convolve the weights on a slightly larger neighborhood and add a sufficiently small positive multiple of $|z|^2$. These smooth plurisubharmonic weights dominate the original weights, converge to them almost everywhere, preserve $S<1$ on the chosen closure, and make $\Phi $ strictly plurisubharmonic. The corresponding integrals $J$ are bounded by the original one. On every compact subset the weights have common upper bounds, so (1.42) gives uniform unweighted local $L^2$ bounds for both components. The holomorphic mean-value inequality and a diagonal extraction give holomorphic limits. Almost-everywhere convergence of the weights and Fatou's lemma yield (1.42) on $\Omega $, and $F_1+F_2=f$ passes to the limit. $\square$

Proposition 1.16.9. Let $\psi $ and $\phi $ be plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $ with $\phi <0$, and let $p>1$. For holomorphic $f$ with $I=\| f\| _{\psi +\phi }^2<+\infty $, define

\[ c=\inf \{ \| g\| _\psi ^2:g\in \mathcal O(\Omega ),\quad f-g\in \Gamma (\Omega ,\mathcal I(p\phi +\psi ))\} . \]

Then $c\leq (1-1/p)I$.

Proof. For $t>0$, apply lemma 1.16.8 with

\[ \phi _1=\log \frac{t}{1+t}+p\phi ,\qquad \phi _2=-\log (1+t). \]

Their exponential sum is $S=(1+t\mathrm e^{p\phi })/(1+t)<1$. The integral $J$ of the lemma is finite, since $\| f\| _\psi ^2\leq I$ and $S\geq 1/(1+t)$. The first component $F_1$ belongs to $\Gamma (\Omega ,\mathcal I(p\phi +\psi ))$, so the second component $F_2=f-F_1$ is admissible for $c$, and the norm estimate gives

\[ \frac{c}{1+t}\leq \int _\Omega \frac{|f|^2\mathrm e^{-\psi }}{(1+t\mathrm e^{p\phi })^2}\, \mathrm{d}V. \]

Set $t=x^ p$ and integrate over $x>0$. With $\kappa _ k=\int _0^\infty (1+x^ p)^{-k}\, \mathrm{d}x$, Tonelli's theorem and the change of variable $x\mapsto x\mathrm e^\phi $ give $c\kappa _1\leq I\kappa _2$; the pole set of $\phi $ is Lebesgue null. Since $p>1$, both integrals are finite, and integrating the derivative of $x/(1+x^ p)$ over $(0,\infty )$ gives $\kappa _2=(1-1/p)\kappa _1$. The argument does not require the infimum to be attained. $\square$

Theorem 1.16.10. Let $\psi $ and $\phi _1,\ldots ,\phi _ r$ be plurisubharmonic functions on $\Omega $, with $r\geq 1$ and $\phi _ i<0$, and choose $p_ i>1$. For holomorphic $f$ with

\[ I=\| f\| _{\psi +\sum _ i\phi _ i}^2<+\infty , \]

put

\[ c=\inf \left\{ \| g\| _\psi ^2:g\in \mathcal O(\Omega ),\quad f-g\in \sum _{i=1}^ r\Gamma (\Omega ,\mathcal I(p_ i\phi _ i+\psi ))\right\} . \]

Then

1.51
\begin{equation} c\leq \prod _{i=1}^ r\left(1-\frac1{p_ i}\right)I. \end{equation}

For $r\leq n$, this constant is optimal for the stated class of data.

Proof. Set $\psi _ i=\psi +\sum _{j>i}\phi _ j$, $a_ i=1-1/p_ i$, and $f_0=f$. For arbitrary $\varepsilon _ i>0$, proposition 1.16.9 successively gives holomorphic $f_ i$ such that

\[ \begin{aligned} \| f_ i\| _{\psi _ i}^2& \leq a_ i\| f_{i-1}\| _{\psi _ i+\phi _ i}^2+\varepsilon _ i,\\ f_{i-1}-f_ i& \in \Gamma (\Omega ,\mathcal I(p_ i\phi _ i+\psi _ i)). \end{aligned} \]

Since $\psi _ i\leq \psi $, these ideals are contained in $\mathcal I(p_ i\phi _ i+\psi )$. As $f-f_ r$ is the sum of the differences $f_{i-1}-f_ i$, the function $f_ r$ is admissible for $c$. Moreover, $\psi _ i+\phi _ i=\psi _{i-1}$ for $i\geq 1$ and $\psi _ r=\psi $, so iteration gives

\[ c\leq \left(\prod _ i a_ i\right)I +\sum _{i=1}^ r\varepsilon _ i\prod _{j>i}a_ j. \]

Letting every $\varepsilon _ i$ tend to zero proves (1.51).

For sharpness when $r\leq n$, take the unit polydisk $\Omega =\Delta ^ n$, $f=1$, $\psi =0$, and $\phi _ i=p_ i^{-1}\log |z_ i|^2$ for $i\leq r$. By lemma 1.16.1, $\mathcal I(p_ i\phi _ i)=(z_ i)$. Every admissible $g$ equals one on $Y=\{ z_1=\cdots =z_ r=0\} $, hence $g(0)=1$. Orthogonality of the monomials on the polydisk gives $\| g\| _0^2\geq \pi ^ n$, with equality for $g=1$. Direct radial integration gives $I=\pi ^ n\prod _ i p_ i/(p_ i-1)$, so equality holds in (1.51). $\square$

The congruence condition in theorem 1.16.10 involves a sum of global sections, and the successive remainders in its proof are global; a condition on a single germ would not produce them. The estimate holds for any number of weights, whereas the optimality assertion uses distinct coordinate directions and therefore requires $r\leq n$.

1.16.2 The optimal constant in dimension one

In this subsection, $\Omega \subset \mathbb C$ is a pseudoconvex domain with Euclidean area $\mathrm{d}V$, and we use the weighted norms and spaces of global sections of 1.16.1. In one variable, every multiplier ideal is determined by vanishing orders at points. This allows us to combine the weights into a single one before applying the one-weight estimate, and leads to a better constant than the product bound.

Lemma 1.16.11. For a subharmonic function $u$ on $\Omega $ and $x\in \Omega $,

1.54
\begin{equation} \mathcal I(u)_ x=(z-x)^{\lfloor \nu (u,x)\rfloor }\mathcal O_{\Omega ,x}. \end{equation}

These ideals form a locally principal ideal sheaf.

Proof. With the normalization of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ fixed in the conventions, $\mu =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is a positive Radon measure and $a=\mu (\{ x\} )=\nu (u,x)$. After removal of this atom, the distribution $u-a\log |z-x|^2$ has positive complex Hessian, so it has a subharmonic representative $v$, and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ has no atom at $x$. Since subharmonic functions that agree almost everywhere agree everywhere,

\[ u=a\log |z-x|^2+v. \]

We claim that, for each $t>0$, the function $\mathrm e^{-tv}$ is integrable on some neighborhood of $x$. Indeed, since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ has no atom at $x$, there is a disk $D=D(x,R)\Subset \Omega $ with $M=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)(D)<1/t$. On $D$ write

\[ v(z)=\int _ D\log |z-y|^2\, \mathrm{d}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)(y)+h(z), \]

where $h$ is harmonic: the integral is locally integrable by Fubini's theorem and has complex Hessian $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ on $D$, so it differs from $v$ by a harmonic distribution. If $M=0$, the claim follows from harmonicity. For $M>0$, Jensen's inequality for the probability measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)/M$ gives

\[ \exp \left(-t\int _ D\log |z-y|^2\, \mathrm{d}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)(y)\right) \leq \frac1M\int _ D |z-y|^{-2tM}\, \mathrm{d}(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)(y). \]

Since $tM<1$, the integral of $|z-y|^{-2tM}$ over $z\in D(x,R/2)$ is bounded uniformly in $y\in D$. The harmonic function $h$ is bounded below on this smaller disk, so Tonelli's theorem proves the claim. (Truncating the logarithmic kernel justifies Jensen's inequality at its poles.)

A nonzero holomorphic germ of order $k$ at $x$ is a unit times $(z-x)^ k$. Since $v$ is locally bounded above, weighted integrability requires $k+1>a$; equality is excluded by the logarithmic divergence of the radial integral. Conversely, if $k+1>a$, choose $b>1$ such that $b(a-k)<1$. Then $|z-x|^{2k-2a}$ belongs locally to $L^ b$, and $\mathrm e^{-v}$ belongs locally to $L^{b/(b-1)}$ by the claim, so Hölder's inequality proves integrability. Thus the germ belongs to $\mathcal I(u)_ x$ exactly when $k\geq \lfloor a\rfloor $.

The points $y$ with $\nu (u,y)\geq 1$ form a locally finite set, since each of them carries an atom of mass at least one of the locally finite measure $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. Near any point, therefore, the ideal sheaf is generated by a single power of a coordinate, which proves the last assertion. $\square$

The distance condition below involves a sum of spaces of global sections. In one variable, this sum coincides with the space of global sections of the sum of the ideal sheaves.

Lemma 1.16.12. If $u_1,\ldots ,u_ r$ are subharmonic functions on $\Omega $, then

1.55
\begin{equation} \Gamma \left(\Omega ,\sum _{i=1}^ r\mathcal I(u_ i)\right) =\sum _{i=1}^ r\Gamma (\Omega ,\mathcal I(u_ i)). \end{equation}

Proof. For $r=1$ there is nothing to prove, so let $r\geq 2$. Put $J_ i=\mathcal I(u_ i)$ and $J=\sum _ iJ_ i$. By lemma 1.16.11, these sheaves are locally free of rank one. At each point, the order of $J$ is the smallest of the orders of the $J_ i$, and a local generator of an ideal $J_ i$ attaining this order also generates $J$. Consequently the sum map $\bigoplus _ iJ_ i\to J$ is locally split, and its kernel $E$ is a holomorphic vector bundle of rank $r-1$.

Fix $f\in \Gamma (\Omega ,J)$. Gluing local holomorphic lifts of $f$ with a smooth partition of unity gives a smooth section $s$ of $\bigoplus _ iJ_ i$ whose image is $f$. Since $f$ is holomorphic, $\beta =\bar\partial s$ is an $E$-valued $(0,1)$-form. It suffices to solve $\bar\partial v=\beta $ for a section $v$ of $E$, for then $s-v$ is a holomorphic lift of $f$.

We solve this equation using a bundle metric of positive curvature. Choose a smooth strictly subharmonic exhaustion function $\rho \geq 1$ of $\Omega $. The Kähler metric

\[ \omega _*=\mathrm i\, \mathrm{d}z\wedge \mathrm{d}\bar z +\mathrm i\partial \bar\partial \rho ^2 \]

is complete: it dominates $2\mathrm i\partial \rho \wedge \bar\partial \rho $, so $\rho $ has bounded gradient with respect to it, and therefore every curve leaving all compact subsets has infinite length.

Equip $E$ with a smooth Hermitian metric $h$. Choose a smooth increasing convex function $\chi $ on $[1,+\infty )$ so that the metric $h_\chi =h\mathrm e^{-\chi (\rho )}$ has

\[ \mathrm i\Theta _{E,h_\chi }\geq \omega _*\operatorname {Id}_ E, \qquad \int _\Omega |\beta |_{h_\chi ,\omega _*}^2\, \mathrm{d}V_{\omega _*}<+\infty . \]

These two requirements can be imposed successively on the compact sublevel sets of $\rho $: increase $\chi '$ so that $\chi '(\rho )$ times the positive Hessian of $\rho $ dominates the negative part of the curvature, and increase $\chi $ so that the integral over each shell $\{ m\leq \rho <m+1\} $ is at most $2^{-m}$. Increasing the slopes preserves the inequalities already achieved.

Use the global frame $\mathrm{d}z$ of the canonical bundle $K_\Omega $ to identify $E$-valued $(0,q)$-forms with $(E\otimes K_\Omega ^{-1})$-valued $(1,q)$-forms, and give $K_\Omega ^{-1}$ its flat coordinate metric. Its curvature is zero, and $\omega _*$ dominates the Euclidean metric, so the identified form $\beta $ remains $L^2$. The Bochner–Kodaira inequality for compactly supported forms $w$ of type $(1,1)$ gives

\[ \| \bar\partial ^*w\| ^2\geq \langle [\mathrm i\Theta _{E,h_\chi },\Lambda _{\omega _*}]w,w\rangle \geq \| w\| ^2. \]

Here we used that there are no $(1,2)$-forms. By completeness, cutoffs and smoothing extend this inequality to the domain of the adjoint. Since $\beta $ is $L^2$, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality bounds the functional $w\mapsto \langle w,\beta \rangle $ by $\| \beta \| \| \bar\partial ^*w\| $, and the Riesz representation theorem gives a distributional solution $v$ of $\bar\partial v=\beta $. Thus $s-v$ is distributionally holomorphic, hence holomorphic in every bundle chart, and it is the required global lift. No global weighted norm condition is imposed on its components. $\square$

Theorem 1.16.13. Let $\psi $ and $\phi _1,\ldots ,\phi _ r$ be subharmonic functions on $\Omega $, with $r\geq 1$ and $\phi _ i<0$, and choose $p_ i>1$. For holomorphic $f$ with $I=\| f\| _{\psi +\sum _ i\phi _ i}^2<+\infty $, define

\[ c=\inf \left\{ \| g\| _\psi ^2:g\in \mathcal O(\Omega ),\quad f-g\in \sum _{i=1}^ r\Gamma (\Omega ,\mathcal I(p_ i\phi _ i+\psi ))\right\} , \qquad s=\sum _{i=1}^ r\frac1{p_ i}. \]

Then

1.56
\begin{equation} c\leq \max \{ 1-s,0\} \, I. \end{equation}

For every fixed choice of the exponents, this constant is optimal over the stated class of data. If $s\geq 1$, the function $g=0$ is admissible.

Proof. Put $J_ i=\mathcal I(p_ i\phi _ i+\psi )$ and $J=\sum _ iJ_ i$. At a point $x$, write $A=\nu (\psi ,x)$ and $B_ i=\nu (\phi _ i,x)$. By lemma 1.16.11, the order of $J_ x$ is $\min _ i\lfloor A+p_ iB_ i\rfloor $.

Suppose first that $s<1$, and put $P=1/s>1$. The numbers $P/p_ i$ sum to one, so

\[ \min _ i p_ iB_ i\leq \sum _ i\frac{P}{p_ i}p_ iB_ i=P\sum _ iB_ i. \]

Taking integer parts and using lemma 1.16.11 gives $\mathcal I(P\sum _ i\phi _ i+\psi )\subseteq J$. By lemma 1.16.12, every global section of this smaller ideal sheaf lies in $\sum _ i\Gamma (\Omega ,J_ i)$, so every remainder admissible for the one-weight problem is admissible for $c$. Proposition 1.16.9 with $\phi =\sum _ i\phi _ i$ and $p=P$ therefore gives $c\leq (1-1/P)I=(1-s)I$.

Now let $s\geq 1$. Since $I<+\infty $, we have $f\in \Gamma (\Omega ,\mathcal I(\psi +\sum _ i\phi _ i))$. If $f\equiv 0$ there is nothing to prove; otherwise let $k$ be its order at $x$. Then $k+1>A+\sum _ iB_ i$. If its germ did not belong to $J_ x$, we would have $k+1\leq A+p_ iB_ i$ for every $i$. With $L=k+1-A>0$, this would give $\sum _ iB_ i\geq sL\geq L$, a contradiction. Hence $f\in \Gamma (\Omega ,J)$, and lemma 1.16.12 shows that $g=0$ is admissible.

For sharpness when $s<1$, take the unit disk, $\psi =0$, $f=1$, and $\phi _ i=p_ i^{-1}\log |z|^2$. Then $J_ i=(z)$ for all $i$, so an admissible $g$ has $g(0)=1$. Orthogonality of the monomials on the disk gives $c=\pi $, attained by $g=1$, whereas $I=\pi /(1-s)$. If $s\geq 1$, keep these weights and take $f=z^ q$ with an integer $q\geq 0$ satisfying $q+1>s$. Then $I=\pi /(q+1-s)>0$ and $c=0$, so the constant $0$ cannot be replaced by a negative number. $\square$

Consequently, for $r\geq 2$ the product constant of theorem 1.16.10 is not optimal on plane domains. The argument above relies on all local ideals being principal; it does not determine the optimal constant when $n\geq 2$ and $r>n$.

1.16.3 Distance for several hyperplane weights

In this subsection, $n\geq 2$, $B\subset \mathbb C^ n$ is the unit ball, and $\mathrm{d}V$ is Euclidean volume. If the weights have logarithmic poles along several hyperplanes through the origin, every admissible remainder must take a prescribed value at their common zero. On the other hand, the input norm stays finite as long as the combined radial exponent is below the dimension. Together these observations give a positive distance even in part of the range where the planar distance of theorem 1.16.13 vanishes.

Example 1.16.14. Fix $r>n$ and $p>r/n$. There are negative plurisubharmonic functions $\phi _1,\ldots ,\phi _ r$ on $B$ for which

\[ I=\int _ B\mathrm e^{-\sum _ i\phi _ i}\, \mathrm{d}V<+\infty \]

and

1.57
\begin{equation} \inf \left\{ \int _ B|g|^2\, \mathrm{d}V:g\in \mathcal O(B),\quad 1-g\in \sum _{i=1}^ r\Gamma (B,\mathcal I(p\phi _ i))\right\} =\frac{\pi ^ n}{n!}>0. \end{equation}

In particular, for $r/n<p\leq r$, the distance is positive although $\sum _ i1/p=r/p\geq 1$.

Proof. Choose distinct real numbers $t_1,\ldots ,t_ r$ and unit linear forms

\[ \ell _ i(z)=\frac{\sum _{k=0}^{n-1}t_ i^ kz_{k+1}}{(\sum _{k=0}^{n-1}t_ i^{2k})^{1/2}}. \]

Any $n$ of these forms are linearly independent, since the determinant of their unnormalized coefficients is a nonzero Vandermonde determinant. Put $\phi _ i=p^{-1}\log |\ell _ i|^2$. Since the coefficient vectors of the $\ell _ i$ have norm one, $|\ell _ i(z)|<1$ on $B$, so the $\phi _ i$ are negative plurisubharmonic functions. By lemma 1.16.1, $\mathcal I(p\phi _ i)=(\ell _ i)$.

At a nonzero point, at most $n-1$ of the forms vanish. These forms are linearly independent and can be used as part of a holomorphic coordinate system, while the other forms are bounded away from zero on a small neighborhood of the point. Hence

\[ W(z)=\prod _{i=1}^ r|\ell _ i(z)|^{-2/p} \]

is locally integrable off the origin, because each factor $|\ell _ i|^{-2/p}$ has exponent $2/p<2$ (note that $p>r/n>1$). By compactness, $W$ is integrable on every closed annulus. Set $s=r/p$ and let $\mathrm{d}\sigma $ denote surface measure on the unit sphere. Homogeneity and polar integration over such an annulus show that

\[ A=\int _{|\zeta |=1}\prod _ i|\ell _ i(\zeta )|^{-2/p}\, \mathrm{d}\sigma (\zeta )<+\infty . \]

Tonelli then gives

\[ I=A\int _0^1\rho ^{2n-1-2s}\, \mathrm{d}\rho =\frac{A}{2(n-s)}<+\infty , \]

since $s<n$. This takes care of the common zero at the origin as well as of the individual hyperplanes.

Every global section of $(\ell _ i)$ vanishes at the origin. Thus every admissible $g$ in (1.57) satisfies $g(0)=1$. For each $z\in B$, the holomorphic function $\lambda \mapsto g(\lambda z)$ is defined on a disk of radius greater than one, and the submean inequality for the subharmonic function $|g(\lambda z)|^2$ gives

\[ \frac1{2\pi }\int _0^{2\pi }|g(\mathrm e^{\mathrm i\tau }z)|^2\, \mathrm{d}\tau \geq |g(0)|^2=1. \]

Integrating in $z$ and using the rotation invariance of $B$ proves $\int _ B|g|^2\, \mathrm{d}V\geq \operatorname {Vol}(B)=\pi ^ n/n!$, also when the integral is infinite. The constant $g=1$ is admissible and attains this bound. $\square$

Hence, for these equal exponents, the optimal universal constant in an estimate of the distance by a multiple of $I$ is at least $\pi ^ n/(n!I)>0$. In particular, the vanishing of the optimal constant for $s\geq 1$ in theorem 1.16.13 does not extend to higher dimensions. The example does not determine the optimal constant for $r>n$.

1.17 Lelong numbers and divisorial estimates

Lelong numbers measure the logarithmic concentration of a positive current. Along a divisor, a lower bound for the generic Lelong number allows us to subtract the corresponding multiple of the divisor without losing positivity. At points, the local mass formula controls Lelong numbers under convergence and uniformly in a fixed background. For a prime divisor $E$ on a complex manifold, $[E]$ denotes its current of integration, and $\nu (w,E)$ is the generic Lelong number along $E$ of a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $w$, as defined in section 1.13.

We use the following local consequence of Siu's decomposition theorem, recalled in [SGPT] and [DemAM11]. If $T$ is a closed positive $(1,1)$-current and $E$ is a prime divisor, the restriction of (the coefficient measures of) $T$ to $E$ equals the generic Lelong number of $T$ along $E$ times $[E]$. In particular, removing finitely many of these divisorial components from $T$ leaves a positive current. Distinct prime divisors do not contribute overlapping mass, since their intersections have codimension at least two. All these are local statements about closed currents.

Lemma 1.17.1. Let $M$ be a complex manifold, let $\beta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $M$, let $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(M,\beta )$, and let $E_1,\ldots ,E_ N$ be distinct prime divisors on $M$, with $N\geq 1$. Then

\[ \beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w-\sum _{j=1}^ N\nu (w,E_ j)[E_ j]\geq 0. \]

The form $\beta $ need not be closed.

Proof. Work on a coordinate ball and choose a smooth function $\rho $ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \beta $. The current

\[ T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w+\rho )=(\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w)+(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho -\beta ) \]

is closed and positive. A smooth form, viewed through its coefficient measures, assigns zero mass to every proper analytic set. Thus $T$ and $\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ have the same restriction to $\bigcup _ jE_ j$, and the consequence of Siu's theorem stated above identifies this restriction with $\sum _ j\nu (w,E_ j)[E_ j]$. Subtracting it from the positive current $\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ leaves the restriction of $\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ to the complement of $\bigcup _ jE_ j$, which is positive. This proves the inequality locally, hence globally. Note that $\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ itself need not be closed. $\square$

Order makes the support theorem a rigidity statement for potentials: two ordered quasi-plurisubharmonic functions whose Hessians agree outside an analytic set already differ by a constant.

Lemma 1.17.2. Let $u\leq v$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on a connected compact complex manifold $X$, and let $Z\subsetneq X$ be analytic. If $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ on $X\setminus Z$, then $v=u+C$ for a real constant $C$.

Proof. The current $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v-u)$ is real, closed, of order zero and supported on $Z$. By the support theorem in [Chapter III, Corollaries 2.11 and 2.14, pp. 141–143, DemCADG],

1.58
\begin{equation} S=\sum _{j=1}^ m c_ j[Z_ j],\qquad c_ j=\nu (v,Z_ j)-\nu (u,Z_ j)\leq 0, \end{equation}

where $Z_ j$ are the prime divisorial components of $Z$. Indeed, the support theorem first gives real constants $c_ j$ such that $S-\sum _ jc_ j[Z_ j]$ is supported in codimension at least two; this closed current of order zero then vanishes by the same theorem. Near a generic smooth point of $Z_ j$, with local equation $z_1=0$, the function $v-u-c_ j\log |z_1|^2$ has zero complex Hessian and is therefore smooth and pluriharmonic. This identifies $c_ j$ with the difference of the generic Lelong numbers, and $c_ j\leq 0$ because $u\leq v$.

Thus $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u-v)=-S\geq 0$. Hence the locally integrable distribution $u-v$ has a global plurisubharmonic representative, which is constant by the maximum principle on the compact connected manifold $X$. Since quasi-plurisubharmonic functions that agree almost everywhere agree everywhere, $v=u+C$ for a constant $C$. $\square$

Every closed real $(1,1)$-current is the sum of a smooth closed form and the complex Hessian of a global distribution, and positivity of the current makes this potential quasi-plurisubharmonic. This decomposition allows us to attach an ordinary singularity type to a divisorial current.

Lemma 1.17.3. Let $Z$ be a complex manifold and $T$ a closed real $(1,1)$-current on $Z$. There are a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $ and a real distribution $v$ such that

\[ T=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v. \]

If $T\geq 0$, then $v$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic. Any two distributional potentials in such decompositions differ by a smooth function.

Proof. The local $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-Poincaré lemma gives real distributional potentials $v_ i$ on the sets of a cover of $Z$ by coordinate neighborhoods, with $T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ i$; for positive $T$ these potentials are plurisubharmonic. See [Chapter III, Section 1.18 and Proposition 1.19, DemCADG]. On overlaps $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ i-v_ j)=0$. Taking the Euclidean trace shows that $v_ i-v_ j$ is a harmonic distribution, so it is smooth by elliptic regularity.

Choose a smooth locally finite partition of unity $(\chi _ i)$ subordinate to the cover and put $v=\sum _ i\chi _ i v_ i$, each term being extended by zero as a distribution. On the $j$th neighborhood,

\[ v-v_ j=\sum _ i\chi _ i(v_ i-v_ j) \]

is smooth. Thus $\beta =T-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v=-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v-v_ j)$ is smooth on each neighborhood, hence on $Z$, and it is real and closed. For positive $T$, the same identity expresses $v$ locally as a plurisubharmonic function plus a smooth function. Finally, if $T=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v=\beta '+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v'$ are two such decompositions, then $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v-v')=\beta '-\beta $ is smooth; taking a smooth Hermitian trace and applying elliptic regularity shows that $v-v'$ is smooth. $\square$

For a potential whose complex Hessian is a divisorial current minus a smooth form, ordinary domination, ideal domination and the corresponding inequalities for generic Lelong numbers along the divisors are all equivalent. Countably many divisors are allowed, provided the divisorial current has finite mass.

Lemma 1.17.4. Let $Y$ be a connected compact complex manifold. Let $(D_ j)$ be a finite or countable family of distinct prime divisors on $Y$ and $c_ j>0$ real coefficients such that $S=\sum _ jc_ j[D_ j]$ is a positive current of finite mass, and let $\beta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $Y$. Let $v\in \mathrm{QPSH}(Y)$ satisfy

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v=S-\beta . \]

Then, for $a\in \mathrm{QPSH}(Y)$, the following conditions are equivalent:

\[ a\preceq v,\qquad a\preceq _{\mathcal I}v,\qquad \nu (a,D_ j)\geq c_ j\quad \hbox{for every }j. \]

Proof. We first note that $\nu (v,D_ j)=c_ j$. Indeed, the restriction of $S$ to $D_ j$ is $c_ j[D_ j]$: distinct divisors intersect in codimension at least two, and such sets carry no mass of any divisor current, also after countable summation. Smooth forms give every divisor zero mass. The local form of Siu's decomposition, applied after a smooth correction, therefore gives $\nu (v,D_ j)=c_ j$.

Ordinary domination implies ideal domination by proposition 1.12.4, and ideal domination implies the asserted inequalities for generic Lelong numbers by theorem 1.16.3. It remains to prove that these inequalities imply ordinary domination.

Choose a Hermitian form $\Lambda $ with $a\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\Lambda )$. By lemma 1.17.1 and the inequalities $\nu (a,D_ j)\geq c_ j$, each finite partial sum of $S$ can be subtracted from $\Lambda +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$ without losing positivity. Letting the partial sums increase to $S$ gives

\[ \Lambda +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a-S\geq 0. \]

Consequently $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a-v)\geq \beta -\Lambda $. Hence the integrable function $a-v$ has a quasi-plurisubharmonic representative, which is bounded above on the compact manifold $Y$. This yields $a\leq v+C$ almost everywhere, and lemma 1.3.3 extends this inequality everywhere. Thus $a\preceq v$. $\square$

Together, the two preceding lemmas attach a canonical ordinary singularity type to a countable divisorial current of finite mass.

Corollary 1.17.5. Let $Z$ be a connected compact complex manifold, let $(D_ j)$ be a finite or countable family of distinct prime divisors on $Z$, and let $c_ j>0$. Suppose that $S=\sum _ jc_ j[D_ j]$ is a closed positive current of finite mass. Then there are quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $v$ on $Z$ satisfying

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v=S-\beta \]

for a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $, and any two such functions differ by a smooth function. In particular, they determine a canonical ordinary singularity type, which depends only on $S$.

Proof. Lemma 1.17.3, applied to $T=S$, gives the decomposition, with $v$ quasi-plurisubharmonic since $S\geq 0$, and shows that any two such potentials differ by a smooth function. By proposition 1.12.2, they have the same ordinary singularity type. $\square$

Lemma 1.17.6. Let $u_ j,u$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on a complex manifold with a common smooth lower curvature bound, that is, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\geq -\beta $ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq -\beta $ for one smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $. If $u_ j\to u$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$, then at every point $x$,

\[ \nu (u,x)\geq \limsup _ j\nu (u_ j,x). \]

Proof. On a coordinate ball about $x$, add one smooth function to all the potentials to make them plurisubharmonic, and write $p_ j,p$ for the resulting functions. Let $n$ be the dimension and $\kappa $ the Euclidean form of the chart. Distributional convergence gives weak convergence of the positive trace measures $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p_ j\wedge \kappa ^{n-1}$ to $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p\wedge \kappa ^{n-1}$. For every sufficiently small radius $r$ such that the sphere of radius $r$ about $x$ has zero mass for the limit measure, the masses of the balls converge. The Lelong monotonicity formula bounds each $\nu (p_ j,x)$ by the normalized trace mass of $p_ j$ on such a ball. Taking the upper limit in $j$, and then letting these radii decrease to zero, gives $\limsup _ j\nu (p_ j,x)\leq \nu (p,x)$. Since smooth additions do not change Lelong numbers, this proves the lemma. $\square$

Lemma 1.17.7. Let $Z$ be a connected compact complex manifold and let $\beta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form. There is a finite constant $C_\beta $ such that

\[ \nu (v,z)\leq C_\beta \qquad \text{for every }v\in \mathrm{PSH}(Z,\beta )\text{ and every }z\in Z. \]

In particular, the generic Lelong numbers of all $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(Z,\beta )$ along all prime divisors are bounded by $C_\beta $. No normalization of the potentials is needed.

Proof. Since adding a constant does not change Lelong numbers, we may assume that $\sup _ Zv=0$. By theorem 1.4.2, these normalized potentials have a common $L^1$ bound. Cover $Z$ by finitely many triples of concentric coordinate balls, a smaller, an intermediate and a larger one, such that the smaller balls cover $Z$. On each larger ball choose a fixed smooth quadratic function $r$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r\geq \beta $, so that $p=v+r$ is plurisubharmonic there. The functions $p$ obtained in this way have uniformly bounded local $L^1$ norms.

Write $d=\dim Z$ and let $\kappa $ be the Euclidean form in the chart. Choose a nonnegative smooth cutoff $\chi $ supported in the larger ball and equal to one on an intermediate ball. Positivity and distributional integration by parts give

\[ \int _{\text{intermediate ball}}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p\wedge \kappa ^{d-1} \leq \int \chi \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p\wedge \kappa ^{d-1} =\int p\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge \kappa ^{d-1} \leq C\| p\| _{L^1}. \]

Every point of the smaller ball is the center of a Euclidean ball of a fixed radius contained in the intermediate ball. The local Lelong monotonicity formula, recalled in [SGPT], bounds the Lelong number at this center by a fixed multiple of the trace mass of the intermediate ball. The finite cover gives one bound on $Z$. Since smooth additions and additive constants do not change Lelong numbers, this proves the assertion for the original potentials. $\square$

The Bergman estimates also control Lelong numbers on a fixed local modification. A local divisor over $X$ is a prime divisor $E$ on the source of a local modification $\pi :Z\to U$ of a coordinate neighborhood $U\subset X$, that is, a modification with smooth source $Z$. For a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $u$ on $X$, let $\nu (u\circ \pi ,E)$ be the generic Lelong number of $u\circ \pi $ along $E$. For a holomorphic function or an ideal sheaf, $\operatorname {ord}_ E$ denotes the generic vanishing order along $E$ (for an ideal, the minimum of the orders of its local generators). Let $\operatorname {Jac}\pi $ be the holomorphic Jacobian determinant of $\pi $ in local coordinates. Write

\[ \nu _ E(u)=\nu (u\circ \pi ,E),\qquad A_ U(E)=1+\operatorname {ord}_ E(\operatorname {Jac}\pi ). \]

The second number is the log discrepancy of $E$. Local modifications are not required to extend over the compact manifold.

Lemma 1.17.8. For a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $u$ on $X$, every local divisor $E$ over $X$, and every real $m>0$, one has

\[ \max \! \left\{ \nu _ E(u)-\frac{A_ U(E)}m,0\right\} \leq \frac1m\operatorname {ord}_ E\mathcal I(mu) \leq \nu _ E(u). \]

Here $\operatorname {ord}_ E\mathcal I(mu)$ is the order along $E$ of the pulled-back ideal. Consequently, if locally $a_ m=m^{-1}\log \sum _ k|f_{m,k}|^2+O(1)$, where the $f_{m,k}$ generate $\mathcal I(mu)$, then $\nu _ E(a_ m)\to \nu _ E(u)$ as $m\to \infty $.

Proof. The upper estimate follows from the local Bergman lower bound in proposition 1.14.1: the logarithm of the sum of squared moduli of local generators of $\mathcal I(mu)$, divided by $m$, is at least $u$ up to a locally bounded term. Pulling back and taking generic Lelong numbers along $E$ reverses this inequality.

For the lower estimate, take a local generator $f$ of the ideal and set $k=\operatorname {ord}_ E(f\circ \pi )$. Near a generic point of $E$, choose a local coordinate $z_1$ defining $E$. Divisorial removal (lemma 1.17.1), after a smooth correction, gives

\[ u\circ \pi \leq \nu _ E(u)\log |z_1|^2+C. \]

Shrink around a point where the factors of $f\circ \pi $ and of the Jacobian complementary to the powers of $z_1$ do not vanish. The change-of-variables formula and local integrability of $|f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-mu}$ then require integrability in $z_1$ of

\[ |z_1|^{2(k+A_ U(E)-1-m\nu _ E(u))}. \]

This implies $k+A_ U(E)>m\nu _ E(u)$, and in particular the weaker non-strict lower bound in the statement. Taking the minimum over a finite set of local generators and using that orders are nonnegative proves the lower estimate. Since $\nu _ E(a_ m)=m^{-1}\operatorname {ord}_ E\mathcal I(mu)$, the last assertion follows by letting $m\to \infty $. $\square$

For rational $m>0$, the function $a_ m$ has analytic singularities in the sense of section 1.13; the estimate for real $m$ does not require this.

Blowing up a point converts the divisorial estimate into a pointwise order bound on any local modification.

Lemma 1.17.9. Let $U$ be a complex manifold of dimension $n$, let $u$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on $U$, and let $\pi :Y\to U$ be a modification with smooth source. Fix $y\in Y$ and an integer $m\geq 1$. Write $J_\pi $ for the holomorphic Jacobian in local coordinates near $y$. If a nonzero holomorphic germ $g$ at $\pi (y)$ belongs to $\mathcal I(mu)$, then

\[ \operatorname {ord}_ y(g\circ \pi ) \geq m\nu (u\circ \pi ,y)-n-\operatorname {ord}_ yJ_\pi . \]

Proof. If $n\geq 2$, let $\rho :\widetilde Y\to Y$ be the blowup of $y$ and let $E$ be its exceptional divisor. For $n=1$, take $\rho $ to be the identity and $E=\{ y\} $. Set $F=\pi \circ \rho $ and write $J_ F$ for its local holomorphic Jacobian determinant. Generic orders along $E$ give

\[ \nu (u\circ F,E)=\nu (u\circ \pi ,y),\qquad \operatorname {ord}_ E(g\circ F)=\operatorname {ord}_ y(g\circ \pi ). \]

On a standard blowup chart the Jacobian of $\rho $ has order $n-1$ along $E$. The chain rule therefore gives the log discrepancy over $U$ as

\[ A_ U(E)=1+\operatorname {ord}_ E J_ F =n+\operatorname {ord}_ yJ_\pi . \]

Since $g\in \mathcal I(mu)$, the order of $g\circ F$ along $E$ is at least the order of this ideal. The lower estimate in lemma 1.17.8, applied to $F$, therefore proves the inequality. $\square$

Lemma 1.17.10. Let $v$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on $X$, and let $a$ have analytic singularities on $X$. If $v\preceq _{\mathcal I}a$, then $v\preceq a$. More precisely, it suffices to assume the finitely many inequalities

\[ \nu (v\circ \pi ,E)\geq \nu (a\circ \pi ,E) \]

for the prime components $E$ of the divisor obtained by principalizing, on one log resolution $\pi $, the ideal of an analytic presentation of $a$.

Proof. Principalize the coherent ideal of an analytic presentation of $a$ by a log resolution $\pi :Y\to X$ with smooth $Y$. In suitable local coordinates $z_1,\ldots ,z_ n$ on $Y$,

\[ a\circ \pi =\sum _{i=1}^ r b_ i\log |z_ i|^2+B, \qquad b_ i\geq 0,\qquad B\in L^\infty _{\mathrm{loc}}, \]

where $r\leq n$. The finitely many inequalities in the statement give $\nu (v\circ \pi ,\{ z_ i=0\} )\geq b_ i$; if $v\preceq _{\mathcal I}a$, these inequalities follow from theorem 1.16.3. Add a smooth local function to $v\circ \pi $ to make it plurisubharmonic. Divisorial removal for this closed positive complex Hessian shows that

\[ v\circ \pi -\sum _ i b_ i\log |z_ i|^2 \]

has a quasi-plurisubharmonic representative; here the difference is first interpreted off the divisor, or as a locally integrable distribution. Such a representative is locally bounded above. Since $B$ is locally bounded, $v\circ \pi \leq a\circ \pi +C$ on each relatively compact chart of $Y$, away from the divisor. Since $\pi $ is proper, finitely many such charts cover the inverse image of a smaller coordinate neighborhood in $X$, which makes $C$ uniform there. Push the inequality down off the analytic exceptional set; as quasi-plurisubharmonic functions are determined by their values almost everywhere, it then holds everywhere. Finitely many neighborhoods cover the compact manifold, so $v\leq a+C$ globally.

The only input from resolution of singularities is the principalization of a coherent ideal. Divisorial removal is applied to the closed positive complex Hessian obtained after a local smooth correction, so no closed background on $X$ is needed. $\square$

Lemma 1.17.11. Let $u,v$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on $X$, and let $s$ have analytic singularities on $X$. Suppose that $u+v\leq s+O(1)$, and let $U_ m,V_ m$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions of analytic types $(1/m,\mathcal I(mu))$ and $(1/m,\mathcal I(mv))$.
