Then there are numbers $\delta _ m\downarrow 0$ such that

\[ U_ m+V_ m\leq (1-\delta _ m)s+O(1) \quad \hbox{for all sufficiently large integers }m. \]

The bounded error may depend on $m$.

Proof. If $s$ is bounded, upper bounds for $U_ m$ and $V_ m$ give the assertion with $\delta _ m=1/m$ for $m>1$. Otherwise principalize the ideal of $s$ by a log resolution $\pi :\widetilde X\to X$ with smooth $\widetilde X$. Let $E_ i$ be the components of the resulting divisor, with positive coefficients $c_ i$ in $s\circ \pi $; in local coordinates $z_ i$ defining these components, the singular part of $s\circ \pi $ is $\sum _ i c_ i\log |z_ i|^2$. Set $A_ i=1+\operatorname {ord}_{E_ i}(\operatorname {Jac}\pi )$. The hypothesis and lemma 1.17.8 give

\[ \nu (U_ m\circ \pi ,E_ i)+\nu (V_ m\circ \pi ,E_ i) \geq \nu (u\circ \pi ,E_ i)+\nu (v\circ \pi ,E_ i)-\frac{2A_ i}{m} \geq c_ i-\frac{2A_ i}{m}. \]

Choose a positive integer $N\geq \max _ i2A_ i/c_ i$ and put $\delta _ m=N/m$, which is less than one for large $m$. Then the right-hand side above is at least $(1-\delta _ m)c_ i$. After a smooth local correction, divisorial removal from the positive Hessian of $(U_ m+V_ m)\circ \pi $ shows that

\[ (U_ m+V_ m)\circ \pi -(1-\delta _ m)\sum _ i c_ i\log |z_ i|^2 \]

has a quasi-plurisubharmonic representative, which is locally bounded above. A finite cover of $\widetilde X$ by charts and descent along the proper map $\pi $, as in the proof of lemma 1.17.10, therefore give $U_ m+V_ m\leq (1-\delta _ m)s+O(1)$. The bounded constant may depend on $m$. $\square$

1.18 Potential regularity and metric distance

A Hölder modulus of continuity of the potential controls the trace mass of a positive current on small balls. Averaging the lengths of segments against this trace measure then bounds the distance of the metric defined by the current on its regular locus. This turns regularity of the potential into a metric estimate without any pointwise bound on the Hessian.

Let $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be a coordinate domain with Euclidean form $\omega _{\mathrm E}=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ and Euclidean volume $\mathrm{d}\lambda $. Let $0<\alpha \leq 1$, let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $U$, and let $v\in C^{0,\alpha }(U)$ satisfy $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\geq 0$. Write $\mu =T\wedge \omega _{\mathrm E}^{n-1}$ for the trace measure of $T$.

Theorem 1.18.1. For every open set $V\Subset U$ there are constants $C,r_0>0$ such that

1.59
\begin{equation} \mu (B(x,r))\leq C r^{2n-2+\alpha },\qquad x\in V,\quad 0<r<r_0. \end{equation}

The total variation of every coefficient measure of $T$ is bounded by a constant multiple of $\mu $. Hence the coefficient measures of $T$ vanish on sets of zero Hausdorff $(2n-2+\alpha )$-measure, and all Lelong numbers of $v$ vanish. The estimate implies neither absolute continuity of $\mu $ nor a pointwise bound for the Hessian of $v$.

Suppose in addition that $T$ is a smooth Hermitian form $\eta $ on $U\setminus A$ for a proper analytic subset $A\subset U$. For $x,y\in U\setminus A$, let $d_\eta ^ U(x,y)$ be the infimum of the $\eta $-lengths of piecewise smooth paths in $U\setminus A$ joining $x$ and $y$. Then every point of $U$ has a neighborhood $V\Subset U$ such that, for a constant $C$,

1.60
\begin{equation} d_\eta ^ U(x,y)\leq C|x-y|^{\alpha /2},\qquad x,y\in V\setminus A. \end{equation}

In particular, all sequences in $V\setminus A$ converging to a given point of $A\cap V$ are Cauchy for $d_\eta ^ U$ and have a common limit in the metric completion. If these local hypotheses hold in charts covering $X$, with a proper analytic subset $A\subset X$ and a Hermitian form $\eta $ on $X\setminus A$, then the metric completion of $X\setminus A$ for its intrinsic $\eta $-distance is compact, and the identity of $X\setminus A$ extends to a continuous surjection of $X$ onto it. The metric $\eta $ is incomplete when $A\ne \varnothing $. Both exponents in (1.59) and (1.60) are sharp.

Proof. Choose a nonnegative cutoff function $\chi $ equal to one on $B(x,r)$ and supported in $B(x,2r)$, with second derivatives bounded by $Cr^{-2}$. Positivity and integration by parts, after subtracting the constant $v(x)$ from $v$, give

\[ \mu (B(x,r))\leq C_1r^{2n}+C_2r^{2n-2} \operatorname {osc}_{B(x,2r)}v \leq Cr^{2n-2+\alpha }. \]

Here we used that the constant $v(x)$ integrates to zero against $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \wedge \omega _{\mathrm E}^{n-1}$. The matrix $(a_{ij})$ of Radon–Nikodym derivatives of the coefficient measures with respect to $\mu $ is positive semidefinite almost everywhere, so $|a_{ij}|\leq (a_{ii}+a_{jj})/2$. A ball cover proves the assertion about Hausdorff null sets. After a smooth local correction making $v$ plurisubharmonic, dividing (1.59) by $r^{2n-2}$ shows that all Lelong numbers vanish.

For the distance estimate, let $m=2n$ and let $\tau $ be the Euclidean trace of $\eta $. Then $\tau \, \mathrm{d}\lambda \leq C\mu $ on the regular locus and $|\xi |_\eta \leq C\sqrt\tau |\xi |_{\mathrm E}$ for tangent vectors $\xi $. Polar integration bounds the average $\eta $-length of the segments from $x$ to the points of $B(x,R)$ by a constant times $\int _{B(x,R)}\sqrt\tau (\zeta )|\zeta -x|^{1-m}\, \mathrm{d}\lambda (\zeta )$. On a dyadic annulus of radius $s$, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and (1.59) bound the contribution to this integral by $Cs^{1-m}s^{m/2}s^{(m-2+\alpha )/2}=Cs^{\alpha /2}$. Summing over the annuli gives the bound $CR^{\alpha /2}$.

Now let $x,y\in V\setminus A$, and average the lengths of the broken segments $[x,z]\cup [z,y]$ over $z$ in the ball of radius $|x-y|/2$ about the midpoint of $x$ and $y$. By the preceding bound, some such path has length at most $C|x-y|^{\alpha /2}$. For almost every $z$, both segments avoid $A$: proper analytic sets are locally countable unions of smooth strata of real codimension at least two, and the cones from a fixed regular point over these strata have zero volume. If $V$ is small enough, all these paths stay inside $U$.

By this estimate, all regular sequences converging to a point of $A$ have a common limit in the completion, and the identity of the regular locus extends continuously across $A$. By compactness, the image of this extension is the entire completion; different points of $A$ may be identified. A sequence converging to a point of $A$ cannot converge in the metric to a regular point, since $\eta $ is bounded below by a positive multiple of the Euclidean metric on a small closed neighborhood of that point. Hence the metric $\eta $ is incomplete when $A$ is nonempty. Finally, the local model $v=|z|^\alpha +|w|^2$, in coordinates $(z,w)\in \mathbb C\times \mathbb C^{n-1}$, has trace mass of exact order $r^{2n-2+\alpha }$ on balls centered on $\{ z=0\} $, and the distance to $\{ z=0\} $ is of exact order $|z|^{\alpha /2}$; this proves both sharpness claims. In later applications to potentials with logarithmic poles, the theorem is applied to the positive residual current that remains after the divisorial part has been removed. $\square$

1.18.1 Dini moduli and limits of distances

The annular argument of theorem 1.18.1 also applies to a continuous potential whose modulus of continuity is not a power; what matters is a Dini condition on the square root of the modulus. This gives uniform distance estimates for families whose Hölder constants are not controlled.

Keep the coordinate domain $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ with its Euclidean form $\omega _{\mathrm E}$ and volume $\mathrm{d}\lambda $, but now let $v\in C^0(U)$ and let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form with $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v\geq 0$. On the relatively compact charts under consideration, choose a nondecreasing function $m:(0,r_0)\to [0,+\infty )$ with $m(r)\to 0$ as $r\to 0$ such that

\[ |v(x)-v(y)|\leq m(|x-y|),\qquad |x-y|<r_0. \]

Put

1.61
\begin{equation} D(r)=r+\int _0^ r\frac{\sqrt{m(t)}}{t}\, \mathrm{d}t. \end{equation}

Theorem 1.18.2. Assume that $D(r_0/2)<+\infty $ and that $T$ is a smooth Hermitian form $\eta $ on $U\setminus A$ for a proper analytic subset $A\subset U$. Then every point of $U$ has a neighborhood $V\Subset U$ with constants $C,r_1>0$ such that, for $x,y\in V$,

\[ d_\eta ^ U(x,y)\leq C D(8|x-y|), \qquad x,y\notin A,\quad |x-y|<r_1. \]

The constants depend only on the charts and an upper bound for $|\theta |$; the dependence on $v$ enters only through $D$. All sequences in $V\setminus A$ converging to a given point of $A\cap V$ have a common limit in the metric completion. If these hypotheses hold on a finite cover of a compact connected manifold by charts, the metric completion of the regular locus is compact, and the identity of the regular locus extends to a continuous surjection of the manifold onto it. If $A$ is nonempty, the metric $\eta $ on the regular locus is incomplete.

Proof. Write $d=2n$ and $\mu =T\wedge \omega _{\mathrm E}^{n-1}$. The same cutoff calculation as in theorem 1.18.1, with a constant subtracted from $v$, gives

\[ \mu (B(x,s))\leq C\bigl (s^ d+s^{d-2}m(2s)\bigr ). \]

This uses only the bound for $\theta $; closedness of $\theta $ is not needed. If $\tau $ is the Euclidean trace of $\eta $, then $\tau \, \mathrm{d}\lambda \leq C\mu $ on the regular locus. Polar integration bounds the average length of the segments from a regular point $x$ to the points of $B(x,R)$ by

\[ C\int _{B(x,R)}\sqrt{\tau (\zeta )} |\zeta -x|^{1-d}\, \mathrm{d}\lambda (\zeta ). \]

On an annulus of outer radius $s$, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the trace bound make the contribution to this integral at most

\[ Cs^{1-d}s^{d/2} \bigl (s^ d+s^{d-2}m(2s)\bigr )^{1/2} \leq C\bigl (s+\sqrt{m(2s)}\bigr ). \]

For $s_ j=2^{-j}R$, monotonicity gives

\[ \sum _{j\geq 0}\sqrt{m(2s_ j)} \leq \frac1{\log 2}\int _0^{4R}\frac{\sqrt{m(t)}}t\, \mathrm{d}t. \]

Indeed, $\sqrt{m(2s_ j)}\log 2$ is at most the integral of $\sqrt{m(t)}/t$ over $[2s_ j,4s_ j]$, and these intervals have disjoint interiors; no doubling condition on $m$ is needed. The average length is therefore at most $CD(4R)$.

For $x,y\in V\setminus A$, average the lengths of the broken segments $[x,z]\cup [z,y]$ over $z$ in the ball of radius $|x-y|/2$ about the midpoint of $x$ and $y$, applying the preceding bound with $R=2|x-y|$ at each endpoint. Since the volume of this ball is comparable to $R^ d$, some broken path has length at most $CD(8|x-y|)$. As in the proof of theorem 1.18.1, almost every such path avoids $A$, and for small $V$ it stays inside $U$.

Since $D(r_0/2)<+\infty $, we have $D(r)\to 0$ as $r\to 0$. Hence any two regular sequences converging to the same point are Cauchy and have the same limit in the completion. The argument for the completion in the proof of theorem 1.18.1 applies with this modulus. A positive lower bound for $\eta $ near a regular point prevents a sequence converging to a point of $A$ from converging in the metric to the regular point. This also proves incompleteness when $A$ is nonempty. $\square$

Corollary 1.18.3. In the setting of theorem 1.18.2, let $V$ be as in the theorem. For $0<\beta \leq 1$, a modulus $m(r)\leq Cr^\beta $ gives $d_\eta ^ U(x,y)\leq C'|x-y|^{\beta /2}$ for sufficiently close $x,y\in V\setminus A$. If instead

\[ m(r)\leq C(-\log r)^{-a},\qquad a>2, \]

then for sufficiently close $x,y\in V\setminus A$,

\[ d_\eta ^ U(x,y)\leq C'(-\log |x-y|)^{1-a/2}. \]

These are only sufficient conditions; we do not claim that the Dini condition is necessary.

Proof. Integrating the square root of the power modulus gives a multiple of $r^{\beta /2}$. For the logarithmic modulus, the substitution $s=-\log t$ gives

\[ \int _0^ r\frac{(-\log t)^{-a/2}}t\, \mathrm{d}t =\frac{(-\log r)^{1-a/2}}{a/2-1}. \]

In either case the term $r$ in $D(r)$ is bounded by the asserted rate for small $r$. The theorem now gives both estimates; the fixed dilation of the argument of $D$ changes only the constants. $\square$

Corollary 1.18.4. Let $\omega _0$ be a Hermitian form on $X$ and $d_{\omega _0}$ the distance of the associated Riemannian metric. For each $j$, let $T_ j=\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ j\geq 0$, where $\theta _ j$ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form, $v_ j$ is continuous, and $T_ j$ is a smooth Hermitian form off a proper analytic subset $A_ j$. Assume

\[ |\theta _ j|_{\omega _0}\leq \Lambda ,\qquad |v_ j(x)-v_ j(y)|\leq m(d_{\omega _0}(x,y)), \]

with one fixed $\Lambda $ and one nondecreasing modulus $m$ tending to zero such that the function in (1.61) satisfies $D(r_0/2)<+\infty $. By theorem 1.18.2, the identity of $X\setminus A_ j$ extends to a continuous surjection of $X$ onto the metric completion of $X\setminus A_ j$; let $d_ j$ be the pseudometric on $X$ obtained by pulling back the completed distance. Then the diameters of the $d_ j$ are uniformly bounded, and a subsequence converges uniformly on $X\times X$ to a continuous pseudometric. If $T_ j\geq c\omega _0$ on the regular loci for one $c>0$, the limit is a metric and dominates $\sqrt c\, d_{\omega _0}$.

Proof. A finite fixed chart cover and the theorem give common constants $C,r_1>0$ such that

\[ d_ j(x,y)\leq CD(Cd_{\omega _0}(x,y)), \qquad d_{\omega _0}(x,y)<r_1. \]

For a pair meeting $A_ j$, approximate it by regular pairs in the manifold topology. The completed distance and $D$ are continuous, so the same estimate holds for all such pairs in $X\times X$. No uniform bound on the geometry of the analytic sets enters the constants. Dividing a minimizing geodesic of $d_{\omega _0}$ into a uniformly bounded number of short pieces and using the triangle inequality gives a uniform diameter bound. The triangle inequality also gives

\[ |d_ j(x,y)-d_ j(x',y')| \leq d_ j(x,x')+d_ j(y,y'), \]

so the distance functions are equicontinuous. The Arzelà–Ascoli theorem gives a subsequence $d_{j_ k}$ converging uniformly on $X\times X$ to a function $d_\infty $. Nonnegativity, symmetry, vanishing on the diagonal and the triangle inequality pass to the limit. The lower bound $T_ j\geq c\omega _0$ gives $d_ j\geq \sqrt c\, d_{\omega _0}$, first for regular pairs and then for all pairs, which proves the last assertion.

Without this lower bound the limit may degenerate. For $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$, take $\theta _ j=\varepsilon _ j\omega _0$ and $v_ j=0$, with $A_ j=\varnothing $. These data satisfy the hypotheses with $m(r)=r^2$, and $d_ j=\sqrt{\varepsilon _ j}\, d_{\omega _0}$ converges uniformly to zero. $\square$

1.19 Obstacle envelopes

Given an obstacle function, one looks for the largest $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function lying below it. The local upper-envelope theorem constructs this envelope as soon as the competitors have a common upper bound, and normalized compactness provides such a bound for an obstacle that is finite almost everywhere or prescribed on a non-pluripolar compact set. Letting the obstacles $\min (u+C,0)$ increase with $C$ produces the envelope $P_\theta [u]$ of the ordinary singularity type of $u$.

A smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ is called big if there are $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $\delta >0$ such that

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \delta \omega . \]

Here $\omega $ is any Hermitian form; changing it changes $\delta $ but not the condition. The definition does not require $\theta $ to be closed.

Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form. For an extended-real function $f$ on $X$, defined at least outside a pluripolar set (the obstacle), write

\[ P_\theta (f)=\left(\sup \{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):v\leq f \ \hbox{q.e.}\} \right)^*. \]

As in section 1.6, q.e. (quasi-everywhere) means outside a pluripolar set. The functions $v$ in the supremum are called competitors; the empty supremum is $-\infty $. For obstacles $f_1,\ldots ,f_ N$, we write $P_\theta (f_1,\ldots ,f_ N)=P_\theta (\min _ if_ i)$; when the $f_ i$ belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, this is their rooftop envelope. For an upper semicontinuous obstacle, the pointwise constraint $v\leq f$ gives the same competitors: by the local submean inequality and upper semicontinuity of $f$, a quasi-plurisubharmonic function lying below $f$ almost everywhere lies below it everywhere.

If the family of competitors is nonempty and uniformly bounded above, then $P_\theta (f)\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. To apply the local upper-envelope theorem while keeping the exact lower bound $-\theta $, fix a constant positive form $\kappa $ in local coordinates. For each $\epsilon >0$, shrink the coordinate neighborhood and choose a constant form $\theta '$ with $-\epsilon \kappa \leq \theta -\theta '\leq \epsilon \kappa $. A smooth function $r$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r=\theta '+\epsilon \kappa $ makes every competitor plus $r$ plurisubharmonic, so the local envelope theorem gives $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}P_\theta (f)\geq -2\epsilon \kappa $ there. Letting $\epsilon $ tend to zero proves the exact bound. The same local theorem shows that the regularization changes the supremum only on a pluripolar set.

The following observation gives the upper bound for an unbounded obstacle. Suppose that $f\leq A$, for a real constant $A$, on a Borel set $E$ with $\operatorname {Vol}(E)=\int _ E\mathrm{d}V>0$, where $\mathrm{d}V$ is the volume form fixed in theorem 1.4.2. If $h$ is a competitor, put $\widetilde h=h-\sup _ Xh$. By theorem 1.4.2,

\[ (\sup _ Xh-A)\operatorname {Vol}(E) \leq \int _ E|\widetilde h|\, \mathrm{d}V\leq C_\theta \quad \hbox{whenever }\sup _ Xh>A. \]

Thus $\sup _ Xh\leq A+C_\theta /\operatorname {Vol}(E)$ for every competitor. In particular, the competitors for a measurable obstacle that is finite almost everywhere have a common upper bound: apply this to a sublevel set $\{ f\leq A\} $ of positive volume.

An obstacle bound on a non-pluripolar compact set also bounds every competitor globally, even when that set has zero smooth volume.

Lemma 1.19.1. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, let $K\subseteq X$ be compact and non-pluripolar, and let $w\in C(K,\mathbb R)$. There is a constant $C=C(\theta ,K,w)$ such that every $a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfying $a\leq w$ quasi-everywhere on $K$ has $\sup _ X a\leq C$.

Proof. Otherwise there are such functions $a_ j$ with

\[ M_ j:=\sup _ Xa_ j\geq 2^ j+\max _ Kw. \]

The normalized functions $v_ j=a_ j-M_ j\leq 0$ have uniformly bounded $L^1$ norms by theorem 1.4.2. Choose $A>0$ with $\theta \leq A\omega $. The partial sums

\[ s_ N=\sum _{j=1}^ N2^{-j}v_ j \]

decrease, have uniformly bounded $L^1$ norms, and satisfy $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s_ N\geq -A\omega $. By corollary 1.4.3, their limit $s$ is a quasi-plurisubharmonic function; in particular, $s$ is not identically $-\infty $.

Let $N_ j\subseteq K$ be a pluripolar set outside which $a_ j\leq w$ on $K$. On $K\setminus \bigcup _ jN_ j$ one has $v_ j\leq -2^ j$ for every $j$, hence $s=-\infty $. The pole set $\{ s=-\infty \} $ of the quasi-plurisubharmonic function $s$ is pluripolar, and so is the countable union of the $N_ j$. Their union would contain all of $K$, contradicting the hypothesis on $K$. $\square$

Suppose that $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is nonempty. Then the envelope of the zero obstacle,

\[ V_\theta =P_\theta (0), \]

belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$: the competitors are bounded above by zero, and they include $u-\sup _ Xu$ for every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. In particular,

\[ u-\sup _ Xu\leq V_\theta \qquad (u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )). \]

A potential $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has minimal singularities if $u\preceq v$ for every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. By the last inequality, $V_\theta $ has minimal singularities, and every potential $v$ with minimal singularities satisfies $v\sim V_\theta $. If $\theta \geq 0$, then $0$ is a competitor, and hence $V_\theta =0$.

Lemma 1.19.2. Let $\eta $ be a smooth semipositive real $(1,1)$-form on $X$. If an obstacle $f$ is finite almost everywhere and bounded below quasi-everywhere by a constant, then $P_\eta (f)$ is a bounded $\eta $-plurisubharmonic function.

Proof. Since $\eta \geq 0$, constants are $\eta $-plurisubharmonic, so the constant lower bound of $f$ is a competitor. The finite-sublevel argument above gives a common upper bound for all competitors. Hence $P_\eta (f)$ is an $\eta $-plurisubharmonic function lying between these two constants. $\square$

Proposition 1.19.3. If $f\in C^0(X)$, then $P_\omega (f)$ is continuous. Moreover,

\[ \| P_\omega (f)-P_\omega (g)\| _\infty \leq \| f-g\| _\infty \quad (f,g\in C^0(X)). \]

Proof. The constant $\min _ Xf$ is a competitor, so $p=P_\omega (f)$ is bounded. The local envelope theorem gives $p\leq f$ quasi-everywhere, and continuity of $f$ makes this inequality hold everywhere. By theorem 1.11.1, there are smooth $\omega $-plurisubharmonic functions $v_ j\downarrow p$. Fix $\epsilon >0$. The continuous functions $v_ j-f$ decrease to a function that is at most zero, so $v_ j\leq f+\epsilon $ everywhere for all large $j$; otherwise the decreasing compact sets $\{ v_ j-f\geq \epsilon \} $ would have nonempty intersection. Hence $v_ j-\epsilon $ is a competitor, and

\[ p\leq v_ j\leq p+\epsilon . \]

Thus $v_ j\to p$ uniformly, which proves continuity. The last inequality follows from the monotonicity of $P_\omega $ in the obstacle and the identity $P_\omega (g+c)=P_\omega (g)+c$ for constants $c$, applied with $c=\| f-g\| _\infty $. $\square$

Definition 1.19.4. For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, set

\[ P_\theta [u] =\left(\lim _{C\to +\infty }P_\theta (u+C,0)\right)^* =\left(\sup \{ h\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):h\preceq u,\ h\leq 0\} \right)^*. \]

We call $u$ an envelope fixed point, or an ordinary model, if $P_\theta [u]=u$; in the Kähler literature such potentials are called model potentials. For $F\in C^\infty (X,\mathbb R)$, replacing the constraint $h\leq 0$ by $h\leq F$ defines $P_{\theta ,F}[u]$.

The two expressions agree, because the family in the second one is the increasing union over $C$ of the families defining $P_\theta (u+C,0)$. They do not change if zero is replaced by $V_\theta $, since every $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function below zero lies below $V_\theta $. The competitors include $u-\sup _ Xu$ and are bounded above by zero, so $P_\theta [u]\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $u-\sup _ Xu\leq P_\theta [u]\leq 0$. In particular, $u\preceq P_\theta [u]$; this does not assert that $P_\theta [u]-u$ is bounded.

Directly from the definition, $P_\theta [u]$ depends only on the ordinary singularity type of $u$, and

\[ u\preceq v\quad \Longrightarrow \quad P_\theta [u]\leq P_\theta [v]. \]

Replacing the condition $h\preceq u$ by $h\sim _{\mathcal I}u$ gives the multiplier-ideal envelope $P_\theta [u]_{\mathcal I}$ of definition 16.2.1. This is a different operator; its relation to $P_\theta [u]$ is studied in chapter 16.

For a bounded obstacle in a big background, exceptional sets in the competitor inequality can be removed by a small singular perturbation.

Lemma 1.19.5. Let $\theta $ be big, let $f$ be a bounded function on $X$, and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $u\leq f$ quasi-everywhere. Then there are $u_ t\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $t>0$, such that $u_ t\leq f$ everywhere and $u_ t\to u$ almost everywhere as $t\downarrow 0$.

Proof. Choose $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \kappa \omega $ for some $\kappa >0$. The exceptional set $E=\{ u>f\} $ is pluripolar. The global form of Josefson's theorem on compact complex manifolds gives a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\chi $ with $\chi =-\infty $ on $E$; see [Theorem 1.1.5, SGPT]. After rescaling and adding a constant, we may assume that $\chi \leq 0$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi \geq -\omega $. Subtract a constant from $\rho $ so that $\rho \leq \inf _ X f$. With $s=t/\kappa $, put

\[ u_ t=\frac{u+t\chi +s\rho }{1+s}. \]

All three functions in the numerator are finite almost everywhere, so $u_ t$ is not identically $-\infty $. Moreover,

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ t =\frac{\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u+t\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\chi +s(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )}{1+s} \geq 0. \]

Outside $E$, both $u$ and $\rho $ are at most $f$, while $\chi \leq 0$, so $u_ t\leq f$. On $E$ the numerator is $-\infty $. Finally, $u_ t\to u$ wherever all three functions are finite, which is a set of full measure. $\square$

For an obstacle prescribed only on a compact set, the same perturbation shows that pluripolar exceptions in the constraint do not change the regularized envelope.

Proposition 1.19.6. Let $\theta $ be big, let $K\subseteq X$ be compact and non-pluripolar, and let $\psi $ be a bounded real function on $K$. Set

\[ \begin{aligned} R& =\sup \{ u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):u\leq \psi \text{ on }K\} ,\\ Q& =\sup \{ u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):u\leq \psi \text{ q.e. on }K\} . \end{aligned} \]

Then $R^*=Q^*$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and has minimal singularities; it is bounded if $\theta \geq 0$. Moreover, this regularized envelope does not change if the pointwise constraint is imposed only on $K\setminus E$, for any pluripolar subset $E\subseteq K$.

Proof. Put $m=\inf _ K\psi $ and $M=\sup _ K\psi $. Since $\theta $ is big, $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is nonempty, and $V_\theta \leq 0$; hence $V_\theta +m$ belongs to the family defining $R$. Applying lemma 1.19.1 with the constant obstacle $M$ gives a common upper bound $C$ for the larger family defining $Q$. The upper-envelope argument at the beginning of section 1.19 therefore shows that $R^*$ and $Q^*$ belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Now fix a competitor $u$ for $Q$ and define a bounded function on $X$ by

\[ f(x)= \begin{cases} \psi (x),& x\in K,\\ \max \{ \sup _ Xu,M\} ,& x\notin K. \end{cases} \]

Then $u\leq f$ quasi-everywhere. By lemma 1.19.5, there are $u_ t\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfying $u_ t\leq f$ everywhere and $u_ t\to u$ almost everywhere. Each $u_ t$ is a competitor for $R$, so $R^*\geq u$ almost everywhere, and lemma 1.3.3 gives this inequality everywhere. Taking the supremum over $u$ and regularizing yields $R^*\geq Q^*$; the inclusion of the competitor families gives the reverse inequality.

Write $F=R^*=Q^*$. Since $F\leq C$, the potential $F-C$ is a competitor for $V_\theta $. Thus

\[ V_\theta +m\leq F\leq V_\theta +C, \]

which proves minimal singularities and, when $\theta \geq 0$, boundedness.

Finally, if $E\subseteq K$ is pluripolar, the family constrained on $K\setminus E$ lies between the families defining $R$ and $Q$, so its regularized supremum also equals $F$. Note that this argument uses the upper bound obtained from the compact set $K$, even when $K\setminus E$ is not compact. $\square$

A potential with minimal singularities stays away from the poles of a strictly positive potential with analytic singularities, in the following sense.

Lemma 1.19.7. Let $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ have analytic singularities with smooth remainder and satisfy $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \varepsilon \omega $ for some $\varepsilon >0$. Set $\Omega =\{ \rho >-\infty \} $. If $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has minimal singularities and is continuous on $\Omega $, then $\varphi -\rho $ is continuous, bounded below, and proper on $\Omega $: all its sublevel sets are compact in $\Omega $.

Proof. Adding constants to $\rho $ and $\varphi $ does not affect the assertions, so we may assume that $\rho \leq 0$ and $\sup _ X\varphi =0$. Since $\varphi $ has minimal singularities, there is a constant $C$ with $\varphi \geq V_\theta -C$. Choose $M\geq 0$ with $\theta \geq -M\omega $. If $a\in (0,1)$ is sufficiently close to one, then

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a\rho ) =a(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho )+(1-a)\theta \geq \bigl (a\varepsilon -(1-a)M\bigr )\omega >0. \]

Thus $a\rho $ is a competitor for $V_\theta $, and therefore

\[ 0\geq \varphi \geq V_\theta -C\geq a\rho -C, \]

and hence

1.66
\begin{equation} \varphi -\rho \geq (1-a)(-\rho )-C. \end{equation}

By hypothesis $\varphi $ is continuous on $\Omega $, and $\rho $ is smooth there because it has analytic singularities with smooth remainder. Hence $\varphi -\rho $ is continuous on $\Omega $, and by (1.66) it is bounded below. Since $\rho \to -\infty $ at $X\setminus \Omega $, the sets $\{ \rho \geq -c\} $ are compact subsets of $\Omega $, and (1.66) places every sublevel set of $\varphi -\rho $ in one of them. Being closed in $\Omega $, each sublevel set is therefore compact. $\square$

1.19.1 A hyperplane Green envelope on the ball

For a logarithmic pole along a hyperplane section of the unit ball, the envelope can be computed explicitly by restricting to the complex lines normal to the hyperplane. The example also shows how envelopes of bounded obstacles recover the finite part of a logarithmic pole, and that continuity inside the domain does not imply continuity up to the boundary.

In this subsection let $n\geq 2$, let $m\geq 1$ be an integer, and write

\[ B=\{ (\zeta ,w)\in \mathbb C\times \mathbb C^{n-1}:|\zeta |^2+|w|^2<1\} , \qquad D=B\cap \{ \zeta =0\} . \]

Define $G_ m$ as the pointwise supremum of the functions $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ such that $u\leq 0$ and, near every point of $D$, $u\leq m\log |\zeta |^2+C$ for some local constant $C$. In particular, $u=-\infty $ on $D$. The constants need not stay bounded as the point of $D$ approaches $\partial B$.

Proposition 1.19.8. The envelope $G_ m$ is given by

1.67
\begin{equation} G_ m(\zeta ,w)=m\log \frac{|\zeta |^2}{1-|w|^2}. \end{equation}

It is plurisubharmonic and continuous on $B$ with values in $[-\infty ,0)$. The finite part $G_ m-m\log |\zeta |^2$, first defined off $D$, extends smoothly across $D$ as the plurisubharmonic function

\[ H_ m(w)=-m\log (1-|w|^2). \]

Proof. The function $-\log (1-|w|^2)$ is smooth and plurisubharmonic on $|w|<1$: its complex Hessian is

\[ \left(\frac{\delta _{jk}}{1-|w|^2} +\frac{\overline w_ jw_ k}{(1-|w|^2)^2}\right)_{j,k}. \]

Thus the right-hand side of (1.67) is plurisubharmonic; it is negative on $B$ and satisfies the pole condition, so it is a competitor. For the reverse inequality, fix a competitor $u$ and a point $w$ with $|w|<1$. The restriction $u(\cdot ,w)$ is either identically $-\infty $ or subharmonic on the disk of radius $R=(1-|w|^2)^{1/2}$. In the latter case, $u(\zeta ,w)-m\log |\zeta |^2$ is subharmonic on the punctured disk and, by the pole condition, bounded above near zero, so it extends as a subharmonic function across zero.

Since $u\leq 0$, this extension is at most $-m\log r^2$ on every circle $|\zeta |=r<R$. The maximum principle and then $r\uparrow R$ give

\[ u(\zeta ,w)\leq m\log \frac{|\zeta |^2}{R^2}. \]

On $D$ both sides are $-\infty $. Taking the supremum over $u$ proves the formula. Near a point of $D$, the function $H_ m$ is bounded and the logarithmic term tends to $-\infty $, which proves continuity there as an extended-real function. $\square$

For a finite continuous obstacle $\Phi $ on $B$, put

\[ P_ B\Phi =\left(\sup \{ v\in \mathrm{PSH}(B):v\leq \Phi \} \right)^*. \]

The local upper-envelope theorem used in section 1.19 applies whenever this family is nonempty and locally uniformly bounded above. Continuity of $\Phi $ ensures that the regularized envelope still satisfies $P_ B\Phi \leq \Phi $.

Proposition 1.19.9. For $t>0$, define

\[ \Phi _ t(\zeta ,w)=\min \{ -m\log |\zeta |^2,t\} , \qquad V_ t=P_ B\Phi _ t, \]

with $\Phi _ t=t$ on $D$. Then $0\leq V_ t\leq t$, and

1.68
\begin{equation} -m\log \bigl (1-(1-\mathrm e^{-t/m})|w|^2\bigr ) \leq V_ t(\zeta ,w)\leq H_ m(w). \end{equation}

In particular, $V_ t\uparrow H_ m$ pointwise on all of $B$, including $D$. The convergence is uniform on every compact subset, with

1.69
\begin{equation} 0\leq H_ m-V_ t \leq \frac{m\mathrm e^{-t/m}R^2}{1-R^2} \qquad \text{where }|w|\leq R<1. \end{equation}

Proof. The obstacle is continuous, nonnegative and at most $t$. Zero is a competitor, so the upper-envelope theorem gives $V_ t\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ with $0\leq V_ t\leq \Phi _ t\leq t$. Consequently $m\log |\zeta |^2+V_ t$ is a competitor for $G_ m$, and proposition 1.19.8 gives $V_ t\leq H_ m$ off $D$. At a point $(0,w)$, the submean inequality on any sufficiently small circle in the normal line gives the same bound, since $H_ m$ does not depend on $\zeta $.

For the lower bound, set $a_ t=1-\mathrm e^{-t/m}$ and $F_ t(w)=-m\log (1-a_ t|w|^2)$. The Hessian calculation above, with $|w|^2$ replaced by $a_ t|w|^2$, shows that $F_ t$ is plurisubharmonic. Moreover, $0\leq F_ t\leq t$, and $F_ t\leq H_ m\leq -m\log |\zeta |^2$ on $B\setminus D$ because $|\zeta |^2<1-|w|^2$. Thus $F_ t\leq \Phi _ t$ everywhere, and hence $F_ t\leq V_ t$. Finally,

\[ H_ m-F_ t =m\log \left(1+\frac{\mathrm e^{-t/m}|w|^2}{1-|w|^2}\right) \leq \frac{m\mathrm e^{-t/m}|w|^2}{1-|w|^2}. \]

This proves (1.69) and the convergence; the monotonicity of $V_ t$ in $t$ follows from that of $\Phi _ t$. $\square$

Example 1.19.10. At every point $p=(0,w_0)\in \partial B$ with $|w_0|=1$, the cluster set of $G_ m$ at $p$, that is, the set of limits of $G_ m$ along sequences in $B$ converging to $p$, is all of $[-\infty ,0]$. Indeed, set $w_ s=(1-s^2)^{1/2}w_0$ for $0<s<1$. For every fixed $0<a<1$, the points $(as,w_ s)$ belong to $B$, tend to $p$ as $s\to 0$, and satisfy $G_ m=m\log a^2$. Choosing $a=a_ s<1$ with $a_ s\to 1$ gives the limit zero, whereas the points $(s^2,w_ s)$ give $G_ m=m\log s^2\to -\infty $. These realize every value in $[-\infty ,0]$, and negativity excludes all others. Thus $G_ m$, although continuous on $B$, has no continuous extension to any point of $\partial B$ in the closure of $D$.

1.20 The minimum principle

An infimum of plurisubharmonic functions need not be plurisubharmonic. Kiselman's minimum principle describes an important exception: the infimum over a real parameter $t$ of a family $u_ t$ whose complexification $u_{\operatorname{Re}z}$ is plurisubharmonic in the joint variables. Freezing the coefficients of the background form, we show that the principle preserves the exact lower curvature bound $-\theta $.

Lemma 1.20.1. Let $I\subset \mathbb R$ be a nonempty open interval, set $S_ I=\{ z\in \mathbb C:\operatorname{Re}z\in I\} $, and let $\pi :X\times S_ I\to X$ be the projection. Let $(u_ t)_{t\in I}$ be a family of functions on $X$ such that $U(x,z)=u_{\operatorname{Re}z}(x)$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic on $X\times S_ I$ and $\pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U\geq 0$. For each $x$, the function $t\mapsto u_ t(x)$ is finite and convex or identically $-\infty $. The finite alternative holds for almost every $x$.

Proof. A local smooth correction making $U$ plurisubharmonic can be chosen to depend only on $x$. Hence the restriction of $U$ to $\{ x\} \times S_ I$ is subharmonic or identically $-\infty $. In the first case its distributional Laplacian is the second derivative of $t\mapsto u_ t(x)$, which is therefore nonnegative. The resulting convex representative is finite on $I$, and by the submean inequality it agrees everywhere with the subharmonic function. Finally, $U$ is locally integrable on the product, and Fubini's theorem, applied on products of coordinate balls with a fixed rectangle in $S_ I$, excludes the identically $-\infty $ alternative for almost every $x$. $\square$

Corollary 1.20.2. Let $I\subset \mathbb R$ be a nonempty open interval. A radial function $z\mapsto q(\log |z|^2)$ is subharmonic on the annulus $\{ z:\log |z|^2\in I\} $ if and only if $q$ is finite and convex on $I$. If the function identically equal to $-\infty $ is also regarded as subharmonic, the alternative $q\equiv -\infty $ has to be added.

Proof. The holomorphic covering map $z=\mathrm{e}^{w/2}$ pulls the function back to $q(\operatorname{Re}w)$, and the argument of lemma 1.20.1 proves necessity. Conversely, a finite convex function $q$ has nonnegative distributional second derivative. Thus $q(\operatorname{Re}w)$ is subharmonic, and subharmonicity descends through the local biholomorphisms of the covering. $\square$

Proposition 1.20.3. Under the hypotheses of lemma 1.20.1, the function

\[ q(x)=\inf _{t\in I}u_ t(x) \]

is either $\theta $-plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $; in particular, the infimum need not be regularized. If $I$ is bounded, then $q\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Proof. The classical local Kiselman principle states that if a plurisubharmonic function on the product of a domain with a strip does not depend on the imaginary part of the strip variable, then its infimum over the real part is plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $; the interval of the real part may be unbounded. We use this local result from [SGPT].

Fix $x_0\in X$, choose local holomorphic coordinates centered at $x_0$, and let $\kappa $ be their Euclidean form. Fix $\eta >0$. On a sufficiently small coordinate ball the constant form $\beta =\theta (x_0)$ satisfies

\[ \beta -\eta \kappa \leq \theta \leq \beta +\eta \kappa . \]

Choose a quadratic function $r$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r=\beta +\eta \kappa $. Then $U+r\circ \pi $ is plurisubharmonic on the product of the ball with $S_ I$. Its infimum over the parameter is $q+r$, so the local principle shows that $q+r$ is plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $ on the ball; in the first case

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq -\beta -\eta \kappa \geq -\theta -2\eta \kappa . \]

In a fixed atlas, the Euclidean forms are bounded by a fixed multiple of $\omega $, so letting $\eta \downarrow 0$ proves the exact lower bound $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq -\theta $. Since a plurisubharmonic function cannot be identically $-\infty $ on a nonempty open set, the local alternative propagates across overlapping balls on the connected manifold $X$. Thus either $q\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ or $q\equiv -\infty $. No regularization is needed, because an infimum of upper semicontinuous functions is upper semicontinuous.

Now suppose that $I$ is bounded. By lemma 1.20.1, the function $t\mapsto u_ t(x)$ is finite and convex for almost every $x$, and a supporting affine function at any point of $I$ bounds it from below on the bounded interval $I$. Hence $q(x)>-\infty $ almost everywhere, which excludes the alternative $q\equiv -\infty $. $\square$

For example, the proposition applies to $u_ t-t\tau $ for a fixed real number $\tau $. On an unbounded interval the infimum may be identically $-\infty $; this alternative will encode the cutoff of a test curve (definition 18.1.1).

1.20.1 Pointwise Legendre transforms

The minimum principle can also show that a supremum is already upper semicontinuous. For a concave family of potentials, the regularized Legendre transform is a potential on a product. The minimum principle identifies its inverse transforms with the original family everywhere, and applying the one-variable duality once more identifies the regularization with the pointwise supremum. We first state this duality precisely, including its behavior at the endpoints; the general Legendre–Fenchel theorem is recalled in [appendix on convex analysis, SGPT].

Lemma 1.20.4. Let $c\in \mathbb R$. Suppose $g:\mathbb R\to [-\infty ,0]$ is upper semicontinuous, concave and decreasing, finite somewhere, and equal to $-\infty $ on $(c,\infty )$. Then

\[ f(t)=\sup _{\tau <c}\bigl (g(\tau )+t\tau \bigr ),\qquad t>0, \]

is finite and convex, satisfies $f(t)\leq ct$, and obeys

\[ g(\tau )=\inf _{t>0}\bigl (f(t)-t\tau \bigr ),\qquad \lim _{t\downarrow 0}f(t)=\sup _{\tau <c}g(\tau ). \]

Conversely, every finite convex $f$ on $(0,\infty )$ with $f(t)\leq ct$ is recovered by the first formula from $g(\tau )=\inf _{t>0}(f(t)-t\tau )$, and satisfies the same endpoint identity. The formulas also hold for $g\equiv -\infty $, with $f\equiv -\infty $.

Proof. Since $g$ is decreasing and finite somewhere, it is finite on a half-line $(-\infty ,\tau _1)$. Hence the defining supremum is finite for $t>0$: it is bounded above by $ct$ and below by any one of its finite terms. Concavity and upper semicontinuity give $g(c)=\lim _{\tau \uparrow c}g(\tau )$, so including $\tau =c$ does not change the supremum. The Legendre transform $t\mapsto \sup _\tau (g(\tau )+t\tau )$ of $-g$ is infinite for $t<0$. At $t=0$ it equals $m=\sup _{\tau <c}g(\tau )$, and the bounds

\[ g(\tau )+t\tau \leq f(t)\leq m+ct \]

show that $f(t)\to m$ as $t\downarrow 0$. The biconjugacy theorem for the closed convex function $-g$ therefore gives the inverse formula; the value at $t=0$ may be omitted without changing the infimum.

Conversely, start with $f$. At each $t>0$, choose the slope $p$ of a supporting line of $f$. The upper bound $f(s)\leq cs$ as $s\to \infty $ forces $p\leq c$. If $p<c$, the supporting line gives $g(p)=f(t)-tp$, so the first formula recovers $f(t)$. If $p=c$, then $f(s)-cs$ is constant for $s\geq t$, because this function is convex and bounded above, hence decreasing, while its supporting line at $t$ is horizontal. Consequently, for every $\tau <c$,

\[ f(t)-ct\leq g(\tau )\leq f(t)-t\tau . \]

and letting $\tau \uparrow c$ again recovers $f(t)$. In particular, $g$ is finite somewhere. As an infimum of affine functions with slopes $-s<0$, it is upper semicontinuous, concave and decreasing. Letting $s\downarrow 0$ in the bound $f(s)-s\tau \leq s(c-\tau )$ gives $g\leq 0$, and letting $s\to \infty $ gives $g=-\infty $ for $\tau >c$. The endpoint identity now follows from the first part. The case of functions identically equal to $-\infty $ follows directly from the formulas. $\square$

Fix $c\in \mathbb R$, and let $\Gamma _\tau \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ for $\tau <c$ be functions with $\Gamma _\tau \leq 0$ such that $\tau \mapsto \Gamma _\tau (x)$ is concave and decreasing for every $x$. Extend the family to $\tau =c$ by the decreasing limit $\Gamma _ c=\lim _{\tau \uparrow c}\Gamma _\tau $, and by $\Gamma _\tau \equiv -\infty $ for $\tau >c$. Define the forward transform of the family by

1.70
\begin{equation} U_ t(x)=\sup _{\tau <c}\bigl (\Gamma _\tau (x)+t\tau \bigr ), \qquad t>0. \end{equation}

For a family $(u_ t)_{t>0}$, the inverse transform is

1.71
\begin{equation} \widehat u_\tau (x)=\inf _{t>0}\bigl (u_ t(x)-t\tau \bigr ). \end{equation}

Lemma 1.20.5. At each $x$, the extended function $\tau \mapsto \Gamma _\tau (x)$ is upper semicontinuous and concave, or identically $-\infty $. Its forward transform satisfies

\[ \Gamma _\tau (x)=\inf _{t>0}\bigl (U_ t(x)-t\tau \bigr ) \qquad (\tau \in \mathbb R). \]

Proof. Fix $\tau <c$ and let $\tau _ j\uparrow \tau $. The decreasing limit of the functions $\Gamma _{\tau _ j}$ dominates $\Gamma _\tau $, so it belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Choose $r\in (\tau ,c)$. Outside the pole set of $\Gamma _ r$, the concave function $s\mapsto \Gamma _ s(x)$ is finite near $\tau $ and hence continuous there. Thus the decreasing limit equals $\Gamma _\tau $ almost everywhere, and hence everywhere by lemma 1.3.3. This proves left continuity at every $\tau <c$; at $c$ it holds by definition. Since the function is decreasing, left continuity gives upper semicontinuity, also where the value is $-\infty $, and concavity survives the extension. The inverse formula is lemma 1.20.4 at each $x$. $\square$

Theorem 1.20.6. For the preceding family $(\Gamma _\tau )_{\tau <c}$, the pointwise supremum in (1.70) is already upper semicontinuous on $X\times (0,\infty )$. Its complexification $U(x,z)=U_{\operatorname{Re}z}(x)$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic on $X\times \{ \operatorname{Re}z>0\} $ and satisfies $\pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}U\geq 0$, where $\pi :X\times \{ \operatorname{Re}z>0\} \to X$ is the projection. Each slice belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Proof. Each term $(x,z)\mapsto \Gamma _\tau (x)+\tau \operatorname{Re}z$ of the supremum satisfies the current inequality on the product, since $\operatorname{Re}z$ is pluriharmonic. The bound $\Gamma _\tau \leq 0$ gives $U_ t\leq ct$, while any fixed $\tau _0<c$ gives the minorant $\Gamma _{\tau _0}+t\tau _0$. By the local upper-envelope theorem, as in section 1.19, the upper semicontinuous regularization $V$ of $U$ on the product is quasi-plurisubharmonic, satisfies $\pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V\geq 0$, and agrees with $U$ almost everywhere. It does not depend on $\operatorname {Im}z$ and satisfies $V_ t\leq ct$. The pointwise supremum $U$ is measurable, since by the left continuity in lemma 1.20.5 the parameters may be restricted to rational numbers.

By Fubini's theorem, there is a set $Z\subset X$ of zero smooth volume, containing the pole set of $\Gamma _{\tau _0}$, such that $U_ t(x)=V_ t(x)$ for almost every $t>0$ whenever $x\notin Z$. For such $x$, both sides are finite convex functions of $t$, by lemma 1.20.4, lemma 1.20.1, so by continuity the equality holds for every $t>0$.

For each $\tau <c$, proposition 1.20.3 gives

\[ \chi _\tau =\inf _{t>0}(V_ t-t\tau ) \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cup \{ -\infty \} . \]

Off $Z$ it equals $\Gamma _\tau $ by lemma 1.20.5. In particular, $\chi _\tau $ is not identically $-\infty $, and lemma 1.3.3 gives $\chi _\tau =\Gamma _\tau $ everywhere. At each $x$, the convex function $t\mapsto V_ t(x)$ can be recovered from these inverse transforms by lemma 1.20.4, also when it is identically $-\infty $. We obtain

\[ V_ t(x)=\sup _{\tau <c}\bigl (\chi _\tau (x)+t\tau \bigr )=U_ t(x). \]

Finally, the minorant $\Gamma _{\tau _0}+t\tau _0$ shows that no slice $U_ t$ is identically $-\infty $, so every slice belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. $\square$

1.21 Chern-connection regularization

Convolution in local coordinates does not directly produce global approximants with a controlled lower curvature bound. Demailly's regularization instead averages over the fibers of the tangent bundle, using the Chern connection of $\omega $, and a Kiselman-type minimum of these averages controls the loss of positivity. The classical curvature estimate for this minimum holds in a Hermitian background, while the $L^1$ and gradient bounds for the averages are elementary.

Fix $C_0>0$ and $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $-C_0\leq u\leq 0$. Distances, gradients and norms of tensors are taken with respect to the Riemannian metric associated with $\omega $.

For small $t>0$, let $R_ tu$ be Demailly's regularization of $u$ formed with the Chern connection of $\omega $. It averages $u$ over the image of the ball of radius $t$ in each tangent space, with a fixed smooth nonnegative radial kernel of integral one. The map used for this purpose is a smooth realization of the holomorphic part of the formal fiberwise expansion of the exponential map; it need not be holomorphic in the fiber. The following classical estimate controls the Kiselman-type transform of these averages, including its mixed base–fiber terms; see section 34.1 for the sources.

Lemma 1.21.1. There are constants $A,K>0$ and $t_*>0$, depending only on $(X,\omega )$ and $C_0$, such that $R_ tu$ is smooth for $0<t<t_*$,

\[ -C_0\leq R_ tu\leq 0,\qquad R_ tu+Kt^2\downarrow u\quad \text{as }t\downarrow 0. \]

For $0<\delta <t_*$ and $c>0$, the bounded function

\[ \Phi _{c,\delta } =\inf _{0<t\leq \delta } \left\{ R_ tu+K(t^2-\delta ^2)+K(t-\delta ) -c\log (t/\delta )\right\} \]

is quasi-plurisubharmonic and satisfies

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _{c,\delta }\geq -(Ac+2K\delta )\omega . \]

At every point, the infimum is attained at some $t>0$.

We use only this curvature estimate from the classical theory. The linear term $K(t-\delta )$ is needed in the Hermitian case, where it absorbs the mixed base–fiber terms in the complex Hessian of the regularization. Since $-c\log (t/\delta )$ tends to $+\infty $ as $t\downarrow 0$ while $u$ is bounded, the infimum is attained away from zero. We need neither smoothness of the minimizing radius nor smoothness of the transform across the region where this radius equals $\delta $.

Next we prove two elementary estimates for the averaging operators. They do not rely on any volume identity from Kähler geometry.

Lemma 1.21.2. For all sufficiently small $t>0$,

\[ \| R_ tu-u\| _{L^1(\mathrm{d}V)}\leq Ct^2, \qquad \| \nabla R_ tu\| _\infty \leq C/t. \]

The constants are uniform for $-C_0\leq u\leq 0$.

Proof. Let $z$ be local coordinates on $X$ and $\xi \in \mathbb C^ n$ the fiber variable with respect to a smooth orthonormal frame of the tangent bundle. Write $\rho (|\xi |^2)$ for the fixed radial averaging kernel, normalized with respect to Lebesgue measure $\mathrm{d}\xi $ and supported in $|\xi |\leq 1$. In these coordinates the averaging map $F$ gives

\[ R_ tu(z)=\int u(F(z,t\xi ))\rho (|\xi |^2)\, \mathrm{d}\xi , \qquad F(z,0)=z. \]

The map $F$ is smooth in these variables, and its fiber differential at zero is an isomorphism. Let $R_ t^*$ be the formal adjoint of $R_ t$ with respect to $\mathrm{d}V$, so that $\int _ X(R_ ta)b\, \mathrm{d}V=\int _ Xa\, R_ t^*b\, \mathrm{d}V$. We first claim that

\[ R_ t^*1=1+O(t^2) \]

uniformly. To see this, insert a smooth partition of unity in the variable $z$, each term being compactly supported in a chart on which the frame is defined. For fixed $\xi $, change variables $x=F(z,t\xi )$ on a neighborhood of the support of a term. The inverse map and its Jacobian have uniform Taylor expansions in $t$. The term of order zero in the transformed density is the chosen partition function at $x$, and the term of order one is linear in $\xi $, so it integrates to zero against the even radial kernel. The remaining density is bounded by $Ct^2\mathrm{d}V(x)$, since the second derivatives of all maps, partition functions and volume coefficients are bounded on these finitely many compact charts. Summing over the partition functions proves the claim.

Consequently,

\[ \left|\int _ X(R_ tu-u)\, \mathrm{d}V\right| =\left|\int _ Xu(R_ t^*1-1)\, \mathrm{d}V\right|\leq Ct^2. \]

By lemma 1.21.1, $R_ tu-u\geq -Kt^2$. A real function $a\geq -Kt^2$ satisfies $|a|\leq a+2Kt^2$; applying this to $a=R_ tu-u$ and integrating gives the first estimate.

For the derivative bound, change variables in the fiber to write $R_ tu(z)=\int _ Xu(x)k_ t(z,x)\, \mathrm{d}V(x)$. In local coordinates the smooth kernel $k_ t$ is supported where $|x-z|\leq Ct$ and satisfies

\[ |\nabla _ z k_ t(z,x)|\leq Ct^{-2n-1}. \]

Indeed, the inverse fiber map and its Jacobian have uniformly bounded derivatives, and differentiating the rescaled radial argument introduces at most one factor $t^{-1}$ in addition to $t^{-2n}$. Since the kernel is smooth with compact support, there are no boundary terms. Integration over a ball of volume at most $Ct^{2n}$ gives $\int |\nabla _ zk_ t|\, \mathrm{d}V\leq C/t$, and multiplication by the uniform bound for $u$ proves the assertion. $\square$

The first estimate relies on the cancellation of the odd first-order term against the even kernel, and not on conservation of Monge–Ampère mass.

1.21.1 The general curvature modulus

Let $\theta $ now be any smooth real $(1,1)$-form and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\cap L^\infty (X)$. We use the same averaging operators and the same metric.

We first state the classical input for this background. The classical Chern-connection regularization theorem applies to the closed current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ with the smooth, possibly nonclosed, lower bound $-\theta $; the curvature of the Chern connection on the tangent bundle is bounded by a fixed multiple of $\omega $. The theorem gives a constant $K_0>0$ such that

\[ R_ tu+K_0t^2\downarrow u. \]

To state its curvature estimate, use local coordinates $z$ on $X$ and a complex radius variable $w$, and put $t=|w|$. Write $\zeta \in T_ z^{1,0}X$ and $\eta \in \mathbb C$ for the base and radius components of a tangent vector, and set $\lambda (z,t)=t\partial _ t(R_ tu(z)+K_0t^2)$. Then $\lambda $ is nonnegative and increasing in $t$, and there are constants $A,C>0$ and a function $\epsilon _0(t)\geq 0$ with $\epsilon _0(t)\downarrow 0$ as $t\downarrow 0$ such that the complex Hessian of $(z,w)\mapsto R_{|w|}u(z)$ is bounded below by the base form $-\theta -A\lambda (z,t)\omega -\epsilon _0(t)\omega $, evaluated on $\zeta $, minus mixed and vertical error terms bounded by $C(|\zeta ||\eta |+|\eta |^2)$. This is the classical regularization estimate for a smooth lower bound.

Lemma 1.21.3. For $K$ sufficiently large, there is an increasing function $\epsilon (\delta )\geq 0$ tending to zero at zero such that, for every small $\delta >0$ and every $c>0$, the function

\[ \Phi _{c,\delta } =\inf _{0<t\leq \delta } \{ R_ tu+K(t^2-\delta ^2)+K(t-\delta )-c\log (t/\delta )\} \]

is bounded and quasi-plurisubharmonic and satisfies

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _{c,\delta } \geq -(Ac+\epsilon (\delta ))\omega . \]

The infimum is attained away from zero. Moreover, $R_ tu+Kt^2\downarrow u$ as $t\downarrow 0$.

Proof. Take $K\geq K_0$ and put $H(z,t)=R_ tu(z)+Kt^2+Kt$. As a function of $\log t$, $H$ is increasing and strictly convex, because $R_ tu+K_0t^2$ has nonnegative increasing logarithmic derivative and the added terms have positive second logarithmic derivative. Its logarithmic derivative $\widetilde\lambda =t\partial _ tH$ is at least $\lambda $.

The complex Hessian of $K|w|$ has a positive vertical term proportional to $K/|w|$. For sufficiently small $|w|=t$, this term absorbs the vertical error and, by the inequality

\[ C|\zeta ||\eta | \leq C' t|\zeta |^2+\frac{K}{8t}|\eta |^2, \]

also the mixed error. After increasing the constants and replacing $\lambda $ by the larger $\widetilde\lambda $, we find that the complex Hessian of $(z,w)\mapsto H(z,|w|)$ is bounded below by the base form

\[ -\theta -(A\widetilde\lambda +\epsilon (t))\omega , \]

where $\epsilon $ is an increasing majorant of $\epsilon _0(t)+C't$.

Since $u$ is bounded, the term $-c\log (t/\delta )$ tends uniformly to $+\infty $ as $t\downarrow 0$, so the minimum occurs on a compact interval of radii away from zero. Strict convexity in $\log t$ makes the minimizing radius unique. At an interior minimum $\widetilde\lambda =c$, while at the upper endpoint $t=\delta $ one has $\widetilde\lambda \leq c$. No differentiability of the minimizing radius is needed.

In more detail, localize near a point of $X$ and fix $a>0$. By continuity of the minimizers and of $\widetilde\lambda $, there is an interval of radii containing the minimizers at all nearby points on which $\widetilde\lambda \leq c+a$. If the minimum is attained at the upper endpoint, keep the upper endpoint $\delta $; an open interval approaching it has the same infimum. Because of the uniform logarithmic term, the lower endpoint can be chosen below all minimizers, and since $\widetilde\lambda $ is increasing, the bound $\widetilde\lambda \leq c+a$ holds throughout the chosen interval. The local Kiselman minimum principle for functions depending only on $|w|$, applied after a local quadratic correction, now gives the lower curvature bound with error $A(c+a)+\epsilon (\delta )$, up to an arbitrarily small error from freezing the smooth base forms. Letting these errors and $a$ tend to zero proves the asserted current inequality and the quasi-plurisubharmonicity. Finally, $K\geq K_0$ preserves the monotone convergence of $R_ tu+Kt^2$ to $u$. $\square$

The classical theorem only gives some modulus $\epsilon $; we do not claim that $\epsilon (\delta )$ is linear in $\delta $. The cancellation and kernel arguments in the proof of lemma 1.21.2 still give

\[ \| R_ tu-u\| _{L^1(\mathrm{d}V)}\leq Ct^2, \qquad \| \nabla R_ tu\| _\infty \leq C/t, \]

because the averaging map is unchanged, $u$ is bounded, and these arguments use only the monotone lower bound $R_ tu-u\geq -Kt^2$.

For continuous potentials, the curvature loss of the averages becomes uniformly small.

Lemma 1.21.4. Let $\alpha $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\alpha )\cap C^0(X)$. There are smooth real functions $u_ j$ and numbers $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$ such that $u_ j\to u$ uniformly and

\[ \alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j\geq -\varepsilon _ j\omega . \]

Proof. Use the Chern averaging operators and the classical estimate stated before lemma 1.21.3, with $\alpha $ in place of $\theta $. For some $K_0,A>0$ and $\epsilon _0(t)\to 0$, put $\lambda (x,t)=t\partial _ t(R_ tu(x)+K_0t^2)$. The estimate with vanishing radius component gives

\[ \alpha +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R_ tu\geq -(A\lambda (\cdot ,t)+\epsilon _0(t))\omega . \]

The function $\lambda $ is nonnegative, continuous for $t>0$, and increasing in $t$. For fixed $x$, the convex function $s\mapsto R_{\mathrm{e}^ s}u(x)+K_0\mathrm{e}^{2s}$ has the finite limit $u(x)$ as $s\to -\infty $. Its nonnegative derivative must therefore tend to zero, since a positive limit of the derivative would make the function tend to $-\infty $. Thus $\lambda (x,t)\to 0$ as $t\to 0$, and for any sequence $t_ j\downarrow 0$, Dini's theorem gives $\| \lambda (\cdot ,t_ j)\| _\infty \to 0$.

The averaging kernels have mass one and only sample points at distance at most $Ct$ from the base point. Uniform continuity of $u$ therefore gives $\| R_ tu-u\| _\infty \to 0$. Set $u_ j=R_{t_ j}u$, and let $\varepsilon _ j\to 0$ be a decreasing positive majorant of $A\| \lambda (\cdot ,t_ j)\| _\infty +\epsilon _0(t_ j)$. $\square$

1.21.2 Hölder reference approximation and weighted smoothing

The Chern averages preserve a Hölder norm uniformly. Their small lower-curvature loss can therefore be absorbed by rescaling, without changing the limiting potential.

Lemma 1.21.5. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap C^{0,\alpha }(X)$ with $0<\alpha \leq 1$, there are smooth $u_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ tending uniformly to $u$, with a common $C^{0,\alpha }$ bound. If $\sup _ Xu=0$, the approximants can also be normalized by $\sup _ Xu_ j=0$.

Proof. Use the Chern averages $R_ tu$ in lemma 1.21.4. For a sequence $t_ j\downarrow 0$ there are $\varepsilon _ j\downarrow 0$ such that

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R_{t_ j}u\geq -\varepsilon _ j\omega , \qquad R_{t_ j}u\longrightarrow u\quad \text{uniformly}. \]

We also have a uniform Hölder bound. In a coordinate chart with a smooth orthonormal tangent frame, the averaging formula is

\[ R_ tu(x)=\int u(F(x,t\xi ))\rho (|\xi |^2)\, \mathrm d\xi . \]

On a smaller chart, smoothness of $F$ bounds $\operatorname {dist}(F(x,t\xi ),F(y,t\xi ))$ by $C\operatorname {dist}(x,y)$, uniformly for small $t$ and $|\xi |\leq 1$. Integrating the Hölder inequality for $u$ gives the same bound for $R_ tu$. A finite cover and the common supremum bound handle pairs outside a common smaller chart.

Set $u_ j=R_{t_ j}u/(1+\varepsilon _ j)$. Then

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ j =\frac{\varepsilon _ j\omega +(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R_{t_ j}u)}{1+\varepsilon _ j}\geq 0. \]

These functions converge uniformly with a common Hölder bound. Subtracting their suprema gives the asserted normalization when $\sup _ Xu=0$. $\square$

Lemma 1.21.6. Let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )\cap C^{0,\alpha }(X)$, $0<\alpha \leq 1$, and let $\mu $ be a positive measure with $\mu \leq A\omega _ u^ n$. For bounded $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $-M\leq \varphi \leq 0$, the Chern averages satisfy

\[ \| R_ t\varphi -\varphi \| _{L^1(\mu )} \leq C t^{2\alpha /(\alpha +2n)} \]

for small $t>0$. The constant is uniform under fixed bounds for $A,M$ and $\| u\| _{C^{0,\alpha }}$.

Proof. First we check a common lower curvature bound for the averages. The base part of the classical Chern curvature estimate stated before lemma 1.21.3 gives

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R_ t\varphi \geq -(A_0\lambda (\cdot ,t)+\epsilon _0(t))\omega , \qquad \lambda (x,t)=t\partial _ t(R_ t\varphi (x)+K_0t^2). \]

Its constants are fixed under the stated bounded range. In the local kernel form used in lemma 1.21.2,

\[ k_ t(x,y)=t^{-2n}a\bigl (x,(y-x)/t,t\bigr ), \]

with uniformly bounded first derivatives and compact support in its rescaled second variable. Differentiating with respect to $t$ at fixed $x,y$ and changing variables shows that

\[ \int |t\partial _ t k_ t(x,y)|\, \mathrm dy\leq C. \]

Thus $|t\partial _ tR_ t\varphi |\leq CM$, so $\lambda $ is uniformly bounded and the displayed curvature estimate supplies one fixed lower bound $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R_ t\varphi \geq -C\omega $. Both $R_ t\varphi $ and $\varphi $ also lie in $[-M,0]$.

Apply corollary 1.8.4 to this pair, whose common lower bound has just been established. By lemma 1.21.2, its smooth-volume $L^1$ error is at most $Ct^2$. Domination by $A\omega _ u^ n$ therefore gives

\[ \int |R_ t\varphi -\varphi |\, \mathrm d\mu \leq C\| R_ t\varphi -\varphi \| _{L^1(\mathrm dV)}^{\alpha /(\alpha +2n)} \leq Ct^{2\alpha /(\alpha +2n)}. \]
$\square$

1.21.3 A linear error for a smooth background

Throughout this subsection, $\theta $ is a smooth real $(1,1)$-form that is big. By theorem 1.15.3 and the remark following it, there is a strict potential $\rho \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, that is, $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \varepsilon \omega $ for some $\varepsilon >0$, which is smooth outside an analytic pole set and has a smooth remainder after resolution. Put $\Omega =X\setminus \{ \rho =-\infty \} $. For normalized potentials that are locally bounded on $\Omega $, the next lemma improves the curvature error of lemma 1.21.3 to a linear one.

Lemma 1.21.7. Let $a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $\sup _ Xa=0$, and suppose that $a$ is locally bounded on $\Omega $. There are constants $A,K,C>0$ such that the averaging operators $R_ t$ of lemma 1.21.1 satisfy

\[ R_ ta+Kt^2\downarrow a,\qquad \| R_ ta-a\| _{L^1(\mathrm{d}V)}\leq Ct^2. \]

For small $\delta >0$ and $c>0$, the transform on $\Omega $

\[ \Phi _{c,\delta } =\inf _{0<t\leq \delta } \{ R_ ta+K(t^2-\delta ^2)+K(t-\delta )-c\log (t/\delta )\} \]

extends canonically to $X$ and satisfies

1.72
\begin{equation} a-K(\delta ^2+\delta )\leq \Phi _{c,\delta }\leq R_\delta a\leq 0, \qquad \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi _{c,\delta }\geq -(Ac+C\delta )\omega . \end{equation}

At each point of $\Omega $ the infimum is attained at a positive radius. On every compact set $K_1\Subset \Omega $, for sufficiently small $t$, $\| \nabla R_ ta\| _{L^\infty (K_1)}\leq C_{K_1}/t$.

Proof. Step 1: the classical expansion. Apply the classical Chern-connection regularization expansion to the closed current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$ and the smooth lower bound $-\theta $. It gives monotonicity and logarithmic convexity: for a constant $K_0>0$, the function $\lambda (x,t)=t\partial _ t(R_ ta(x)+K_0t^2)$ is nonnegative and increasing in $t$. Instead of inferring a rate from the unspecified modulus in that theorem, we estimate the error terms of the expansion directly, using the smoothness of $\theta $.

Choose the auxiliary curvature form to be $A\omega $, with $A$ large enough that the Chern curvature plus $A\omega \otimes \operatorname {Id}$ is nonnegative on every decomposable tensor. Equivalently, after absorbing the normalization constants, the Chern curvature coefficients $R_{j\bar k\ell \bar m}$ in a unitary frame satisfy

\[ \sum R_{j\bar k\ell \bar m}\xi _ j\bar\xi _ k\zeta _\ell \bar\zeta _ m \geq -A|\xi |^2|\zeta |^2 \]

for all tangent vectors $\xi ,\zeta $. Such an $A$ exists by compactness. Pairing this inequality with the nonnegative eigenvalues of a positive complex Hessian bounds its curvature contraction from below by $-A$ times its trace, with no further error term.

Step 2: a frozen quadratic correction. Fix $x\in X$ and a complex radius variable $w\ne 0$, and put $t=|w|$. Write $\xi \in T_ x^{1,0}X$ and $\tau \in \mathbb C$ for the base and radius components of a tangent vector. In a coordinate chart of uniform size about $x$, write $\beta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}|z|^2$ and choose a quadratic function $q_{x,t}$ with

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_{x,t}=\theta (x)+Bt\beta , \]

where the constant $B$ is chosen so large that $a+q_{x,t}$ is plurisubharmonic on a ball of radius of order $t$ containing the support of the averages at $x$ and at nearby points. This is possible by the bound $\| \theta (y)-\theta (x)\| \leq C|y-x|$ on such a ball. For the Hessian calculation at $(x,w)$, the quadratic function is held fixed; its coefficients and all needed derivatives are uniformly bounded.

Step 3: the error terms. By normalized compactness, $\| a\| _{L^1}$ is bounded. On each fixed larger chart, add one fixed strictly plurisubharmonic quadratic function $Q$ to $a$. For the trace measure $\mu =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a+Q)\wedge \beta ^{n-1}$, the local Lelong mass inequality and a bound for the mass of a fixed larger ball give $\mu (B(x,t))\leq Ct^{2n-2}$. The coefficients of $\partial \bar\partial (a+q_{x,t})$ are controlled in total variation by those of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(a+Q)$ plus a uniformly bounded smooth form. Write $|\partial \bar\partial (a+q_{x,t})|$ for the sum of the total variations of its coefficient measures. The rescaled averaging kernels are supported in $B(x,Ct)$ and bounded by a constant times $t^{-2n}$, so their integrals against this measure are bounded by

\[ C t^{-2n}|\partial \bar\partial (a+q_{x,t})|(B(x,Ct)) \leq C't^{-2}. \]

Thus the terms of the expansion with a coefficient $O(t^3)$ contribute $O(t)$, and the remainder of the expansion of order two is $O(t)$ as well. The mixed and vertical contributions are bounded by $C(|\xi ||\tau |+|\tau |^2)$. By the choice of $A$, the curvature term is bounded below by $-A\lambda (x,t)|\xi |^2$; here the radial term of the same expansion identifies the relevant mass with $\lambda $ up to $O(t^2)$. Subtracting the regularization of $q_{x,t}$ restores $-\theta (x)$: its base Hessian differs from $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_{x,t}$ by $O(t)$, its mixed and vertical Hessians are bounded, and its radial logarithmic derivative is $O(t^2)$. The extra term $Bt\beta $ also costs only $O(t)$. For a real $(1,1)$-form $\alpha $, write $\alpha [v]^2$ for its associated Hermitian quadratic form evaluated on $v$. Consequently, for a fixed constant $C_1>0$, the complex Hessian of $(x,w)\mapsto R_{|w|}a(x)$ satisfies

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_{x,w}R_{|w|}a[\xi ,\tau ]^2 \geq -\theta _ x[\xi ]^2-(A\lambda (x,t)+Ct)|\xi |^2 -C_1(|\xi ||\tau |+|\tau |^2). \]

All constants are independent of $x,t,c,\delta $ and uniform for normalized $a$. In particular, setting $\xi =0$ in the same expansion gives a uniform lower bound for the vertical Hessian. Adding a fixed multiple of $|w|^2$ makes the function subharmonic in $w$, which gives the logarithmic convexity and monotonicity above with a uniform constant $K_0$.

Step 4: the minimum. Take $K\geq K_0$ and add $K(t^2+t)$ to the regularization. The positive vertical Hessian of $K|w|$ absorbs the mixed term by the inequality

\[ C_1|\xi ||\tau |\leq C't|\xi |^2+\frac{K}{8t}|\tau |^2, \]

and it absorbs the remaining bounded vertical error for small $t$. The logarithmic derivative of the corrected function is at least $\lambda $. The Kiselman minimum argument with an upper endpoint, as in the proof of lemma 1.21.3, therefore gives the last inequality in (1.72), now with error $C\delta $. On compact subsets of $\Omega $, local boundedness of $a$ makes the term $-c\log (t/\delta )$ dominate uniformly as $t\downarrow 0$, so that the minimum is attained away from zero. As before, minimizers at the upper endpoint are handled without differentiating their radius.

Step 5: the remaining bounds. The other bounds in (1.72) follow from the monotonicity of $R_ ta+Kt^2$ and from evaluation at $t=\delta $. In particular, the transform is bounded above near the analytic set $X\setminus \Omega $. The removable singularity theorem therefore extends it across this set, and local freezing and passage to the limit preserve the exact smooth lower bound for its Hessian. Its lower bound $a-K(\delta ^2+\delta )$ also extends, since quasi-plurisubharmonic functions are determined by their values off an analytic set.

The adjoint computation in the proof of lemma 1.21.2 gives $R_ t^*1=1+O(t^2)$. It only needs $a\in L^1$, since

\[ \left|\int _ X(R_ ta-a)\, \mathrm{d}V\right| \leq Ct^2\| a\| _1. \]

Together with $R_ ta-a\geq -Kt^2$, this proves the stated $L^1$ estimate. Finally, near $K_1$ the smoothing kernel only samples a fixed larger compact subset of $\Omega $, on which $a$ is bounded. The kernel derivative estimate in the proof of lemma 1.21.2 then gives the bound $C_{K_1}/t$. $\square$

1.22 Pullback and descent

Pullback by a holomorphic map preserves the inequality $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$. Conversely, on a submersion with compact connected fibers, a potential that is positive with respect to a pulled-back background, which has no vertical component, must be constant on the fibers and therefore descends. For maps to a curve, descent also holds across critical fibers, and pullback preserves and reflects all-weight ideal types. If the fibers are disconnected, potentials descend to the curve of fiber components rather than the original base. Potentials also descend under modifications, and there a Jacobian factor accounts for the change in multiplier-ideal integrability.

1.22.1 Holomorphic disks

A smooth background form need not have local $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$-potentials, but its pullback to a disk does, since a real $(1,1)$-form on a disk is closed. Testing on holomorphic disks therefore characterizes the exact lower curvature bound in a way that is preserved by holomorphic maps.

Lemma 1.22.1. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on a complex manifold $M$, and let $u$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on $M$. Then $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ if and only if the following condition holds: for every holomorphic map $f:\Delta \to M$ from the unit disk such that $u\circ f$ is not identically $-\infty $, and for every smooth function $a$ on an open subset of $\Delta $ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a=f^*\theta $, the function $u\circ f+a$ is subharmonic there.

Proof. Suppose first that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$. On a coordinate ball, convolve $u$ and $\theta $ with a smooth nonnegative radial kernel. The resulting smooth functions $u_\epsilon $ satisfy

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_\epsilon \geq -\theta _\epsilon , \qquad \theta _\epsilon \longrightarrow \theta \quad \text{smoothly}. \]

Write $u=p-r$ locally, with $p$ plurisubharmonic and $r$ smooth. The convolutions $p_\epsilon $ decrease to $p$. On a smaller disk on which $u\circ f$ is not identically $-\infty $, $p_\epsilon \circ f$ therefore converges in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ to $p\circ f$, while $r_\epsilon \circ f$ converges smoothly. Pulling back the smooth inequality and passing to the limit in the sense of distributions gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u\circ f)+f^*\theta \geq 0$. Since $u\circ f=p\circ f-r\circ f$ is the sum of a subharmonic and a smooth function, this distributional inequality makes $u\circ f+a$ subharmonic.

Conversely, test on affine complex lines in a coordinate ball. For a fixed nonzero constant direction $\xi $, local integrability of $u$ implies that the restriction of $u$ to almost every line parallel to $\xi $ is not identically $-\infty $. Fubini's theorem, applied to nonnegative smooth test functions, then gives positivity of the scalar distribution obtained by evaluating the Hermitian coefficient matrix of $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ on $\xi $. Positivity for a countable dense set of constant directions proves positivity of the Hermitian matrix of coefficient measures, which is the current inequality. $\square$

Consequently, holomorphic pullback preserves the inequality $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ wherever the pulled-back function is not identically $-\infty $: apply the disk criterion to the compositions with the given map. We also use descent of subharmonicity under the finite map $\rho (t)=t^ k$, $k\geq 1$, of the disk. If $h\circ \rho $ is subharmonic, local inverse branches of $\rho $ show that $h$ is subharmonic off zero. Moreover, $h$ is bounded above near zero, and its value at zero is its upper limit there, since

\[ h(0)=(h\circ \rho )(0) =\limsup _{t\to 0,\ t\ne 0}h(t^ k) =\limsup _{z\to 0,\ z\ne 0}h(z). \]

By the removable-singularity theorem, $h$ is subharmonic at zero as well.

1.22.2 Compact fibers

The connected components of the fibers of a submersion determine a finite covering of the base, and potentials first descend to this covering.

Lemma 1.22.2. Let $p:M\to Y$ be a surjective holomorphic submersion between connected compact complex manifolds. There is a factorization

\[ M\xrightarrow {\pi }Y'\xrightarrow {r}Y,\qquad p=r\circ \pi , \]

where $Y'$ is a connected compact complex manifold, $r$ is a finite unramified holomorphic covering and $\pi $ is a surjective holomorphic submersion with connected fibers. The points of $r^{-1}(y)$ correspond to the connected components of $p^{-1}(y)$, which are the fibers of $\pi $ over those points.

Proof. Fix a Hermitian metric on $M$. The orthogonal complement of $\ker \mathrm dp$ gives a smooth horizontal lift of tangent vectors on $Y$. Around $y_0\in Y$ choose a coordinate ball $B$ centered at $y_0$, with compact closure inside a larger coordinate ball. For $y\in B$, lift the straight segment from $y_0$ to $y$. The ordinary differential equation for horizontal transport has a solution along the whole segment, since its projection remains in the compact closure of $B$, whose inverse image is compact. Smooth dependence on the starting point and on $y$, together with transport along the reversed segment, gives a smooth trivialization

\[ p^{-1}(B)\simeq B\times p^{-1}(y_0) \]

over $B$.

The compact manifold $p^{-1}(y_0)$ has finitely many connected components. The trivialization partitions $p^{-1}(B)$ into finitely many open and closed pieces, each of which meets every fiber in one connected component. On overlaps, two such local partitions identify the same fiber components, and their labels differ by locally constant permutations. Glue one copy of each base ball for every component, using these identifications. Equivalently, take the quotient of $M$ that identifies points in the same connected component of a fiber. The local partitions make this quotient a Hausdorff covering space: distinct points over one base point lie on different sheets, and points over different base points are separated by their images in $Y$.

Call the quotient $Y'$ and its projection $r$. Coordinates of $Y$ on each sheet give holomorphic charts with holomorphic transition maps, so $r$ is a local biholomorphism. It is a finite covering, and $Y'$ is compact because a finite covering of a compact space is compact. Locally, the quotient map $\pi $ is $p$ restricted to one of the open pieces, so it is a holomorphic submersion with the asserted connected fibers. Surjectivity of $\pi $ and connectedness of $M$ make $Y'$ connected. $\square$

Proposition 1.22.3. Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be a surjective holomorphic submersion between connected compact complex manifolds, with connected fibers. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$. Pullback gives a bijection

\[ \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\longrightarrow \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta ). \]

Every member on the right is constant on each fiber, with the value $-\infty $ allowed on an entire fiber.

Proof. On a coordinate ball $V\subset X$, choose a smooth function $\rho $ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \theta $. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$, then $u+\rho \circ \pi $ is plurisubharmonic on $\pi ^{-1}(V)$. Its restriction to a fiber is plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $, and since the fiber is compact and connected, the maximum principle shows that it is constant in either case. As $\rho \circ \pi $ is constant on fibers, $u=v\circ \pi $ for a uniquely defined function $v$ on $X$.

A holomorphic submersion admits local holomorphic sections. Composing $u+\rho \circ \pi $ with such a section shows that $v+\rho $ is plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $ on a smaller ball. The latter alternative would make $u=-\infty $ on a nonempty open set, which is impossible for a quasi-plurisubharmonic function. Hence $v$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic. Distributional differentiation in local product coordinates gives

\[ \pi ^*(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v)=\pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0. \]

Positivity of a pulled-back current of this form implies positivity on the base: test in product coordinates against products with a nonnegative compactly supported volume form of integral one in the fiber variables. It follows that $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Conversely, by lemma 1.22.1, the pullback of a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function by $\pi $ is $\pi ^*\theta $-plurisubharmonic; in local product coordinates it is clearly not identically $-\infty $. The pullback map is injective because $\pi $ is surjective. $\square$

1.22.3 Maps to a curve

Let $p:X\to C$ be a surjective holomorphic map with connected fibers, where $X$ is a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$ and $C$ is a connected compact Riemann surface. Critical fibers are allowed. Fix any smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $ on $C$.

Proposition 1.22.4. Pullback gives a bijection

\[ \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )\longrightarrow \mathrm{PSH}(X,p^*\beta ). \]

It preserves the supremum and the ordinary singularity order.

Proof. The critical set is a proper compact analytic subset of $X$. It has finitely many irreducible components, and $p$ is constant on each: its differential vanishes on the smooth locus of that component, and continuity extends the constant to the component. Thus the set $D\subset C$ of critical values is finite.

Let $U\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,p^*\beta )$. On a base coordinate disk choose a smooth $a$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a=\beta $. Over $C\setminus D$, the function $U+a\circ p$ restricts to a plurisubharmonic function, or the constant $-\infty $, on each compact connected fiber. The maximum principle makes it constant there. Local holomorphic sections then give a function $v$ with $U=v\circ p$ and $a+v$ subharmonic. This is the local argument of proposition 1.22.3; it uses compactness of the fibers, not compactness of the punctured total space.

The function $v$ is bounded above by $\sup _ XU$. The removable singularity theorem extends $a+v$ subharmonically across the finitely many punctures. The extensions glue, giving $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$. Its pullback is $p^*\beta $-plurisubharmonic by lemma 1.22.1. It agrees with $U$ off the proper analytic set $p^{-1}(D)$, hence almost everywhere and everywhere by lemma 1.3.3.

Conversely pullback preserves the curvature inequality by the disk criterion. It is not identically $-\infty $, because $p$ is surjective. Surjectivity also proves uniqueness and preserves the supremum and every inequality $v\leq w+c$. $\square$

Lemma 1.22.5. Let $f:(\mathbb C^ m,0)\to (\mathbb C,0)$ be a nonconstant holomorphic germ, with $m\geq 1$, and let $e=\operatorname {ord}_0f$. For every quasi-subharmonic germ $v$ at zero,

\[ \nu (v\circ f,0)=e\nu (v,0). \]

Proof. A smooth correction of $v$ pulls back to a smooth function, so we may assume that $v$ is subharmonic. The Taylor expansion of $f$ gives $|f(z)|\leq M|z|^ e$ on a small ball. Choose a unit vector $\xi $ on which the leading homogeneous term is nonzero. Then

\[ f(\zeta \xi )=a\zeta ^ e+O(\zeta ^{e+1}),\qquad a\ne 0. \]

For all small $r$, the remainder has modulus at most $|a|r^ e/4$ on $|\zeta |=r$. If $|t|<|a|r^ e/4$, Rouché's theorem shows that $f(\zeta \xi )-t$ has $e$ zeros in $|\zeta |<r$. Consequently, with $c=|a|/4$,

\[ \{ |t|<cr^ e\} \subset f(B(0,r)) \subset \{ |t|<Mr^ e\} . \]
