The suprema of $v$ on these three sets are ordered. Dividing by $\log r^2<0$, the two disk terms both tend to $e\nu (v,0)$ by the definition in section 1.13. The middle term therefore has the same limit, proving the formula. $\square$

Corollary 1.22.6. For $v,w\in \mathrm{QPSH}(C)$, the inclusions $\mathcal I(tv)\subseteq \mathcal I(tw)$ for every $t>0$ hold if and only if

\[ \mathcal I(t(v\circ p))\subseteq \mathcal I(t(w\circ p)) \quad \text{for every }t>0. \]

In particular pullback preserves and reflects all-weight multiplier-ideal equivalence.

Proof. On a smooth curve, local modifications are isomorphisms. By theorem 1.16.3, the base inclusions are therefore equivalent to $\nu (v,s)\geq \nu (w,s)$ for every $s\in C$. Let $\pi $ be any local modification of a neighborhood in $X$ with smooth source, and let $y$ be a point of its source. In a coordinate at $s=p(\pi (y))$, the germ $p\circ \pi -s$ is nonconstant. Indeed, a modification is an isomorphism on a dense open set, and a nonconstant holomorphic map cannot be constant on an open set. Its order $e_ y$ is positive and finite. The preceding lemma gives

\[ \nu (v\circ p\circ \pi ,y)=e_ y\nu (v,s),\qquad \nu (w\circ p\circ \pi ,y)=e_ y\nu (w,s). \]

The base inequalities thus imply all inequalities upstairs, and the same valuative theorem gives the ideal inclusions. Conversely the upstairs inclusions give the inequalities for the identity modification at any $x\in p^{-1}(s)$. Dividing by $\operatorname {ord}_ x(p-s)>0$ recovers the base inequalities. Apply this argument in both directions for equality. $\square$

Lemma 1.22.7. If $v_ j,v\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$ and $v_ j\to v$ in $L^1(C)$, then $v_ j\circ p\to v\circ p$ in $L^1(X)$ for every smooth positive volume form on $X$.

Proof. Put $M_ j=\sup _ Cv_ j$ and $M=\sup _ Cv$. Hartogs' lemma applied at moving maximum points gives $\limsup M_ j\leq M$. A subsequence with limiting suprema below $M$ would give $v$ below that limit almost everywhere, hence everywhere, a contradiction. Thus $M_ j\to M$.

By surjectivity, $(v_ j-M_ j)\circ p$ has supremum zero. Normalized compactness, theorem 1.4.2, makes these pullbacks precompact in $L^1(X)$. On every relatively compact product chart over $C\setminus D$, where $D$ is the finite set of critical values, Fubini's theorem and bounded smooth volume densities give local $L^1$ convergence to $(v-M)\circ p$. Every subsequential limit therefore equals this pullback off $p^{-1}(D)$, and hence almost everywhere, because that analytic set has smooth volume zero. All subsequential limits agree, so the entire normalized sequence converges. Adding $M_ j\to M$ proves the assertion. $\square$

Lemma 1.22.8. A function $v\in \mathrm{QPSH}(C)$ has analytic singularities if and only if $v\circ p$ has analytic singularities.

Proof. Composing a local analytic presentation with $p$ proves the forward implication. The pulled-back generators are not all identically zero, since a nonconstant map to a curve is open.

Conversely fix $s\in C$ and $x\in p^{-1}(s)$. In coordinates, choose a complex line through $x$ on which $p-s$ has its finite order $e$ and is nonconstant, as in the Lelong lemma. On that line write

\[ (p-s)(\zeta )=h(\zeta )^ e, \]

where $h$ is a local biholomorphism fixing zero; take an analytic $e$th root of the nonvanishing factor of the germ. The restriction of $v\circ p$ to the line is not identically $-\infty $, since $v$ is finite almost everywhere and this map is open. By proposition 1.13.1, it has analytic singularities. In one variable its presentation is $r\log |\zeta |^2+O(1)$ with $r\in \mathbb Q_{\geq 0}$, obtained by taking the least order of its finitely many generators. Changing to $w=h(\zeta )$ gives

\[ v(s+w^ e)=r\log |w|^2+O(1). \]

Every sufficiently small $t$ has an $e$th root $w$, so $v(s+t)=(r/e)\log |t|^2+O(1)$. This is an analytic presentation on the base, including a bounded one when $r=0$. $\square$

1.22.4 The curve of fiber components

Dropping the connected-fiber hypothesis requires replacing the base by the curve of fiber components. The following construction is the curve case of Stein factorization. It also identifies the multiplicities needed when pulling back curvature measures.

Proposition 1.22.9. Let $p:X\to C$ be a surjective holomorphic map, where $X$ is a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$ and $C$ is a connected compact Riemann surface. There is a factorization

\[ X\xrightarrow {q}C'\xrightarrow {r}C,\qquad p=r\circ q, \]

where $C'$ is a connected compact Riemann surface, $q$ is surjective and holomorphic with connected fibers, and $r$ is a finite holomorphic map. The points of $r^{-1}(s)$ correspond to the connected components of $p^{-1}(s)$, which are the fibers of $q$ over those points. The factorization is unique up to a unique isomorphism commuting with $q$ and $r$.

If $t\in r^{-1}(s)$ has ramification index $e_ t$, then, as effective divisors with multiplicities,

\[ [p^{-1}(s)]=\sum _{t\in r^{-1}(s)}e_ t[q^{-1}(t)]. \]

The degree of $r$ is the number of components of a regular fiber of $p$.

Proof. We first construct the covering over the regular values, complete it across the critical values, and then extend its component quotient to $X$.

The critical values form a finite set $D\subset C$, by the critical-set argument in the proof of 1.22.4; that argument does not use connectedness of the fibers. Over $C^\circ =C\setminus D$, the map $p$ is a proper submersion. The local construction in lemma 1.22.2 applies there: horizontal transport over a disk has compact inverse image over its closure. Compact fibers have finitely many components, and the resulting local labels are glued by locally constant permutations. We obtain

\[ X^\circ \xrightarrow {q^\circ }C'^\circ \xrightarrow {r^\circ }C^\circ , \qquad X^\circ =p^{-1}(C^\circ ), \]

with $q^\circ $ a holomorphic submersion with connected fibers and $r^\circ $ a finite unramified covering of degree $d$. The complement of a proper analytic subset in a connected complex manifold is connected, so $X^\circ $ and its image $C'^\circ $ are connected. Here only the fiber divisor is removed. Locally, the complement of the zero set of a nonzero holomorphic function in a ball is path connected: join two outside points through an outside intermediate point by two complex-line disks. Each restriction is nonzero because it contains an outside endpoint, and its isolated zeros can be avoided. Applying this local argument along a chain of balls proves the required global connectedness.

For each $s\in D$, take a small coordinate disk centered at $s$ and disjoint from the other critical values. A connected component of the covering over its puncture is a finite connected covering of a punctured disk, of some degree $e$. It has a coordinate $w$ in which $r^\circ (w)=w^ e$: the universal cover of the punctured disk is a half-plane with projection $z=\exp \zeta $, and the index-$e$ subgroup of its deck group is generated by $\zeta \mapsto \zeta +2\pi \mathrm i e$. The coordinate $w=\exp (\zeta /e)$ gives the asserted form. Attach one point $w=0$ to each such component. There are finitely many, with their degrees summing to $d$. Gluing these disks to $C'^\circ $ gives a connected Riemann surface $C'$ and a finite holomorphic map $r:C'\to C$. The new points have disjoint disk neighborhoods, so this gluing is Hausdorff. The inverse image of a small closed base disk is a finite union of closed disks; over the complement of their interiors, the original finite covering is proper. Thus $C'$ is compact.

We extend $q^\circ $. Fix $x\in p^{-1}(s)$, $s\in D$. On a small coordinate ball $B$ about $x$, the image of $p$ lies in the chosen disk. The complement $B\setminus p^{-1}(s)$ is connected. Its image under $q^\circ $ therefore lies in a single punctured disk just attached to $C'$, since those disks are the connected components of $r^{-1}$ of the punctured base disk. In its coordinate $w$, the function $w\circ q^\circ $ is holomorphic and bounded, and

\[ (w\circ q^\circ )^ e=p-s. \]

The bounded holomorphic removable-singularity theorem extends it across $p^{-1}(s)\cap B$. To see this without assuming that the divisor is smooth, first extend at its smooth points by the one-variable theorem in transverse coordinates. The singular locus of the reduced divisor has complex codimension at least two in the ambient manifold, so Hartogs extension across that locus completes the extension. In dimension one there are only isolated points. Its $e$th power remains $p-s$ by the identity theorem, and hence its value is zero on that fiber. This defines a holomorphic map from $B$ to the attached disk. The local extensions agree on their dense common regular locus and therefore glue to $q:X\to C'$ with $p=r\circ q$. Its image is closed, by compactness, and contains the dense set $C'^\circ $, so $q$ is surjective.

Every fiber of $q$ is connected. Otherwise write one fiber as a union of two disjoint nonempty compact subsets and put them in disjoint open neighborhoods $U_1,U_2$. Properness gives a disk $V$ about their base point $t$ such that

\[ q^{-1}(V)\subset U_1\cup U_2. \]

The map $q$ is open: at any point, a complex line on which the nonconstant germ $q-t$ has its first nonzero term gives an open one-variable restriction. Consequently both $q(U_ i)$ contain a neighborhood of $t$. A nearby point of $C'^\circ $ then has a fiber meeting both neighborhoods, contradicting connectedness of the regular fibers.

The fibers of $p$ are the disjoint union of these connected $q$-fibers over the finitely many points of $r^{-1}(s)$. They are its components. Locally at $t$, the formula for a finite map is $r(w)-s=w^{e_ t}$ after changing the coordinate by a holomorphic root of a nonvanishing factor. Pulling back this equality by $q$ gives the divisor multiplicities stated above. Over $C^\circ $, $r$ is unramified and each sheet is one component, proving the degree assertion.

Finally, any factorization with the stated properties has over $C^\circ $ precisely this covering of fiber components. It is unramified there: a critical point of its finite map, together with surjectivity of its first factor, would make $p$ critical over that base point. Thus the component identification is holomorphic on $C'^\circ $. On every punctured disk it identifies two completions of the same finite covering, and the coordinates $w^ e$ above extend it biholomorphically across the added point. It gives a global isomorphism; density and surjectivity make it unique and give commutation with both maps. $\square$

1.22.5 Finite maps between curves

Let $r:C'\to C$ be a finite surjective holomorphic map of degree $d$ between connected compact Riemann surfaces. Write $e_ x$ for its local degree at $x\in C'$, so that $\sum _{r(x)=s}e_ x=d$ for every $s\in C$. Fix a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\beta $ on $C$ and put $\sigma =r^*\beta $. Potentials in background $\sigma $ need not descend to $C$. Taking the maximum on each fiber nevertheless gives a base potential that dominates the original potential after pullback. Its Lelong numbers are the smallest ramification-adjusted Lelong numbers on the fiber.

Lemma 1.22.10. For $h\in \mathrm{PSH}(C',\sigma )$, the function

\[ m_ h(s)=\max _{r(x)=s}h(x) \]

belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$ and satisfies $h\leq m_ h\circ r$. For every $s\in C$,

\[ \nu (m_ h,s)=\min _{r(x)=s}\frac{\nu (h,x)}{e_ x}. \]

If $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$, then $h\preceq v\circ r$ implies $m_ h\preceq v$. The analogous implication holds for the all-weight ideal order.

Proof. On a base disk choose a smooth function $a$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a=\beta $. Off the branch values, $m_ h+a$ is the maximum of finitely many subharmonic functions obtained from $h+a\circ r$ by inverse branches. It is subharmonic there and locally bounded above. The removable-singularity theorem extends it across the branch values. This extension has the stated value: upper semicontinuity and properness give the upper bound at the fiber, while the genuine upper limits of $h+a\circ r$ at its points give the reverse bound. Thus the extensions glue. The function is not identically $-\infty $, since $h$ is finite at some point. This proves the curvature inequality and $h\leq m_ h\circ r$.

Choose coordinates in the disjoint disks over $s$ so that the map on the disk centered at $x$ is $t=z^{e_ x}$. Then

\[ \sup _{|t|<\delta }m_ h(t) =\max _{r(x)=s}\sup _{|z|<\delta ^{1/e_ x}}h(z). \]

Divide by $\log \delta ^2<0$ and use the supremum definition of Lelong number. Each quotient on the right tends to $\nu (h,x)/e_ x$, and the maximum becomes the minimum after division. Smooth local corrections do not affect these limits.

An ordinary bound $h\leq v\circ r+c$ gives $m_ h\leq v+c$ on every fiber. On a curve, the all-weight ideal order is equivalent to the pointwise Lelong inequalities, by theorem 1.16.3. The pullback formula 1.22.5 makes $h\preceq _{\mathcal I}v\circ r$ equivalent to $\nu (h,x)\geq e_ x\nu (v,r(x))$ at every $x$. The minimum formula gives the required base inequalities. $\square$

Finite pullback of measures includes ramification at atoms. For a continuous real function $f$ on $C'$, put

\[ (\operatorname {Tr}_ r f)(s) =\sum _{r(x)=s}e_ xf(x). \]

This function is continuous: in the local description $t=z^ e$, the $e$ values tend to the same value at the branch point. For a finite positive measure $\mu $ on $C$, define $r^*\mu $ by

\[ \int _{C'}f\, \mathrm d(r^*\mu ) =\int _ C\operatorname {Tr}_ r f\, \mathrm d\mu , \]

and extend the definition to signed measures by linearity. Positivity and the bound $|\operatorname {Tr}_ r f|\leq d\| f\| _\infty $ give a finite measure by the Riesz representation theorem.

Lemma 1.22.11. For every finite signed measure $\mu $ and Borel set $B\subset C$,

\[ \begin{gathered} r_*r^*\mu =d\mu ,\qquad \| r^*\mu \| _{\mathrm{TV}}=d\| \mu \| _{\mathrm{TV}},\\ r^*(\mathbf1_ B\mu )=\mathbf1_{r^{-1}(B)}r^*\mu , \qquad r^*\delta _ s=\sum _{r(x)=s}e_ x\delta _ x. \end{gathered} \]

For $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(C,\beta )$, writing $T_ v=\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$ and $\mu _ v=\mathbf1_{\{ v=-\infty \} }T_ v$, one has

\[ T_{v\circ r}=r^*T_ v,\qquad \mu _{v\circ r}=r^*\mu _ v. \]

Images and inverse images of polar sets under $r$ are polar. Pullback and pushforward of positive measures commute with taking the atomic part.

Proof. The trace of $g\circ r$ is $dg$, giving the pushforward identity. The same weighted fiber formula for Borel test functions follows from the monotone-class theorem and gives the restriction and atom formulas. The positive and negative Jordan parts of a signed measure have disjoint Borel carriers; their pullbacks are positive with disjoint inverse-image carriers. Each has $d$ times its original mass, which proves the total-variation identity.

Choose a local smooth $a$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a=\beta $; then $v+a$ is a subharmonic potential of $T_ v$. Its Riesz representation is a logarithmic potential plus a harmonic function. In coordinates $t=z^ e$, the identity

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}_ z\log |z^ e-t|^2 =\sum _{z^ e=t}\operatorname {ord}_ z(z^ e-t)\delta _ z \]

shows that pulling back this potential gives the weighted measure above. Fubini's theorem applies locally: factorization of $z^ e-t$ into its $e$ linear factors gives a uniform local $L^1$ bound for these logarithmic kernels as $t$ varies on a smaller disk. The harmonic term has zero Hessian after pullback. This proves $T_{v\circ r}=r^*T_ v$. The pole set of $v\circ r$ is exactly $r^{-1}\{ v=-\infty \} $, so the restriction formula proves the polar identity.

Inverse images of polar sets are polar by composition of local subharmonic witnesses. For images, take local witnesses on the finitely many inverse-image disks of a small base disk. Off the branch values, the sum of their values over the inverse branches is subharmonic and is $-\infty $ wherever any witness has a pole. It is locally bounded above and is not identically $-\infty $. Its extension across the branch values is subharmonic. Adding the finitely many branch values to the resulting polar set still gives a polar set. This proves the image assertion locally, hence globally.

The pullback of a nonatomic measure has no atoms by the atom formula. The pushforward of a nonatomic measure has no atoms because every fiber is finite. Atomic measures pull back and push forward to atomic measures. Splitting a positive measure into these two parts proves the last assertion. $\square$

1.22.6 Modifications

Proposition 1.22.12. Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be a modification of connected compact complex manifolds, and let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$. Pullback gives a bijection

\[ \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\longrightarrow \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta ). \]

Proof. Let $S\subset X$ be a proper analytic subset over whose complement $\pi $ is an isomorphism, and put $D=\pi ^{-1}(S)$. By lemma 1.22.1, pullback preserves the curvature inequality, and the pullback of a $\theta $-plurisubharmonic function is not identically $-\infty $, since $\pi $ is an isomorphism off $D$. The pullback map is injective because $\pi $ is surjective.

To prove surjectivity, let $b\in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$, and define $u=b\circ \pi ^{-1}$ off $S$. On a coordinate neighborhood in $X$, choose a smooth function $\rho $ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho \geq \theta $. Then $b+\rho \circ \pi $ is plurisubharmonic on the inverse image of this neighborhood. By properness, its local upper bounds give a local upper bound for $u+\rho $ near $S$, so the removable-singularity theorem extends $u+\rho $ plurisubharmonically across $S$. The resulting quasi-plurisubharmonic extensions of $u$ agree almost everywhere on overlaps, hence everywhere, and glue to a function on $X$.

The current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is positive off $S$. Locally it is the sum of the positive current $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+\rho )$ and the smooth form $\theta -\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\rho $. The smooth form gives $S$ zero mass, so the restriction of $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ to $S$ is positive as well. Thus $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. The plurisubharmonic functions $b+\rho \circ \pi $ and $(u+\rho )\circ \pi $ agree off $D$, hence almost everywhere, hence everywhere. Therefore $b=u\circ \pi $. $\square$

Pullback by a modification preserves $L^1$ convergence within a family with a common lower curvature bound. Compactness controls the pullbacks near the exceptional locus, where a direct change of variables would involve a vanishing Jacobian.

Lemma 1.22.13. Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be a modification between connected complex manifolds, and let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$. If $v_ j\to v$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)$ and all $v_ j,v$ belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, then

\[ v_ j\circ \pi \longrightarrow v\circ \pi \quad \hbox{in }L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(Y). \]

In particular, on compact manifolds this is convergence in $L^1$.

Proof. By lemma 1.4.1, the functions $v_ j$ are uniformly bounded above on relatively compact subsets of $X$. The pullbacks are therefore locally uniformly bounded above and have the common smooth lower curvature bound $-\pi ^*\theta $. After a smooth local correction, the compactness theorem for plurisubharmonic functions shows that every subsequence has a further subsequence that either converges in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ or tends locally uniformly to $-\infty $ on each connected coordinate region in $Y$.

Every such region meets the complement of the exceptional locus. There $\pi $ is a biholomorphism, and change of variables gives $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ convergence to $v\circ \pi $, which excludes convergence to $-\infty $. Each subsequential limit agrees with $v\circ \pi $ off the exceptional locus, hence almost everywhere, since this analytic set has smooth volume zero. Thus the limit is unique, and applying this argument on a countable cover by coordinate regions proves convergence of the whole sequence in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(Y)$. $\square$

Convergence in capacity is also preserved by modifications. The comparison takes place away from small neighborhoods of the exceptional sets, so no uniform comparison of capacities on the whole manifolds is needed.

Lemma 1.22.14. Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be a modification between connected compact complex manifolds, equipped with Hermitian forms $\omega _ Y,\omega _ X$. Write $\operatorname {Cap}_ Y=\operatorname {Cap}_{\omega _ Y}$ and $\operatorname {Cap}_ X=\operatorname {Cap}_{\omega _ X}$ for the Hermitian capacities of section 1.8. For quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $v_ j,v$ on $X$,

\[ v_ j\longrightarrow v\text{ in capacity on }X \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad v_ j\circ \pi \longrightarrow v\circ \pi \text{ in capacity on }Y. \]

Proof. Choose a proper analytic subset $S\subset X$ such that $\pi $ is an isomorphism outside $D=\pi ^{-1}(S)$. Both $S$ and $D$ have capacity zero. For $\epsilon >0$, choose an open neighborhood $V$ of $D$ of capacity less than $\epsilon $. By properness, there is a neighborhood $U_0$ of $S$ with $\pi ^{-1}(U_0)\subset V$; shrink it to an open set $U$ with $\operatorname {Cap}_ X(U)<\epsilon $. The inverse of $\pi $ is biholomorphic near the compact set $X\setminus U$. A finite cover by nested coordinate charts and the comparison of local and global capacities in section 1.8 give a constant $C_\epsilon $ such that, for every Borel set $E\subset X$,

\[ \begin{split} \operatorname {Cap}_ Y(\pi ^{-1}E)& \leq \epsilon +C_\epsilon \operatorname {Cap}_ X(E),\\ \operatorname {Cap}_ X(E)& \leq \epsilon +C_\epsilon \operatorname {Cap}_ Y(\pi ^{-1}E). \end{split} \]

Apply these inequalities to $E=\{ |v_ j-v|>\delta \} $ for each fixed $\delta >0$, omitting the pluripolar pole sets, and let first $j\to \infty $ and then $\epsilon \downarrow 0$. $\square$

1.22.7 Multiplier ideals under modifications

Throughout this subsection, $\pi :Y\to X$ is a modification of connected compact complex manifolds of dimension $n$. There is an analytic subset $S\subset X$ of codimension at least two such that $\pi $ is an isomorphism over $X\setminus S$. Let $K_ X$ and $K_ Y$ be the canonical line bundles. Set $D=\pi ^{-1}(S)$ and

\[ R_\pi =K_ Y\otimes \pi ^*K_ X^{-1}. \]

The determinant of the differential of $\pi $ is a canonical holomorphic section $J_\pi $ of $R_\pi $; in local coordinates its coefficient is the holomorphic Jacobian determinant $\operatorname {Jac}\pi $, and its zeros are contained in $D$. These standard facts about modifications of complex manifolds include the case $n=1$, in which every modification is an isomorphism.

Lemma 1.22.15. Let $a$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on $X$. Pullback followed by multiplication with $J_\pi $ induces an isomorphism

\[ \pi _*\bigl (R_\pi \otimes \mathcal I(a\circ \pi )\bigr ) =\mathcal I(a). \]

More precisely, for every holomorphic line bundle $M$ on $X$, the map

\[ s\longmapsto J_\pi \, \pi ^*s \]

is an isomorphism

\[ H^0\bigl (X,M\otimes \mathcal I(a)\bigr ) \simeq H^0\bigl (Y,\pi ^*M\otimes R_\pi \otimes \mathcal I(a\circ \pi )\bigr ). \]

Proof. The assertion is local on $X$. In coordinates and local frames, the map sends a holomorphic coefficient $f$ to $(f\circ \pi )\operatorname {Jac}\pi $. By the change-of-variables formula, local integrability of

\[ |f|^2\mathrm{e}^{-a} \quad \hbox{and}\quad |f\circ \pi |^2|\operatorname {Jac}\pi |^2\mathrm{e}^{-a\circ \pi } \]

are equivalent. For the implication from integrability on $Y$ to integrability near a point of $X$, take a smaller relatively compact neighborhood of the point; by properness, its inverse image has compact closure, so finitely many charts on which the integrability holds suffice. Smooth positive coordinate densities are comparable on these charts, and analytic exceptional sets have smooth volume zero, so they do not change the integrals.

It remains to show that every section on $Y$ is of the form $J_\pi \pi ^*s$. Divide it by $J_\pi $ off $D$ and use the inverse of $\pi $ there; this gives a holomorphic section of $M$ off $S$. Since $S$ has codimension at least two, Hartogs' extension theorem gives a holomorphic section $s$ on $X$. Its image $J_\pi \pi ^*s$ agrees with the given section off $D$, hence everywhere by the identity theorem. The integrability equivalence then places $s$ in the required multiplier ideal. The same argument on each open set proves the sheaf identity as well as the global identification. $\square$

Note that the lemma does not identify $\pi ^*\mathcal I(a)$ with $\mathcal I(a\circ \pi )$: the Jacobian is part of the holomorphic coefficient on $Y$ and cannot be removed from the integrability test.

We also need the invariance of the ideal order $\preceq _{\mathcal I}$ of section 1.12, which involves all positive weights. The proof is local, so that we never have to extend a modification of a small open subset of $Y$ to a global one.

Lemma 1.22.16. For quasi-plurisubharmonic $a,b$ on $X$,

\[ a\preceq _{\mathcal I}b \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad a\circ \pi \preceq _{\mathcal I}b\circ \pi . \]

The assertion is local on $X$ and also holds for a proper bimeromorphic map between noncompact smooth complex manifolds.

Proof. The reverse implication follows at every positive weight from the sheaf identity in lemma 1.22.15: tensor the inclusion of ideals on $Y$ with $R_\pi $ and apply the left-exact direct image functor. The proof of that sheaf identity is local on the target, since properness makes each fiber compact and gives the required finite cover by integrability charts; compactness of the manifolds themselves is not needed.

For the forward implication, fix a coordinate neighborhood $W\subset Y$, a local modification $\sigma :Z\to W$ with smooth source, and a prime divisor $E$ on $Z$. After shrinking $W$ around the point under consideration, its image is contained in a coordinate neighborhood $U\subset X$. Put $F=\pi \circ \sigma $ and

\[ A_ F(E)=1+\operatorname {ord}_ E\operatorname {Jac}F. \]

The map $F$ is holomorphic, and it is injective and biholomorphic off a proper analytic subset; it need not be a proper modification of $U$.

Let $m$ be a positive integer, and let $\nu _ E$ and $\operatorname {ord}_ E$ be the generic Lelong number and the generic vanishing order along $E$, as in section 1.17. The proof of lemma 1.17.8 applies to the map $F$ on relatively compact coordinate patches and gives

\[ \max \left\{ \nu _ E(a\circ F)-\frac{A_ F(E)}m,0\right\} \leq \frac1m\operatorname {ord}_ E\bigl (\mathcal I(ma)\mathcal O_ Z\bigr ) \leq \nu _ E(a\circ F). \]

In more detail, the upper inequality follows by pulling back the local Bergman lower bound on $U$. For the lower one, a germ $f\in \mathcal I(ma)$ pulls back to a coefficient $(f\circ F)\operatorname {Jac}F$ that is square integrable against $\mathrm e^{-ma\circ F}$, because on a relatively compact patch of $Z$ the change-of-variables integral is bounded by the integral over the image of the patch. Generic divisorial removal and the one-variable integral then give

\[ \operatorname {ord}_ E(f\circ F)+A_ F(E)>m\nu _ E(a\circ F). \]

This estimate only uses the injectivity of $F$ off the exceptional set and the compactness of the patch, not properness of $F$ over $U$. The same estimates hold for $b$.

The hypothesis gives $\mathcal I(ma)\subseteq \mathcal I(mb)$ for every $m>0$, so the orders of the pulled-back ideals satisfy the reverse inequality. Letting $m\to \infty $ in the two estimates proves

\[ \nu _ E(a\circ F)\geq \nu _ E(b\circ F). \]

This holds for every local divisor over $Y$. Blowing up a point on the source of any local modification recovers the Lelong number at that point as a generic Lelong number along the exceptional divisor; in dimension one the point already is a divisor. The local criterion in theorem 1.16.3 now gives $a\circ \pi \preceq _{\mathcal I}b\circ \pi $. No extension of $\sigma $ beyond its local domain was used. $\square$

Lemma 1.22.17. For a proper bimeromorphic map $\rho :Z\to Y$ between complex manifolds and quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $a,b$ on $Y$,

\[ a\sim _{\mathcal I}b \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad a\circ \rho \sim _{\mathcal I}b\circ \rho . \]

The statement is local on $Y$.

Proof. Apply the local order equivalence of lemma 1.22.16 to both $(a,b)$ and $(b,a)$. Together, the two order relations are equivalent to equality of the multiplier ideals at every positive weight. $\square$

1.22.8 Restriction of analytic approximants

A quasi-plurisubharmonic function with zero generic Lelong number along a submanifold may still restrict to the function identically equal to $-\infty $ (section 1.13). Analytic regularization removes this obstruction: the restrictions of the analytic approximants are quasi-plurisubharmonic, and a fixed lower curvature bound controls their generic Lelong numbers along divisors on every fixed modification.

Corollary 1.22.18. Let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form and $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and let $u_ m$, with curvature errors $\epsilon _ m$, be the approximants of corollary 1.15.2. Let $Y\subset X$ be a connected compact complex submanifold along which the generic Lelong number of $u$ vanishes:

\[ \nu (u,Y)=\inf _{y\in Y}\nu (u,y)=0. \]

Then each restriction $u_ m|_ Y$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic on $Y$; in particular, it is not identically $-\infty $. If $\pi :Z\to Y$ is a modification with smooth compact source and $E\subset Z$ is a prime divisor, then

\[ \sup _ m\nu \bigl ((u_ m|_ Y)\circ \pi ,E\bigr )<\infty . \]

Proof. For each $m$, choose $y\in Y$ with $\nu (u,y)<1/m$. Skoda's local integrability criterion gives $\mathcal I(mu)_ y=\mathcal O_{X,y}$, so the image ideal $\mathcal I(mu)\mathcal O_ Y$ does not vanish identically. Since $u_ m$ has analytic type $(1/m,\mathcal I(mu))$, proposition 1.13.1 shows that $u_ m|_ Y$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic on $Y$. Its pullback to $Z$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic as well, since $\pi $ is biholomorphic off a proper analytic subset.

Restricting and pulling back the curvature inequality of corollary 1.15.2, which is allowed by lemma 1.22.1, gives

\[ \pi ^*(\theta |_ Y)+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl ((u_ m|_ Y)\circ \pi \bigr ) \geq -\epsilon _ m\pi ^*(\omega |_ Y). \]

Since the errors $\epsilon _ m$ are bounded, a sufficiently large multiple of a single Hermitian form on $Z$ dominates $\pi ^*(\theta |_ Y)+\epsilon _ m\pi ^*(\omega |_ Y)$ for all $m$. Lemma 1.17.7, applied on $Z$ in this fixed background, therefore bounds the generic Lelong numbers along $E$ uniformly in $m$. $\square$

1.23 Integration against non-pluripolar measures

For uniformly bounded potentials, $L^1$ convergence with respect to a smooth volume form can be transferred to any fixed finite measure that does not charge pluripolar sets. Passing to bounded exponentials extends this to families that are only bounded above. The same mechanism applies to moving mixed Monge–Ampère measures of bounded potentials and to smoothings of a fixed measure.

1.23.1 A fixed measure

Cegrell's fixed-measure convergence lemma states that a uniformly bounded sequence of plurisubharmonic functions converging in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ converges in $L^1(\nu )$ on compact subsets, for every finite non-pluripolar measure $\nu $. The usual formulation, which allows passing to a subsequence, gives the same conclusion: apply it to every subsequence after extracting a further subsequence converging almost everywhere for the smooth volume, and use the subsequence criterion for convergence. This is the classical fixed-measure argument used in [Section 11.1.2, GZ17], and we use this local lemma as a standard input.

Lemma 1.23.1. Let $\nu $ be a finite non-pluripolar measure on $X$. If uniformly bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $a_ j,a$ have a common smooth lower curvature bound and $a_ j\to a$ in $L^1(X)$, then

\[ \int _ X|a_ j-a|\, \mathrm{d}\nu \longrightarrow 0. \]

If instead $u_ j,u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ have a common upper bound and $u_ j\to u$ in $L^1(X)$, then, for every $b>0$,

\[ \int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{bu_ j}-\mathrm{e}^{bu}|\, \mathrm{d}\nu \longrightarrow 0. \]

Proof. For the first statement, add a fixed smooth strictly plurisubharmonic function on each of finitely many coordinate balls, so that the resulting functions are uniformly bounded and plurisubharmonic there. Apply the local lemma on smaller compact balls covering $X$ and sum the integrals; the added functions cancel in the differences.

For the second statement, choose $A>0$ with $A\omega \geq \theta $. If $u_ j\leq C$, the convex chain rule gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\mathrm{e}^{bu_ j}\geq -bA\mathrm{e}^{bC}\omega $, also for unbounded $u_ j$ by bounded truncation; here the exponential is given the value zero at a pole. Thus the functions $\mathrm{e}^{bu_ j}$ are uniformly bounded and quasi-plurisubharmonic, with a common lower curvature bound. They converge in $L^1(\mathrm{d}V)$, because the exponential is Lipschitz with constant $b\mathrm{e}^{bC}$ on $(-\infty ,C]$. The first statement now applies. $\square$

1.23.2 Vanishing exponents

Proposition 1.23.2. Let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ be unbounded below, and for each $\lambda >0$ let $u_\lambda $ be a quasi-plurisubharmonic function with $u_\lambda \sim \varphi $. Then, for each $\lambda >0$, the function $\lambda u_\lambda $ has infinite distance in $L^\infty (\mathrm{d}V)$ from every constant. In particular, $\lambda u_\lambda $ cannot converge uniformly on $X$ to a finite constant as $\lambda \downarrow 0$.

Proof. Since $u_\lambda \leq \varphi +C$ for a constant $C$, the function $u_\lambda $ is unbounded below. Every nonempty strict sublevel set $\{ u_\lambda <t\} $ of the upper semicontinuous function $u_\lambda $ is open, hence of positive smooth volume. Thus $u_\lambda $, and with it $\lambda u_\lambda $, is essentially unbounded below, and both conclusions follow. $\square$

A vanishing exponent gives convergence of the exponentials to one, even when the normalized potentials vary.

Lemma 1.23.3. Let $v_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ satisfy $\sup _ Xv_ j=0$, and let $\lambda _ j>0$ decrease to zero. For every finite non-pluripolar measure $\mu $,

\[ \int _ X\left|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ jv_ j}-1\right|\, \mathrm{d}\mu \longrightarrow 0. \]

If $f\in L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)$ for some $p>1$, then more precisely

\[ \int _ X\left|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ jv_ j}-1\right|\, |f|\, \mathrm{d}V \leq C_ p\lambda _ j\| f\| _{L^ p(\mathrm{d}V)}. \]

The constant depends on the fixed background, $p$ and $\mathrm{d}V$.

Proof. Choose a Hermitian form $\Omega \geq \theta $, so that $v_ j\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\Omega )$. By lemma 1.8.1, for every $T>1$,

\[ \operatorname {Cap}_\Omega (\{ v_ j<-T\} )\leq C/T \]

uniformly in $j$. Since $v_ j\leq 0$ and $1-\mathrm{e}^{-x}\leq x$ for $x\geq 0$, splitting the integral at this sublevel set gives

\[ \int _ X(1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ jv_ j})\, \mathrm{d}\mu \leq \lambda _ jT\mu (X)+\mu (\{ v_ j<-T\} ). \]

By lemma 1.8.11, the second term is uniformly small when $T$ is large, and for this fixed $T$ the first term tends to zero as $j\to \infty $. This proves the general assertion; here the exponential is given the value zero at a pole, and pole sets have zero $\mu $-measure.

For the quantitative assertion, lemma 1.5.1, applied in the background $\Omega $, gives a uniform $L^ q$ bound for $-v_ j$, where $q=p/(p-1)$. Hölder's inequality and the bound

\[ 0\leq 1-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda _ jv_ j}\leq \lambda _ j|v_ j| \]

give the claimed estimate for every $j$. $\square$

1.23.3 Measures with bounded potentials

The fixed measure can be replaced by moving mixed Monge–Ampère measures of uniformly bounded potentials, provided these potentials converge in capacity. Since $\omega $ need not be closed, the proof has to control the torsion terms that appear in integration by parts.

Lemma 1.23.4. Let $a_ j,a,w_ j,w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ be uniformly bounded, and write $\omega _ v=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v$. If $a_ j\to a$ in $L^1(X)$ and $w_ j\to w$ in capacity, then, for $0\leq k\leq n$,

\[ \int _ X|a_ j-a|\, \omega _{w_ j}^{k}\wedge \omega ^{n-k} \longrightarrow 0. \]

The same conclusion holds if, instead of $a_ j,a\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, the uniformly bounded functions $a_ j,a$ have any fixed smooth lower curvature bound.

Proof. By lemma 1.4.1, the tail envelopes $A_ j=(\sup _{\ell \geq j}a_\ell )^*$ decrease to $a$. Since $a\leq \max (a_ j,a)\leq A_ j$, monotone convergence in capacity of bounded potentials (lemma 1.8.12) implies

\[ \alpha _ j:=\max (a_ j,a)\longrightarrow a\quad \text{in capacity}. \]

We split $|a_ j-a|=(\alpha _ j-a)+(\alpha _ j-a_ j)$. The integral of the first part against the moving products tends to zero by lemma 1.8.9.

For the second part, put $h_ j=\alpha _ j-a_ j\geq 0$. We compare its integrals against the measures with indices $j$ and $\ell $. The case $k=0$ follows from the assumed $L^1$ convergence. For $k\geq 1$, put $z=w_ j-w_\ell $ and

\[ T=\sum _{s=0}^{k-1}\omega _{w_ j}^{s}\wedge \omega _{w_\ell }^{k-1-s}\wedge \omega ^{n-k}. \]

Factorization and Stokes' theorem on the compact manifold $X$ give

\begin{align*} & \int _ Xh_ j\bigl (\omega _{w_ j}^{k}-\omega _{w_\ell }^{k}\bigr ) \wedge \omega ^{n-k}\\ & \hspace{20mm}=\int _ Xh_ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}z\wedge T =\int _ Xz\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h_ jT). \end{align*}

Since $\omega $ is not assumed to be closed, $T$ need not be closed, and the product rule reads

1.75
\begin{equation} \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(h_ jT)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ j\wedge T+\mathrm{d}h_ j\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}T -\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}h_ j\wedge \mathrm{d}T+h_ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T. \end{equation}

None of the terms involving derivatives of $T$ need vanish.

We estimate the four terms. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h_ j=\omega _{\alpha _ j}-\omega _{a_ j}$, the absolute contribution of the first is at most

\[ \int _ X|z|(\omega _{\alpha _ j}+\omega _{a_ j})\wedge T. \]

It tends to zero as $j,\ell \to \infty $ by lemma 1.8.9, since $w_ j-w_\ell \to 0$ in capacity. Differentiating each factor shows that $\mathrm{d}T=\mathrm{d}\omega \wedge R$ and $\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}T=\mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\omega \wedge R$, where $R$ is a positive linear combination of mixed products of bidegree $(n-2,n-2)$. Moreover, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T$ is a sum of terms consisting of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega $ times mixed products of degree $n-2$, or of $\mathrm{d}\omega \wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\omega $ times mixed products of degree $n-3$; terms of negative degree are absent. These smooth torsion factors are bounded in absolute value by fixed multiples of $\omega ^2$ and $\omega ^3$, respectively. As $h_ j$ is uniformly bounded, the contribution of $h_ j\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}T$ also tends to zero, by the same capacity estimate.

For the two gradient terms, the pointwise Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for a positive mixed product gives, for example,

\[ \left|\int _ Xz\, \mathrm{d}h_ j\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\omega \wedge R\right|^2 \leq C\left(\int _ X|z|\, \mathrm{d}h_ j\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}h_ j\wedge \omega \wedge R\right) \left(\int _ X|z|\, \omega ^2\wedge R\right). \]

The second factor tends to zero, and the first is uniformly bounded. Indeed, if $p$ is any of the uniformly bounded potentials and $S$ any positive mixed product of degree $n-1$ occurring here, then

\[ \int _ X\mathrm{d}p\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}p\wedge S =\frac12\int _ Xp^2\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}S-\int _ Xp\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p\wedge S. \]

Expand $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}S$ with all its torsion terms as above, and write $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p=\omega _ p-\omega $. Boundedness of $p$ and lemma 1.8.9 bound both integrals uniformly. Finally, $\mathrm{d}h_ j\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}h_ j\leq 2\mathrm{d}\alpha _ j\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}\alpha _ j+2\mathrm{d}a_ j\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}a_ j$ as positive quadratic forms, which proves the asserted bound. The other gradient term is estimated in the same way. In dimension one, $T=1$ and all torsion terms in (1.75) vanish.

These integration-by-parts and energy identities hold for smooth potentials. They extend to bounded potentials through smooth decreasing approximations in the Hermitian background, using the local Bedford–Taylor chain rule, its gradient products and quasi-continuity; equivalently, they follow from the bounded current calculus of section 1.7. Only the common bound on the potentials enters the estimates.

We have shown that the difference between the integrals of $h_ j$ against the measures with indices $j$ and $\ell $ tends to zero as $j,\ell \to \infty $. For a fixed index $\ell $, the measure $\omega _{w_\ell }^ k\wedge \omega ^{n-k}$ is a fixed finite non-pluripolar measure, and lemma 1.23.1, applied separately to $\alpha _ j\to a$ and $a_ j\to a$ in $L^1(X)$, gives

\[ \int _ Xh_ j\, \omega _{w_\ell }^{k}\wedge \omega ^{n-k}\longrightarrow 0 \qquad \text{as }j\to \infty . \]

Choosing first $\ell $ large and then letting $j\to \infty $, we conclude that the integrals of $h_ j$ against $\omega _{w_ j}^ k\wedge \omega ^{n-k}$ tend to zero. Together with the estimate for $\alpha _ j-a$, this proves the result. If the $a_ j$ only have some other fixed lower curvature bound, choose a Hermitian form $\Omega \geq \omega $ such that they are $\Omega $-plurisubharmonic. By section 1.8, convergence in capacity is unchanged by this replacement. Apply the result just proved with $\Omega $ in place of $\omega $; by positivity, the original mixed measures are bounded by their counterparts for $\Omega $. $\square$

1.23.4 Smoothing the measure

Convolution in coordinate charts gives smooth approximations of a finite measure. For a varying quasi-plurisubharmonic integrand, the submean inequality transfers the error to the original measure; this gives the additional control needed beyond weak convergence of the measures themselves.

For a smooth real $(1,1)$-form $\theta $ with $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\ne \varnothing $ and a non-pluripolar probability measure $\nu $ on $X$, put

\[ A_\theta (\nu )=\sup \left\{ \int _ X(-w)\, \mathrm{d}\nu : w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ),\ \sup _ Xw=0\right\} . \]

This number may be infinite. It is finite for Monge–Ampère measures of bounded potentials.

Lemma 1.23.5. Let $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ be bounded, and put $\nu =(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n$. There is a finite constant $C$, depending only on $\omega $, $\theta $, $\mathrm{d}V$ and $\| \psi \| _\infty $, such that

\[ \int _ X(-w)\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq C \qquad (w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ),\ \sup _ Xw=0). \]

Proof. Choose finitely many pairs of concentric coordinate balls $B'_ a\Subset B_ a$ such that the $B'_ a$ cover $X$, and fixed smooth functions $q_ a,r_ a$ on $B_ a$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_ a\geq \omega $ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}r_ a\geq \theta $, with $r_ a\leq 0$. Then $\psi +q_ a$ is bounded and plurisubharmonic, $w+r_ a$ is negative and plurisubharmonic, and positivity of bounded products gives

\[ \int _{B'_ a}(-w)\, \mathrm{d}\nu \leq \int _{B'_ a}(-w-r_ a)\bigl (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi +q_ a)\bigr )^ n \leq C_ a\bigl (1+\| w\| _{L^1(B_ a)}\bigr ). \]

The last step is the weighted Chern–Levine–Nirenberg estimate of section 1.6. Summing over the cover and using the normalized $L^1$ bound of theorem 1.4.2 proves the lemma. $\square$

To construct the smoothings, choose finitely many coordinate balls $B_ a$ and smooth functions $\eta _ a\geq 0$ with compact support in $B_ a$ and $\sum _ a\eta _ a=1$. Let $\chi \geq 0$ be a smooth radial kernel of integral one supported in the unit ball of $\mathbb C^ n$, and fix $\epsilon _ j\downarrow 0$ so small that all convolutions below stay in the corresponding coordinate ball. For a function $F$, let $S_{a,j}F$ be its convolution, in the chart of $B_ a$, with the kernel $\epsilon _ j^{-2n}\chi (\cdot /\epsilon _ j)$. For a probability measure $\nu $ on $X$, define a smooth probability measure $\nu _ j$ by convolving each coordinate measure $\eta _ a\nu $ with the same kernel and adding the extensions by zero. It is characterized by the identity

1.76
\begin{equation} \int _ X F\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ j =\sum _ a\int _ X\eta _ a S_{a,j}F\, \mathrm{d}\nu , \end{equation}

for Borel functions $F$; since the kernel is even, the same convolution occurs on the right. This identity first holds for nonnegative Borel functions and then for integrable signed functions. In particular, $\nu _ j\rightharpoonup \nu $ weakly.

Lemma 1.23.6. Let $\nu $ be a non-pluripolar probability measure with $A_\theta (\nu )<\infty $, and let $\nu _ j$ be its smoothings defined by (1.76). Then

\[ \sup _ j A_\theta (\nu _ j)<\infty . \]

Moreover, let $z_ j,z$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions with a common upper bound and a common smooth lower curvature bound, and suppose that $z_ j\to z$ both in $L^1(X)$ and in $L^1(\nu )$. Then

1.77
\begin{equation} \int _ X|z_ j-z|\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ j\longrightarrow 0. \end{equation}

Consequently, for every $\lambda >0$,

\[ \mathrm{e}^{\lambda z_ j}\nu _ j\rightharpoonup \mathrm{e}^{\lambda z}\nu . \]

Proof. On each coordinate ball $B_ a$ choose a fixed smooth function $q_ a$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_ a\geq \theta $. If $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $\sup _ Xw=0$, then $w+q_ a$ is plurisubharmonic, and the submean inequality gives

\[ -S_{a,j}w\leq -w+S_{a,j}q_ a-q_ a\leq -w+C, \]

with a constant independent of $w$ and $j$. Integrating and using (1.76) proves $A_\theta (\nu _ j)\leq A_\theta (\nu )+C$.

For the second assertion, the tail envelopes

\[ H_ j=\left(\sup _{k\geq j}z_ k\right)^* \]

decrease to $z$ by lemma 1.4.1. Enlarge the functions $q_ a$ so that $H_ j+q_ a$, $z_ j+q_ a$ and $z+q_ a$ are all plurisubharmonic. Since $H_ j\geq z_ j$ and $H_ j\geq z$, we have $|z_ j-z|\leq 2H_ j-z_ j-z$, and the identity (1.76) and the submean inequality imply

\[ \begin{split} \int _ X|z_ j-z|\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ j & \leq \sum _ a\int _ X\eta _ a S_{a,j}(2H_ j-z_ j-z)\, \mathrm{d}\nu \\ & \leq 2\sum _ a\int _ X\eta _ a \bigl (S_{a,j}(H_ j+q_ a)-(z+q_ a)\bigr )\, \mathrm{d}\nu +\int _ X(z-z_ j)\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \end{split} \]

All these integrals are well defined: each function on the larger side of a submean inequality is bounded above, and its negative part is controlled by that of the integrable function on the smaller side plus a constant.

Radial averages of a plurisubharmonic function decrease when the radius decreases. Therefore $S_{a,j}(H_ j+q_ a)$ decreases to $z+q_ a$ on the support of $\eta _ a$: to identify the limit, fix an index $k$, use $H_ j\leq H_ k$ for $j\geq k$, let $j\to \infty $ in the radius, and then let $k\to \infty $; the reverse inequality is the submean inequality. The nonnegative integrand in the second line of the last display is dominated on this compact support by a constant minus $z$, which is $\nu $-integrable, so its integral tends to zero by dominated convergence. The final integral tends to zero by convergence in $L^1(\nu )$. This proves (1.77).

Since the exponential is Lipschitz on the common range of the functions, (1.77) also gives $\int _ X|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda z_ j}-\mathrm{e}^{\lambda z}|\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ j\to 0$. If $\zeta >0$ is smooth, then $\zeta \mathrm{e}^{\lambda z}=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda z+\log \zeta }$ is bounded and quasi-plurisubharmonic. For any fixed bounded quasi-plurisubharmonic function $F$, the same local submean argument and dominated convergence in (1.76) give

\[ \int _ XF\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ j\longrightarrow \int _ XF\, \mathrm{d}\nu . \]

Applying this to $F=\zeta \mathrm{e}^{\lambda z}$ gives $\int _ X\zeta \mathrm{e}^{\lambda z_ j}\, \mathrm{d}\nu _ j\to \int _ X\zeta \mathrm{e}^{\lambda z}\, \mathrm{d}\nu $ for every smooth $\zeta >0$. Since every smooth function is a difference of two positive smooth functions, the convergence holds for all smooth test functions, and uniformly bounded masses together with uniform approximation of continuous functions by smooth ones prove weak convergence. $\square$

1.24 Curvature of line bundles

Let $M$ be a complex manifold and let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $M$. To relate potentials to holomorphic sections, we ask when a current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$, with $u$ locally integrable, can be the curvature current of a metric on a line bundle. The transition law for the local weights of such a metric forces a closedness condition on the background.

Proposition 1.24.1. The curvature current of a singular Hermitian metric with locally integrable weights on a holomorphic line bundle is closed. In particular, a nonclosed form $\theta $ cannot itself be the curvature current of such a metric, and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ is not closed on any neighborhood of a point where $\mathrm{d}\theta \neq 0$.

Proof. Let $h$ be a singular Hermitian metric on a holomorphic line bundle $L\to M$. Choose a local holomorphic frame $e$ of $L$ and write its squared norm as $|e|_ h^2=\mathrm{e}^{-\varphi }$; the curvature current is then locally $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $. If $e'=ge$ is another frame, with $g$ holomorphic and nowhere zero, the new weight is $\varphi -\log |g|^2$. Since $\log |g|^2$ is pluriharmonic, these local currents agree on overlaps and glue to a global current, which is closed because $\mathrm{d}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}=0$. The final assertion follows from the identity

\[ \mathrm{d}(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)=\mathrm{d}\theta \]

of currents. $\square$

Accordingly, when we study sections subject to multiplier-ideal conditions, we specify both the line bundle, whose curvature is closed, and any auxiliary Hermitian form on $M$. An algebraic interpretation then requires relating the invariants of the line bundle to this analytic background.

1.25 Polar curvature

For $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, the restriction of the current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$ to the pole set $\{ u=-\infty \} $ is called the polar curvature of $u$. A bounded change of the potential preserves more than its Lelong numbers: it preserves the whole polar curvature. Indeed, a local energy estimate puts the bounded difference of two potentials in a Sobolev space, while pole sets admit smooth test functions of arbitrarily small energy, and these test functions show that no coefficient measure of the Hessian of the difference charges the pole set. Polar curvature is closed even when the pole set is not closed, so a local elliptic equation recovers it from its scalar Hermitian trace.

Lemma 1.25.1. Let $U\subset \mathbb R^ d$ be open and let $w\in L^\infty _{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$. If $\Delta w$ is a locally finite signed Radon measure, then $w\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)$.

Proof. Fix a smooth function $0\leq \psi \leq 1$ with compact support in $U$, and a relatively compact open neighborhood $V$ of its support. Mollify $w$ inside $V$ with a standard mollifier $\rho _\varepsilon $, writing $w_\varepsilon =w*\rho _\varepsilon $ and $\nu _\varepsilon =(\Delta w)*\rho _\varepsilon $. For small $\varepsilon $, integration by parts and Young's inequality give

\[ \begin{split} \int \psi ^2|\nabla w_\varepsilon |^2 & =-\int \psi ^2w_\varepsilon \nu _\varepsilon -2\int \psi w_\varepsilon \nabla \psi \cdot \nabla w_\varepsilon \\ & \leq \| w\| _{L^\infty (V)}|\Delta w|(V) +\tfrac 12\int \psi ^2|\nabla w_\varepsilon |^2 +2\int w_\varepsilon ^2|\nabla \psi |^2. \end{split} \]

Absorbing the middle term on the right, we obtain a bound for $\int \psi ^2|\nabla w_\varepsilon |^2$ that is independent of $\varepsilon $. Taking $\psi =1$ on a prescribed smaller compact set, weak compactness in $W^{1,2}$ and convergence of the mollifications in $L^2$ identify the weak limit with $w$. This proves the assertion. $\square$

Lemma 1.25.2. Let $v$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on a complex manifold, let $U$ be a coordinate ball with compact closure in a larger chart, and let $K\subset U\cap \{ v=-\infty \} $ be compact. For every open $O\subset U$ containing $K$, there are real $\varphi _ j\in C^\infty _ c(O)$ with $0\leq \varphi _ j\leq 1$, equal to one near $K$, such that $\| \varphi _ j\| _{W^{1,2}(U)}\to 0$. The norm uses the Euclidean coordinates.

Proof. Adding a smooth quadratic function and subtracting a constant, we obtain a negative plurisubharmonic function $h$ on $U$ with the same pole set as $v$. Put $h_ M=\max (h,-M)$ for $M\geq 1$, and choose $\psi \in C^\infty _ c(O)$ with $0\leq \psi \leq 1$ and $\psi =1$ near $K$. On a fixed compact neighborhood of $\operatorname {supp}\psi $, the positive measures $\Delta h_ M$ have uniformly bounded mass: choose a nonnegative smooth function $\chi $ with compact support in $U$, equal to one on this neighborhood, and use

\[ \int \chi \, \Delta h_ M=\int h_ M\Delta \chi , \qquad |h_ M|\leq |h|\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(U). \]

Apply the integration-by-parts estimate of the preceding proof to mollifications of $h_ M$. Their absolute values are at most $M$, and their squares are at most $M$ times their absolute values. The uniform bounds for the mass of the Laplacians and for the local $L^1$ norms therefore give, after passing to the weak limit in $W^{1,2}$,

\[ \int \psi ^2|\nabla h_ M|^2\leq CM, \qquad \left\| \frac{-\psi h_ M}{M}\right\| _{W^{1,2}(U)}^2\leq \frac{C}{M}. \]

Here $C$ depends on the fixed cutoffs and the local $L^1$ norm of $h$, but not on $M$. The second inequality also uses $h_ M^2\leq M|h|$ in the terms containing $\psi $ and $\nabla \psi $.

The Sobolev function $-\psi h_ M/M$ takes values between zero and one. It equals one on a neighborhood of $K$, since $h$ is upper semicontinuous and therefore $\{ h<-M\} $ is an open neighborhood of $K$. Extend it by zero and mollify with a sufficiently small radius: the support stays in $O$, the function remains equal to one on a smaller neighborhood of $K$, and its Sobolev norm does not increase. Letting $M\to \infty $ gives the required smooth functions. $\square$

For a current $T$ of order zero and a Borel set $B$, we write $\mathbf1_ BT$ for the restriction of $T$ to $B$, taken coefficientwise on the Radon coefficient measures of $T$. These restrictions commute with smooth changes of coordinates.

Proposition 1.25.3. Let $u,v$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic functions on a complex manifold such that every point has a neighborhood on which $u-C\leq v\leq u+C$ for some constant $C$. Then $u$ and $v$ have the same pole set $S=\{ u=-\infty \} =\{ v=-\infty \} $, and

\[ \mathbf1_ S\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u=\mathbf1_ S\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v. \]

Consequently, for any smooth real $(1,1)$-forms $\theta ,\beta $,

\[ \mathbf1_ S(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)=\mathbf1_ S(\beta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v). \]

Proof. The two local inequalities show that the pole sets agree. Off $S$ put $w=u-v$, and give $w$ arbitrary bounded values on $S$. Since $S$ has Lebesgue measure zero, this defines the locally bounded distribution $u-v$. The coefficient measures of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ are locally finite, since after a smooth correction the complex Hessians of $u$ and $v$ are positive currents of order zero. Taking the Euclidean trace shows that $\Delta w$ is a signed Radon measure, so lemma 1.25.1 gives $w\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

Consequently, the real and imaginary parts of the coefficient measures of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ are continuous linear functionals on $W^{1,2}_0$ of a smaller ball, since a second distributional derivative of $w$ is a first derivative of its $L^2$ gradient. Let $\nu $ be any one of these real signed measures, with Jordan decomposition $\nu =\nu ^+-\nu ^-$. Choose a compact set $K$ contained in the intersection of $S$ with a positive set of a Hahn decomposition, so that $\nu ^-(K)=0$. For $\epsilon >0$, take an open neighborhood $O$ of $K$ in the ball with $\nu ^-(O)<\epsilon $. The test functions $\varphi _ j$ of lemma 1.25.2, applied to $u$, satisfy

\[ \nu ^+(K)\leq \int \varphi _ j\, \mathrm d\nu ^+ =\langle \nu ,\varphi _ j\rangle +\int \varphi _ j\, \mathrm d\nu ^- \leq |\langle \nu ,\varphi _ j\rangle |+\epsilon . \]

By continuity on the Sobolev space, the first term tends to zero. Letting $\epsilon \downarrow 0$ and using inner regularity gives $\nu ^+(S)=0$ locally, and the same argument applied to $-\nu $ gives $\nu ^-(S)=0$. Thus all coefficient measures of $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w$ vanish on $S$, which proves the first identity. Since smooth forms also give $S$ zero mass, the second identity follows. $\square$

For closed positive currents, local potentials turn equality of the scalar traces into an elliptic equation. If both currents are carried by a Lebesgue null set, the resulting smooth difference vanishes.

Lemma 1.25.4. Let $T,S$ be closed positive $(1,1)$-currents on a complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$, both carried coefficientwise by a Borel set of Lebesgue measure zero. For any Hermitian form $\eta $,

\[ T\wedge \eta ^{n-1}=S\wedge \eta ^{n-1} \quad \Longrightarrow \quad T=S. \]

Proof. On a small coordinate ball, take plurisubharmonic local potentials $a,b$ with $T=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$ and $S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b$, by [Chapter III, Proposition 1.19, p. 135, DemCADG]. Put $h=a-b\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$. The trace identity gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h\wedge \eta ^{n-1}=0$ in the sense of distributions. Dividing by the smooth positive volume form $\eta ^ n$ gives a scalar second-order operator with smooth coefficients whose principal matrix is a positive multiple of the inverse of the Hermitian matrix of $\eta $; it is uniformly elliptic on every smaller ball. Local elliptic regularity therefore makes $h$ smooth. Thus $T-S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$ is smooth and carried by a Lebesgue null set, so its coefficient measures vanish. This proves equality locally, and hence globally. $\square$

Pushforward by a proper map commutes with restriction to the inverse image of a Borel set. Hence polar curvature is compatible with modifications, even when the pole set is not closed.

Proposition 1.25.5. Let $\pi :Y\to X$ be a modification of connected compact complex manifolds, let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form on $X$, and let $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Put $S=\{ u=-\infty \} $ and

\[ T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u,\qquad T_ Y=\pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u\circ \pi ). \]

Then

1.82
\begin{equation} \pi _*\bigl (\mathbf1_{\pi ^{-1}(S)}T_ Y\bigr )=\mathbf1_ ST. \end{equation}

Proof. By proposition 1.22.12, $u\circ \pi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$. Change of variables on the locus where $\pi $ is an isomorphism, whose complement has Lebesgue measure zero in both manifolds, gives $\pi _*(u\circ \pi )=u$ as distributions and $\pi _*\pi ^*\theta =\theta $ as currents. Since pushforward by a proper map commutes with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$, we get $\pi _*T_ Y=T$.

For any Borel set $B\subset X$, the coefficient measures of $\pi _*T_ Y$ are pushforwards of the coefficient measures of $T_ Y$, multiplied by the smooth coefficients that arise from pulling back a test form. Restriction therefore gives $\pi _*(\mathbf1_{\pi ^{-1}(B)}T_ Y)=\mathbf1_ B\pi _*T_ Y$. Applying this to the Borel set $S$ proves (1.82); closedness of the restricted currents is not used. $\square$

The restriction of a closed positive current to a closed complete pluripolar set is closed; the classical proof uses bounded cutoff functions. Since these cutoff functions converge pointwise to the indicator function of the complement of the pole set, the argument also applies to pole sets that are not closed.

Proposition 1.25.6. Let $X$ be a complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$, let $u$ be quasi-plurisubharmonic on $X$, and let $\theta $ be a smooth real $(1,1)$-form. Then

\[ \operatorname {Pol}_\theta (u) :=\mathbf1_{\{ u=-\infty \} }(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u) \]

is a closed positive $(1,1)$-current.

Proof. Locally, adding a smooth quadratic function to $u$ and subtracting a constant gives a negative plurisubharmonic function $a$ with pole set $S=\{ u=-\infty \} $. Put $\Theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}a$. Since smooth forms give $S$ zero mass, the polar current equals $\mathbf1_ S\Theta $ and is positive. In dimension one it is closed for degree reasons.

Now suppose that $n\geq 2$. Choose a smooth convex nondecreasing function $\chi $ on $\mathbb R$ with $\chi (t)=0$ for $t\leq 1/2$ and $\chi (1)=1$, and a smooth function $0\leq \vartheta \leq 1$ that vanishes near zero and equals one near one. Set

\[ w_ k=\chi (\mathrm{e}^{a/k}),\qquad k\geq 1. \]

These functions are bounded and plurisubharmonic, with $0\leq w_ k\leq 1$. They vanish on $S$ and tend to one at every point outside $S$. Hence $\vartheta (w_ k)\Theta $ converges locally, coefficientwise in total variation, to $\mathbf1_{X\setminus S}\Theta $. Moreover, $\vartheta '(w_ k)$ vanishes on $S$ and tends to zero everywhere.

Fix a constant strongly positive $(n-2,n-2)$-form $\beta $ and put $\Xi =\Theta \wedge \beta $. The bounded Bedford–Taylor chain rule gives

\[ \mathrm d\bigl (\vartheta (w_ k)\Xi \bigr ) =\vartheta '(w_ k)\, \mathrm dw_ k\wedge \Xi . \]

Indeed, approximate $w_ k$ on a smaller ball by a decreasing sequence of smooth plurisubharmonic functions. Dominated convergence gives convergence of the corresponding $\vartheta $-weighted currents, while the convergence of bounded Bedford–Taylor energies and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality give convergence of the differentiated expressions; this proves the chain rule for $w_ k$. For a smooth compactly supported test one-form $\alpha $, choose a nonnegative smooth cutoff $\tau $ equal to one near its support. Since $w_ k\geq 0$ and $\Xi $ is closed and positive, bounded integration by parts gives

\[ 2\int \tau \, \mathrm dw_ k\wedge \mathrm d^{\mathrm c}w_ k\wedge \Xi \leq \int \tau \, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(w_ k^2)\wedge \Xi =\int w_ k^2\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\tau \wedge \Xi \leq C. \]

Here $C$ depends only on the local mass of $\Xi $ and the fixed cutoff. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for a positive current now yields

\[ \bigl |\langle \vartheta '(w_ k)\, \mathrm dw_ k\wedge \Xi , \alpha \rangle \bigr |^2 \leq C_\alpha \int _{\operatorname {supp}\alpha } |\vartheta '(w_ k)|^2\, \Xi \wedge \kappa \longrightarrow 0, \]

where $\kappa $ is the Euclidean form; the limit follows by dominated convergence with respect to the fixed locally finite trace measure. Hence $\mathrm d(\vartheta (w_ k)\Xi )\to 0$, and $\mathbf1_{X\setminus S}\Theta \wedge \beta $ is closed. Since constant strongly positive forms span all constant forms of bidegree $(n-2,n-2)$, the current $\mathbf1_{X\setminus S}\Theta $ is closed. Subtracting it from $\Theta $ shows that $\mathbf1_ S\Theta $ is closed. The local currents agree on overlaps and give the asserted global current. $\square$
